☸️ Grafana Associate — Complete Learning Guide

From Zero to Certified: Visualize, Alert, and Master the LGTM Stack

A comprehensive, highly illustrated learning guide for the Certified Grafana Associate exam. Every concept demonstrated through the anihpj/jobpost Django project deployed on Docker and Kubernetes. Covers all 6 exam domains with 25 chapters, 250+ practice questions, and rich visual diagrams.

☸️ Grafana Associate 🐳 Docker ☸️ Kubernetes 🟢 All Levels 📊 25 Chapters 📎 6 Appendices

📚 Grafana Associate — Complete Learning Guide

Welcome to your Certified Grafana Associate journey! This guide uses the anihpj Django project (with jobpost app, models: Skills, User, Author, Location, JobPost) deployed on Docker & Kubernetes as a real-world demonstration. Content will be built chapter by chapter.

🛠️ SRE / Platform Engineer Ops Guide

Daily Operations with Grafana — The SRE Perspective

How Site Reliability Engineers and Platform Engineers use Grafana daily to maintain observability, respond to incidents, and optimize infrastructure for the anihpj job board platform.

🟢 Beginner⏱️ ~30 min🛠️ Operations
👁️
Daily Dashboard Review

Start every day by reviewing the anihpj Production Overview dashboard. Check: error rates, P95 latency, active users, pod health. Spot anomalies before users report them.

🔔
Alert Response

When an alert fires: 1) Open linked dashboard, 2) Check Query Inspector to verify data, 3) Cross-reference Loki logs (L→T bridge), 4) Check Tempo traces. Full LGTM workflow in action.

📊
Capacity Planning

Use Mimir metrics to track trends: CPU/memory growth over weeks. Set up recording rules for expensive queries. Configure alerts at 70% capacity — not 95%.

🏗️
IaC & Automation

Manage Grafana as code: dashboards in Git, provisioned via CI/CD, deployed with Terraform. Never click-configure in production — always version control.

🚨
Incident Response

During incidents: 1) Mute non-critical alerts (silences), 2) Create annotation marking incident start, 3) Use Explore for ad-hoc queries, 4) Snapshot dashboard for postmortem.

💰
Cost Optimization

Monitor Grafana Cloud usage: metrics series count, log ingestion rate, trace volume. Set up dashboards tracking YOUR Grafana usage. Free tier: 10K metrics, 50GB logs, 50GB traces.

📋 Daily Grafana Ops Tasks — The SRE Morning Routine

1
Check Production Overview

Start with the anihpj Overview dashboard — glance at error rates, P95 latency, pod health. Any red/green indicators? Time: 2 min.

2
Review Firing Alerts

Alerting → Alert Rules → Filter: Firing. Triage: any new alerts? Any still firing from last shift? Time: 3 min.

3
Check Error Budgets

SLO dashboard: verify error budgets are above 30%. If burning fast, escalate. Time: 2 min.

4
Scan Loki for Errors

Explore → Loki → {job="anihpj"} |= "ERROR" — any new error patterns? Time: 3 min.

5
Verify Data Sources

Connections → Data Sources — all green checkmarks? Prometheus scraping? Postgres reachable? Time: 1 min.

6
Handoff Notes

Document anomalies, open annotations for ongoing incidents. Time: 2 min.

📈 Monitoring Stack Architecture

Layer 4: VISUALIZATIONGrafana Dashboards + Explore + Alerting UI
Layer 3: STORAGEMimir (metrics) + Loki (logs) + Tempo (traces) + PostgreSQL (business)
Layer 2: COLLECTIONGrafana Agent / Prometheus scraping / Promtail / OpenTelemetry SDK
Layer 1: INSTRUMENTATIONDjango Prometheus exporter, K8s kube-state-metrics, node-exporter, structured logging

Key principle: Each layer is independent. You can swap Prometheus for Mimir without changing Grafana dashboards. You can change visualization tools without touching data collection. This layered architecture is what makes Grafana so flexible.

🏗️ IaC & Automation — Provisioning Grafana as Code

Golden rule: Never click-configure in production. Provision EVERYTHING: dashboards, data sources, alert rules, notification policies, folders. Store in Git. Deploy via CI/CD.

Provisioning Directory Structure
grafana/provisioning/
├── dashboards/
│   ├── dashboard.yml              # Provider config
│   └── anihpj/
│       ├── overview.json          # Dashboard JSON
│       └── postgres-metrics.json
├── datasources/
│   ├── prometheus.yml             # Data source YAML
│   └── postgres.yml
├── alerting/
│   └── rules.yml                  # Alert rules
└── notifiers/
    └── contact-points.yml         # Contact points

CI/CD pipeline: Git push → GitHub Actions → curl -X POST /api/admin/provisioning/dashboards/reload → Verify dashboards load. Service Account with Editor role handles the API calls. Full GitOps: ArgoCD watches the Git repo and syncs to Grafana automatically.

🚨 Incident Response with Grafana — Step-by-Step

🚨 INCIDENT RESPONSE WORKFLOW
  🔔 ALERT FIRES (PagerDuty notification received)
  │
  ├─ Step 1: ACKNOWLEDGE (30s)
  │  Acknowledge in PagerDuty. Open Incident channel in Slack.
  │
  ├─ Step 2: TRIAGE (2 min)
  │  Open linked Grafana dashboard → verify the alert is real
  │  Check: Is this a known issue? Is there a runbook?
  │
  ├─ Step 3: SILENCE NON-CRITICAL (1 min)
  │  Alerting → Silences → New: {severity=warning}, duration=2h
  │  This prevents alert storms while you work
  │
  ├─ Step 4: INVESTIGATE (5-15 min)
  │  Explore → Prometheus: check related metrics
  │  Explore → Loki: {job="anihpj"} |= "ERROR" — any new errors?
  │  Explore → Tempo: {resource.service.name="anihpj"} — slow traces?
  │  Dashboard → Annotate: mark incident start time
  │
  ├─ Step 5: MITIGATE (variable)
  │  Apply the fix. Monitor dashboards for recovery.
  │
  └─ Step 6: POSTMORTEM (after resolved)
     Dashboard → Snapshot: capture state for postmortem doc
     Export alert timeline: Alerting → Alert Rules → View history

💰 Cost Optimization — Monitoring Your Monitoring

Cost DriverWhat to WatchOptimization
Metrics SeriesActive series count (Mimir/Prometheus)Drop unused labels, reduce cardinality, use recording rules
Log VolumeGB ingested/day (Loki)Filter at source (promtail), drop debug logs, limit retention
Trace VolumeSpans/second (Tempo)Head sampling (keep 10%), tail sampling for errors only
Grafana CloudActive users, dashboards, alertsFree tier: 10K metrics, 50GB logs, 50GB traces, 3 users

Grafana Cloud Free Tier Limits: 10,000 active series (metrics), 50 GB log ingestion, 50 GB trace ingestion, 3 active users, 14-day retention. Exceed any → need paid plan. Build a dashboard tracking YOUR usage against these limits.

📊 Capacity Planning Dashboards — Plan Before You Crash

Build dashboards that project FUTURE growth, not just show current state. Key metrics to track:

📈
Growth Rate

deriv(container_memory_usage_bytes[30d]) — how fast is memory growing? Project 30/60/90 days forward.

⚠️
Saturation Points

Set alerts at 70% capacity — NOT 95%. At 70% you have time to scale. At 95% you're in emergency mode.

🔄
Recording Rules

Pre-compute job:memory:trend30d — 50 dashboards querying the same trend → 1 recording rule → massive savings.

📟 On-Call Rotation & Escalation

How Grafana Alerting integrates with on-call: Contact Points (PagerDuty, Opsgenie, VictorOps, xMatters) connect to your on-call platform. Notification Policies route severity=critical → PagerDuty (wakes someone up), severity=warning → Slack (async notification).

Escalation path: Alert fires → PagerDuty pages primary on-call → No ack in 5 min → Escalate to secondary → No ack in 10 min → Escalate to manager. Exam note: Grafana itself doesn't manage rotations — that's done in PagerDuty/Opsgenie. Grafana just sends the notification to the right service.

📝 Post-Mortem Analysis — Learn from Every Incident

After every incident, use Grafana to build a data-driven postmortem:

1. Capture the Timeline

Alerting → Alert Rules → View history. Export the exact timeline: when did each alert fire? When did the condition clear? This is your factual incident timeline.

2. Snapshot the Dashboard

Dashboard → Share → Snapshot. Capture the EXACT state during the incident. Snapshots are immutable — perfect for postmortem evidence.

3. Correlate with Deployments

Cross-reference annotations (deployment markers) with alert timeline. Did the incident start right after a deploy? That's your root cause indicator.

4. Create Action Items

Based on findings: update alert thresholds, add new panels, create new alert rules. Link to dashboards in the postmortem doc for easy reference.

☸️ Multi-Cluster Kubernetes Monitoring

☸️ MULTI-CLUSTER MONITORING ARCHITECTURE
  ┌─────────────────────┐  ┌─────────────────────┐  ┌─────────────────────┐
  │ ☸️ K8s Cluster A    │  │ ☸️ K8s Cluster B    │  │ ☸️ K8s Cluster C    │
  │ (us-east-1, prod)   │  │ (us-west-2, prod)   │  │ (eu-west-1, staging)│
  │                     │  │                     │  │                     │
  │ Grafana Agent       │  │ Grafana Agent       │  │ Grafana Agent       │
  │ ┌─────────────────┐ │  │ ┌─────────────────┐ │  │ ┌─────────────────┐ │
  │ │ Mimir (metrics) │ │  │ │ Mimir (metrics) │ │  │ │ Mimir (metrics) │ │
  │ │ Loki (logs)     │ │  │ │ Loki (logs)     │ │  │ │ Loki (logs)     │ │
  │ │ Tempo (traces)  │ │  │ │ Tempo (traces)  │ │  │ │ Tempo (traces)  │ │
  │ └────────┬────────┘ │  │ └────────┬────────┘ │  │ └────────┬────────┘ │
  └──────────┼──────────┘  └──────────┼──────────┘  └──────────┼──────────┘
             │                        │                        │
             └────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┘
                                     │
                          ┌──────────▼──────────┐
                          │   🎨 GRAFANA        │
                          │                     │
                          │ Variable: $cluster  │
                          │ (A, B, C — dropdown)│
                          │                     │
                          │ One dashboard →     │
                          │ all 3 clusters      │
                          └─────────────────────┘

Key techniques: 1) Use a $cluster variable to switch between clusters in one dashboard. 2) Federation: Mimir can query across clusters. 3) Cross-cluster alerting: sum by (cluster) (rate(errors_total[5m])) > 5 — one alert rule covers all clusters. 4) Use cluster labels to distinguish metrics: {cluster="us-east-1"}.

🎯 SLO/SLI/SLA Monitoring — The Reliability Triad

One of the most commonly tested concepts. Know the difference between SLI, SLO, and SLA — they form the foundation of modern observability and SRE.

TermDefinitionExample for anihpjWho Sets It?
SLI
Service Level Indicator		The ACTUAL measurement. A metric that quantifies a dimension of service quality.P95 latency for /api/jobs/ = 320ms (measured)Engineering (measured)
SLO
Service Level Objective		The TARGET value. The goal the service aims to meet over a time window.P95 latency must be ≤ 500ms over 30 daysEngineering + Product
SLA
Service Level Agreement		The CONTRACT. What happens if you miss the SLO — refunds, credits, penalties."If P95 > 500ms for >1hr/month, 10% refund"Legal + Business
🎯 ERROR BUDGET — The Key SRE Concept
  SLO: 99.9% uptime over 30 days = 43,200 minutes
  Allowed downtime: 0.1% × 43,200 = 43.2 minutes/month
  ERROR BUDGET = 43.2 minutes of "acceptable failure"

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │████████████████████████████████████████████████████░░░░░░░░░░│
  │◀─────────── 99.9% (43,157 min uptime) ──────────────────▶│
  │                                        ◀─ 0.1% budget ──▶│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  🔑 ERROR BUDGET POLICY:
  • Budget > 50% remaining → Safe to deploy, experiment, take risks
  • Budget < 20% remaining → FREEZE all changes, focus on reliability
  • Budget EXHAUSTED → STOP everything, fix reliability FIRST

  ⚠️ Burn rate matters:
  • Slow burn (0.5%/day) → Normal, plan fixes for next sprint
  • Fast burn (5%/day) → EMERGENCY, page on-call, rollback immediately
📊
SLO Dashboard Panel

Use a Gauge or Stat showing current SLI vs SLO target. PromQL: histogram_quantile(0.95, rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket{job="anihpj"}[30d]))

🔥
Error Budget Burn

Use a Time Series panel tracking budget remaining. Green = safe, Yellow = warning (<20%), Red = exhausted. Alert at 50% and 80% burn.

⚠️ Exam Trap: SLI vs SLO vs SLA

SLI = what you MEASURE. "P95 latency is 320ms." SLO = what you PROMISE internally. "We'll keep P95 under 500ms." SLA = what you CONTRACT. "If we break our promise, customer gets money back." Exam question pattern: "You need to define a target for 99.9% availability — SLI, SLO, or SLA?" → SLO. "A contract guarantees uptime with penalties — SLI, SLO, or SLA?" → SLA.

🔌 Grafana Agent / Alloy — How Data Gets INTO Grafana

The Universal Data Collector for Metrics, Logs & Traces

Grafana doesn't collect data — it visualizes data that's already been collected. But HOW does that data get there? Enter Grafana Agent (and its successor, Grafana Alloy).

🟢 Beginner⏱️ ~20 min🔌 Collection
🔌 DATA COLLECTION — Before Grafana Can Visualize
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                      THE DATA COLLECTION LAYER                            │
  │                                                                          │
  │  ┌─────────────┐   ┌─────────────┐   ┌─────────────┐                    │
  │  │ 🐍 anihpj   │   │ ☸️ K8s Nodes│   │ 📱 Mobile   │                    │
  │  │ /metrics    │   │ kubelet      │   │ App events  │                    │
  │  └──────┬──────┘   └──────┬──────┘   └──────┬──────┘                    │
  │         │                 │                 │                            │
  │         ▼                 ▼                 ▼                            │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │              🔌 GRAFANA AGENT (or Alloy)                          │   │
  │  │                                                                  │   │
  │  │  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐           │   │
  │  │  │ 📈 Metrics   │  │ 📜 Logs      │  │ 🔍 Traces    │           │   │
  │  │  │ (Prometheus  │  │ (Loki        │  │ (OTLP        │           │   │
  │  │  │  remote_write)│  │  push)      │  │  exporter)   │           │   │
  │  │  └──────┬───────┘  └──────┬───────┘  └──────┬───────┘           │   │
  │  └─────────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────┼───────────────────┘   │
  │            ▼                 ▼                 ▼                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │         🎯 BACKENDS: Mimir        Loki          Tempo            │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
ComponentGrafana AgentGrafana Alloy (Newer)Prometheus + Promtail
StatusStable, widely usedNew — recommendedTraditional approach
Metrics✅ Prometheus remote_write✅ Built-in✅ Scrape + remote_write
Logs✅ Loki push✅ Built-in✅ Promtail (separate binary)
Traces✅ OTLP export✅ Built-in❌ Not supported
Single Binary?✅ Yes✅ Yes❌ No (multiple daemons)
Exam RelevanceMediumLow (too new)High (know the ecosystem)

Key exam takeaways: 1) Grafana Agent = single binary replacing 3+ tools (Prometheus + Promtail + OTel collector), 2) Uses remote_write to ship metrics, 3) For logs: tails files → pushes to Loki, 4) For traces: receives OTLP → exports to Tempo. The agent is NOT Grafana itself — it's the data collection layer BEFORE Grafana. Exam trap: "Grafana Agent stores metrics" → WRONG. Grafana Agent SHIPS metrics, Mimir/Prometheus STORES them, Grafana VISUALIZES them.

📖 Agent vs Grafana: What's the Difference?

Grafana = Visualization + Alerting UI (port :3000). Grafana Agent = Lightweight daemon that collects and ships telemetry to backends. Think: Agent = "plumbing" (moves data), Grafana = "dashboard" (shows data). You need BOTH.

🎯 Certified Grafana Associate — Quick Exam Strategy

✅ DO This

  • ✅ 3-Pass: Sweep (30m) → Deep Dive (40m) → Verify (20m)
  • ✅ Flag hard Qs immediately — NEVER get stuck
  • ✅ Eliminate 2 wrong answers first (50%→25% odds)
  • ✅ Read EVERY word — especially NOT/EXCEPT/MOST
  • ✅ Focus study on Dashboards (25%) + Data Sources (20%)

❌ DON'T Do This

  • ❌ Spend 5+ min on one question
  • ❌ Leave any question blank (no penalty!)
  • ❌ Confuse Contact Points with Notification Policies
  • ❌ Forget $__timeFilter() in SQL queries
  • ❌ Study LGTM (10%) more than Dashboards (25%)

⚠️ Top 5 Must-Memorize Concepts
  1. rate(metric[5m]) — ALWAYS use rate() on counters, NOT raw values
  2. $__timeFilter(column) — ALWAYS add this macro to SQL time-series queries
  3. GF_SECTION_KEY — Environment variable override pattern for grafana.ini
  4. Provisioned resources = READ-ONLY in UI — edit YAML files, not the UI
  5. Grafana does NOT store your monitoring data — it's purely a visualization layer

📋 5-Phase Implementation Roadmap

Phase 1: Foundation (Ch 1-3)

Install Grafana OSS via Docker. Login. Add Prometheus data source. Create your first dashboard with 3 panels. Time: 2 hours

Phase 2: Dashboards (Ch 4-11)

Build the anihpj production dashboard: RED metrics panels, USE resource panels, PostgreSQL business panels. Apply transformations (join Prometheus + Postgres data). Add variables (environment, service, endpoint). Time: 8 hours

Phase 3: Alerting (Ch 12-14)

Create 6 production alert rules for anihpj. Configure Slack + PagerDuty contact points. Build notification routing tree. Set up silences and mute timings for maintenance windows. Time: 4 hours

Phase 4: Admin & Operations (Ch 15-17)

Set up Organizations (prod/staging). Create Teams (SRE, Dev, Business). Assign folder permissions. Provision dashboards as code via YAML files. Setup Service Accounts for CI/CD automation. Time: 3 hours

Phase 5: Production & LGTM (Ch 18-23)

Deploy to Kubernetes with Helm. Add Loki for logs (LogQL), Tempo for traces, Mimir for scaled metrics. Configure HA (multiple replicas, Postgres backend). Set up GitOps with ArgoCD. Time: 6 hours

⚡ Quick-Start: Zero to First Dashboard in 10 Minutes

Step 1: Start the Stack

cd anihpj && docker-compose up -d — Starts Django, PostgreSQL, Redis, Prometheus, Grafana, Loki, Promtail. All 7 services in one command. Grafana at http://localhost:3000.

Step 2: Login

Default credentials: admin / admin. You'll be prompted to change the password. Do it. This is your Grafana admin account.

Step 3: Add Prometheus Data Source

Connections → Data Sources → Prometheus. URL: http://prometheus:9090. Click Save & Test. Green checkmark? You're connected.

Step 4: Create Dashboard

Dashboards → New → Add Visualization. Select Prometheus data source. Enter query: rate(django_http_requests_total[5m]). Click Run Query. You see a graph!

Step 5: Add More Panels

Add: error rate panel (status=~"5.."), P95 latency panel (histogram_quantile(0.95, ...)), request count stat. Arrange in a row. Save as "anihpj Overview".

Step 6: Set Up First Alert

Alerting → Alert Rules → New. Query: error rate > 5%. Evaluate every 1m, for 2m. Contact point: your email. Now you're monitoring!

💡 Key Insight: What Grafana Does NOT Do

Grafana does NOT store your monitoring data. It is purely a visualization and alerting layer. Prometheus stores metrics. Loki stores logs. PostgreSQL stores business data. Grafana queries them all and displays the results. This is the #1 misconception the exam tests — if a question says "Grafana stores metrics," that's the WRONG answer.

⚠️ Top 8 Implementation Pitfalls — And How to Avoid Them
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ PITFALL                       │ HOW TO AVOID IT                          │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 1. Using SQLite in production │ Always use PostgreSQL for HA + backups   │
  │    → Lost all dashboards      │ Ch 18 — Grafana DB Configuration        │
  │                               │                                         │
  │ 2. Forgetting $__timeFilter() │ ALWAYS add this to SQL time queries.    │
  │    → Dashboard scans ALL data │ Without it: full table scan, 30s load   │
  │                               │ With it: index seek, 200ms load         │
  │                               │                                         │
  │ 3. Rate() on gauges           │ rate() = COUNTERS only! Gauges = raw.   │
  │    → Wrong values displayed   │ Counter: cumulative (always ↑). Gauge:  │
  │                               │ absolute (up & down). Ch 6 covers this. │
  │                               │                                         │
  │ 4. No "NoData" alerts         │ Set NoData=Alerting for ALL critical    │
  │    → Silent outage for days   │ rules. If metrics stop, that IS an      │
  │                               │ emergency. Ch 13.2d.                    │
  │                               │                                         │
  │ 5. Disabling alerts instead   │ Use SILENCES for temporary muting.      │
  │    of silencing               │ Disabling rules → forget to re-enable   │
  │                               │ → 3 days later: outage, no alert.       │
  │                               │                                         │
  │ 6. Editing provisioned        │ Provisioned resources are READ-ONLY in  │
  │    dashboards in UI           │ the UI. Edit the YAML file instead,     │
  │                               │ then restart. Ch 17 covers this.        │
  │                               │                                         │
  │ 7. No label standardization   │ Agree on labels: {env, team, service,   │
  │    → Can't route alerts       │ severity}. Without labels, notification │
  │                               │ policies can't route. Label EVERYTHING. │
  │                               │                                         │
  │ 8. All alerts go to one       │ Build a routing tree: critical→PagerDuty│
  │    channel → alert fatigue    │ warning→Slack, info→Email. Ch 14.3.     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Chapter 1: What is Grafana? — The Big Picture 🟢 Beginner

Chapter 1: What is Grafana? — The Complete Introduction for Absolute Beginners

Before diving into dashboards, panels, and PromQL queries, let's understand why Grafana exists, what problems it solves, and how it fits into the modern observability stack. We'll use our anihpj/jobpost Django app — deployed on Docker & Kubernetes — to make every concept concrete and practical.

  • ✅ Grafana internal architecture — how the Go backend, React frontend, and plugins work together

Understanding "Why" Matters on the Grafana Associate Exam

The Grafana Associate exam tests both concepts and practical skills. Chapter 1 lays the foundation for everything that follows. Key exam points from this chapter:

  • Grafana is NOT a data store — it's purely a visualization layer. This is the #1 misconception tested on the exam.
  • Data Source plugins are what connect Grafana to your actual data. Know the difference between Server-side and Browser (Direct) access modes.
  • Dashboards → Panels → Queries hierarchy is fundamental. Every panel has a query, every dashboard has panels.
  • Provisioning vs UI configuration — the exam expects you to know both approaches.

1.1 The World Before Grafana — Why We Need Visualization

Imagine you've deployed anihpj — a Django job board application with the jobpost module where employers list jobs filtered by Skills, Location, and Author. It's running on Docker containers orchestrated by Kubernetes. But something's wrong — users are complaining the site is slow. How do you figure out what's happening?

📖 Key Term: Observability

Observability is the ability to understand what's happening inside your system by examining its outputs — metrics, logs, and traces — without deploying new code. Grafana is the visualization layer of observability: it doesn't collect data, but it makes data understandable.

The "Dark Age" — Managing anihpj WITHOUT Grafana

😰 Debugging anihpj WITHOUT Grafana (The Pain)
  • 🔴 SSH into Server-01: Run top — CPU looks fine...
  • 🔴 SSH into Server-02: Run free -m — Memory at 92%! But is that normal?
  • 🔴 SSH into Server-03: Run df -h — Disk at 95% on /var/log
  • 🔴 Tail Django logs: tail -f /var/log/anihpj/django.log | grep ERROR
  • 🔴 Check PostgreSQL: psql -c "SELECT count(*) FROM jobpost_jobpost WHERE status='active'"
  • 🔴 Check Redis: redis-cli INFO stats | grep connected_clients
  • 🔴 Check Nginx: tail -1000 /var/log/nginx/access.log | awk '{print $9}' | sort | uniq -c
  • 💀 5 different tools. 7 different commands. ZERO unified view. MTTR: 45+ minutes.
🎉 Debugging anihpj WITH Grafana (The Solution)
  • ✅ Open ONE browser tab → "anihpj Production Overview" dashboard
  • ✅ Row 1: HTTP Stats — Request Rate (850/s), Error Rate (2.3%), P95 Latency (340ms)
  • ✅ Row 2: System Health — All 3 nodes: CPU 45%, Memory 78%, Disk 62%
  • ✅ Row 3: Application Logs — Last 50 errors from Loki, filterable by endpoint
  • ✅ Row 4: Business Metrics — 1,247 active jobs, 89 new today, top skill: "Python"
  • ✅ Problem identified in 2 minutes. Alert sent to Slack before users notice.

💡 The Key Insight

The shift from "monitoring" to "observability" is the shift from "Is my server up?" to "Why is checkout latency p95 at 4.2 seconds?" Grafana is the tool that enables this transition — it's the window through which you observe your entire system.

1.2 Grafana Defined — The Simple Answer

📖 Official Definition

"Grafana is an open-source analytics and interactive visualization platform. It provides charts, graphs, and alerts when connected to supported data sources."

In plain English: Grafana is the control tower for your data. It connects to where your data lives (databases, monitoring systems, log aggregators), lets you build beautiful dashboards, and alerts you when things go wrong. It does NOT store your data — it just visualizes it.

🏗️ THE PORT CITY ANALOGY — How Grafana Works
                        ┌─────────────────────────┐

1.3 Core Concepts & Terminology — The Building Blocks

Before you can use Grafana, you need to understand its core vocabulary. Every concept below is a fundamental building block — and they're ALL tested on the Grafana Associate exam.

🔌
Data Source

What: Where Grafana pulls data FROM.
anihpj example: Prometheus (for server metrics), PostgreSQL (for job/skills data), Loki (for app logs). Each has its own connection URL, credentials, and query language.

📊
Dashboard

What: A collection of panels on one page.
anihpj example: "Production Overview" dashboard shows HTTP metrics, system health, business KPIs — all on ONE screen. Dashboards are stored as JSON objects.

📈
Panel

What: A single visualization (graph, gauge, table, stat).
anihpj example: A Time Series panel showing "HTTP Requests/sec" for the last 6 hours. Each panel has: a data source, a query, and a visualization type.

🔍
Query

What: The request sent to a Data Source to retrieve data.
anihpj example: rate(django_http_requests_total[5m]) — PromQL query that calculates per-second request rate over 5 minutes.

🔄
Transformation

What: Data processing BETWEEN query and visualization.
anihpj example: Merge Prometheus server metrics with PostgreSQL business data into one unified table for a "Business Impact" dashboard.

🎛️
Variable

What: Dynamic placeholder ($var) for interactive dashboards.
anihpj example: $environment dropdown switches between prod/staging/dev — one dashboard serves all environments! Variables are the key to reusable dashboards.

🔔
Alert Rule

What: A condition that triggers a notification.
anihpj example: "IF error rate > 5% for 5 minutes THEN send Slack message to #alerts-dev". Alert rules are evaluated on a schedule.

📧
Contact Point

What: Where alerts are sent.
anihpj example: Slack for dev warnings, PagerDuty for critical production alerts, Email for daily digests. Multiple contact points can be configured per alert.

🏢
Organization

What: A tenant boundary for users & resources.
anihpj example: Engineering org (SRE dashboards), Product org (business dashboards), Analytics org (data dashboards). Each org has its own users, data sources, and dashboards.

📁
Folder

What: Namespace for organizing dashboards.
anihpj example: /anihpj/Production, /anihpj/Staging, /anihpj/Development — hierarchical organization with folder-level permissions.

📍
Annotation

What: Event markers overlaid on dashboard graphs.
anihpj example: Vertical lines marking "v2.1 Deployed", "DB Migration", "Incident #42 resolved". Annotations provide context — "Why did latency spike at 2 PM? Oh, that's when we deployed!"

🔗
Dashboard Link

What: Navigation between related dashboards.
anihpj example: Click on a spike in the "Overview" dashboard → drill down to "Django Detailed Metrics" dashboard. Links create a connected dashboard ecosystem.

1.4 Grafana Architecture — How It All Connects

Grafana's architecture is elegantly simple. Understanding it helps you troubleshoot, configure, and extend Grafana effectively.

🧠 GRAFANA INTERNAL ARCHITECTURE
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                    GRAFANA SERVER (Go Backend)                        │
│                                                                      │
│  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
│  │                     🔌 PLUGIN SYSTEM                          │   │
│  │  ┌────────────────┐  ┌────────────┐  ┌──────────────────┐    │   │
│  │  │ Data Source    │  │   Panel    │  │   App Plugins    │    │   │
│  │  │ Plugins(100+)  │  │ Plugins(13)│  │  (Full Apps)     │    │   │
│  │  └────────────────┘  └────────────┘  └──────────────────┘    │   │
│  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
│                                                                      │
│  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌────────────────────────┐     │
│  │ 🔐 AUTH      │  │ 📊 API Layer │  │ 🔔 ALERTING ENGINE     │     │
│  │ Built-in/LDAP│  │ REST API     │  │ Scheduler→State→Notify │     │
│  │ OAuth2/SAML  │  │ /api/...     │  │                        │     │
│  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └────────────────────────┘     │
│                                                                      │
│  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
│  │  💾 DATABASE (SQLite / PostgreSQL / MySQL)                    │   │
│  │  Stores: Users, Orgs, Dashboards(JSON), DataSources,          │   │
│  │          AlertRules, API Keys, Preferences, Annotations       │   │
│  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
💡 Stateless Design

Grafana itself is stateless — it stores ONLY configuration (dashboard JSON, data source settings, user preferences) in its database. Your actual data lives in Prometheus, Loki, PostgreSQL, etc. Grafana queries these sources on-demand when you open a dashboard. This means you can restart, upgrade, or replace the Grafana server without losing any of your monitoring data.

🏢 Grafana Enterprise — Complete Feature List (Exam-Relevant)

The official docs list these Enterprise-only features. Exam tip: If a question mentions RBAC, SAML, reporting, audit logs, or data source permissions — the answer is likely "Enterprise edition."

CategoryEnterprise FeatureWhat It Does
AuthSAML AuthenticationSingle sign-on with SAML providers (Okta, Azure AD, Ping)
AuthEnhanced LDAPActive LDAP synchronization with group mapping
AuthTeam SyncAuto-map OAuth/LDAP/SAML groups → Grafana teams
AuthProtected RolesPrevent role conversion when switching auth providers
AccessRBAC (Advanced)Fine-grained role-based access control
AccessData Source PermissionsRestrict query access to specific teams/users
AccessQuery & Resource CachingCache query results to reduce data source load
ReportingPDF ReportsGenerate scheduled PDF reports from any dashboard
ReportingExport Dashboard as PDFOne-click PDF export of any dashboard
AuditAudit LoggingTrack all changes for compliance and security

AdminUsage InsightsDashboard and data source usage analytics

OperationsSettings at RuntimeUpdate grafana.ini settings without restart
Data30+ Enterprise PluginsSplunk, Datadog, New Relic, MongoDB, Oracle, Snowflake, Salesforce, Jira, GitLab, etc.

Key Exam Point: Features available in OSS: PromQL, Grafana Alerting, dashboards, variables, transformations, provisioning, OAuth/OIDC, built-in data sources. Features that are Enterprise-ONLY: SAML, advanced RBAC, PDF reporting, audit logs, data source permissions, custom branding. Don't confuse Cloud features with Enterprise features — Grafana Cloud includes some Enterprise features in paid tiers.

1.6 Your First Grafana Dashboard — anihpj "Hello World"

Let's walk through creating your very first Grafana dashboard, step by step. By the end of this section, you'll have a working dashboard showing live metrics from the anihpj Django application.

1
Install Grafana

docker run -d -p 3000:3000 grafana/grafana

2
Login

http://localhost:3000
admin / admin

3
Add Data Source

Connections → Prometheus
URL: http://prometheus:9090

4
Create Dashboard

+ New Dashboard
→ Add Panel

5
Build Query

rate(django_http_requests_total[5m])

6
Save & Share!

Your first graph is LIVE! 🎉

📊 HOW A DASHBOARD RENDERS — Request Flow
  Step 1: 🌐 Browser → GET /api/dashboards/uid/anihpj-overview → Grafana Server
  Step 2: 🔌 Grafana executes queries IN PARALLEL:
          → Prometheus: rate(django_http_requests_total[5m])
          → PostgreSQL: SELECT COUNT(*) FROM jobpost_jobpost WHERE ...
          → Loki: {job="anihpj"} |= "ERROR"
  Step 3: 📊 Transform → Map to visualization → Render SVG/Canvas in browser
  Step 4: 🔄 Auto-refresh every 30s → Repeat Steps 2-3
  ⏱️ Typical dashboard render: 50-200ms for 10 panels

1.8 Real-World Grafana Use Cases

🖥️
Infrastructure Monitoring

Monitor 500+ VMs: CPU, memory, disk, network. Node Exporter → Prometheus → Grafana dashboards with automated alerts.

📱
Application Performance (APM)

RED metrics (Rate, Errors, Duration) for microservices. OpenTelemetry instrumentation → Tempo traces → Grafana dashboards.

💼
Business Intelligence

Sales dashboards from PostgreSQL/MySQL. "Revenue by region", "Top products", "Customer acquisition trend" — all in Grafana.

🏭
IoT & Sensor Data

Temperature, humidity, energy consumption from 10,000+ sensors. InfluxDB → Grafana with real-time gauges and heatmaps.

🚀
DevOps & CI/CD

Build times, deployment frequency, failure rates. Jenkins/GitLab metrics → Grafana dashboards for DORA metrics.

🎯
anihpj Unified Platform

Technical metrics (HTTP rate, latency, errors) + Business metrics (active jobs, skills demand, user growth) — ONE dashboard for SRE AND Product teams.

📝 Chapter 1 — Grafana Practice Questions

Q1

What is Grafana? Choose the MOST accurate definition.

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Grafana is an open-source analytics and interactive visualization platform that connects to data sources, creates dashboards and panels, and alerts on conditions. It is NOT a data store — it queries external data sources and visualizes the results.

💡 EXPLANATION

The exam frequently tests this distinction. Grafana does NOT store your metrics/logs/traces — it only stores its own configuration (dashboards, users, data source connections). Your actual data lives in Prometheus, Loki, PostgreSQL, etc. Grafana queries these sources on-demand when you view a dashboard.

Q2

Which of the following is NOT a core Grafana concept?

  A) Data Source    B) Dashboard    C) Data Lake    D) Panel    E) Alert Rule
Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

C) Data Lake is not a core Grafana concept. Grafana connects TO data sources (which could include data lakes), but "Data Lake" is not a Grafana feature.

💡 EXPLANATION

Core Grafana concepts: Data Source (where data comes from), Dashboard (collection of panels), Panel (single visualization), Alert Rule (condition→notification), Contact Point (where alerts go), Organization (tenant boundary), Variable (dynamic placeholder). "Data Lake" is an infrastructure concept, not a Grafana concept.

Q3

Where does Grafana store your monitoring data (metrics, logs, traces)?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Nowhere. Grafana does NOT store monitoring data. It stores only configuration (dashboards, users, data source settings) in its internal database (SQLite/PostgreSQL/MySQL). Your actual monitoring data stays in external systems: Prometheus, Loki, Tempo, InfluxDB, PostgreSQL, etc.

💡 EXPLANATION

This is the #1 misconception tested on the exam. Grafana is the visualization LAYER, not the storage layer. It queries external data sources via plugins in real-time when you view a dashboard. Key implication: you can restart Grafana without losing any monitoring data.

Q4

What is the difference between a Dashboard and a Panel?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

A Dashboard is a collection of panels arranged on a page. A Panel is a single visualization (graph, gauge, table, stat, etc.) that queries one or more data sources. One dashboard can contain many panels; each panel is independent with its own query and visualization type.

💡 EXPLANATION

Hierarchy: Organization → Folder → Dashboard → Row → Panel → Query. Think of a dashboard as a webpage and panels as the widgets on that page. Each panel has: a data source, a query, a visualization type, and configuration options (colors, thresholds, legends, etc.).

Q5

You want to deploy Grafana for a 5-person startup team. Which option is most cost-effective?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Grafana Cloud Free Tier — includes 10,000 metrics, 50GB logs, 50GB traces, and 3 active users at no cost. For 5 users, the free tier covers 3, and you can add paid seats for the remaining 2 (~$29/user/month). Alternatively, self-hosted OSS is completely free with unlimited users.

💡 EXPLANATION

Grafana Cloud Free tier is perfect for startups: managed hosting (no server maintenance), automatic updates, built-in Loki/Tempo/Mimir. OSS is better if you have infrastructure expertise and want full control. Enterprise is for large orgs needing SAML, audit logging, and 24/7 support.

Q6

What is the Port 3000 used for in Grafana?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Port 3000 is the default HTTP port for the Grafana web UI and API. You access Grafana at http://localhost:3000 by default. This can be changed via GF_SERVER_HTTP_PORT environment variable or [server].http_port in grafana.ini.

💡 EXPLANATION

Default ports to know for the exam: Grafana=3000, Prometheus=9090, Loki=3100, Tempo=3200, Alertmanager=9093, PostgreSQL=5432. You can change any of these via configuration.

Q7

Which component handles user authentication in Grafana?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Grafana's Auth Module handles authentication. It supports multiple methods: Built-in (username/password in Grafana DB), LDAP, OAuth 2.0 / OpenID Connect (Google, GitHub, Okta, Azure AD), SAML (Enterprise), and Auth Proxy (nginx/Apache).

💡 EXPLANATION

Authentication is configured in grafana.ini under [auth] sections. Grafana also supports: anonymous access (public dashboards), sign-up toggle, and auto-assigning users to organizations on first login. SAML and advanced team sync are Enterprise features.

Q8

What does "Grafana is stateless" mean and why does it matter?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Grafana stores only configuration (dashboards, users, data source settings) — not your monitoring data. It queries external data sources in real-time. This means you can restart, upgrade, or replace a Grafana instance without losing any metrics, logs, or traces. It also means Grafana itself has minimal storage requirements.

💡 EXPLANATION

Practical implications: (1) Grafana's database is small (MB, not GB), (2) Horizontal scaling is possible — multiple Grafana instances can share a PostgreSQL backend, (3) High availability: if one Grafana instance dies, start another pointing to the same DB — all dashboards are back instantly.

Q9

Which of these is a valid Grafana deployment method?

  A) Docker container    B) Kubernetes Helm chart    C) Linux APT package
  D) macOS Homebrew      E) Grafana Cloud (SaaS)     F) All of the above
Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

F) All of the above. Grafana can be deployed as a Docker container, on Kubernetes via Helm chart, as a Linux package (APT/YUM), via macOS Homebrew, as a Windows MSI installer, or as a fully managed SaaS (Grafana Cloud). It also runs on ARM (Raspberry Pi).

💡 EXPLANATION

Grafana is written in Go and compiles to a single binary, making it extremely portable. The Docker image is the most popular deployment method. Kubernetes Helm chart is preferred for production. Grafana Cloud is the simplest (no infrastructure to manage).

Q10

Using the Ports & Ships analogy from this chapter, what does the Control Tower represent?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

The Control Tower represents Grafana itself. Just as a port's control tower has a unified view of all ships, berths, and cargo — Grafana provides a unified view of all your data sources, metrics, logs, and alerts. The ships are your data sources (Prometheus, PostgreSQL, Loki), the berths are where they dock (network connections), the cargo is the data they carry, and the lighthouse is Grafana's alerting system.

💡 EXPLANATION

The Port City analogy helps remember: (1) Grafana doesn't own the ships (data) — it just watches them, (2) Different berths = different data source types, (3) The lighthouse = alerting (signals danger), (4) The hangars = where applications run (Docker/K8s). This mental model makes Grafana's architecture intuitive.

Chapter 2: Installing & Configuring Grafana 🟢 Beginner

Chapter 2: Installing & Configuring Grafana — From Zero to Running Dashboard

Learn every installation method, master the grafana.ini configuration file, understand provisioning for GitOps, and set up the complete anihpj monitoring stack with Docker & Kubernetes. By the end of this chapter, you'll have Grafana running with Prometheus, Loki, and a pre-configured anihpj dashboard.

🟢 Beginner⏱️ ~2 hours📖 Hands-On🐳 Docker☸️ K8s

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ System requirements and supported platforms for Grafana
  • ✅ Five installation methods: Docker, Kubernetes (Helm), Linux (APT/YUM), macOS (Homebrew), Windows
  • ✅ The grafana.ini configuration file — every section explained with examples
  • ✅ Provisioning: Infrastructure as Code for dashboards, data sources, and alerts

  • ✅ Environment variables cheat sheet — 30+ essential GF_ variables
Installation & Configuration on the Exam

The Grafana Associate exam tests your knowledge of configuration methods, not just "click the UI." Key exam topics: grafana.ini sections ([server], [database], [security], [auth], [smtp]), environment variable override pattern (GF_SECTION_KEY), provisioning YAML structure, and Docker vs Kubernetes deployment differences. Expect questions like "Which environment variable changes the default HTTP port?"

2.1 System Requirements

📖 Minimum vs Recommended

Minimum: 255MB RAM, 1 CPU core — enough for basic dashboards with a few users.
Recommended (production): 1GB+ RAM, 2+ CPU cores — for 10+ concurrent users with complex dashboards.
Database: SQLite (default, fine for <10 users), PostgreSQL (10-100+ users), MySQL (alternative).
Disk: Minimal for Grafana itself (~100MB). Dashboards are small JSON files. Most storage is for the database (users, annotations, alert history).

💡 Browser Support

Grafana supports the latest 2 versions of Chrome, Firefox, Safari, and Edge. No Internet Explorer support. The frontend uses modern JavaScript (React + TypeScript) — keep your browser updated.

2.2 Installation Methods Deep Dive

5 WAYS TO INSTALL GRAFANA
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  🐳 DOCKER       ☸️ KUBERNETES   🐧 LINUX       🍎 macOS    🪟 WINDOWS │
  │  docker run      helm install    apt install    brew install  MSI     │
  │  -p 3000:3000    grafana/        grafana         grafana       installer│
  │  grafana/        grafana         systemctl       brew services          │
  │  grafana                         start           start                 │
  │                                                                        │
  │  ⚡ Fastest dev   🏗️ Production   🔧 Traditional  👨‍💻 Dev laptop  🏢 Corporate│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

a) Docker Installation

bash
# Quick start (ephemeral)
docker run -d -p 3000:3000 --name=grafana grafana/grafana

# With persistent storage
docker run -d -p 3000:3000 --name=grafana \
  -v grafana-storage:/var/lib/grafana \
  -e GF_SECURITY_ADMIN_PASSWORD=mysecurepass \
  -e GF_INSTALL_PLUGINS=grafana-clock-panel \
  grafana/grafana
💡 Docker Best Practices

Always use named volumes for persistence. Install plugins at container startup via GF_INSTALL_PLUGINS env var. For custom configs, mount grafana.ini as a volume: -v ./grafana.ini:/etc/grafana/grafana.ini.

b) Kubernetes Installation (Helm Chart)

bash
helm repo add grafana https://grafana.github.io/helm-charts
helm repo update
helm install grafana grafana/grafana \
  --set persistence.enabled=true \
  --set persistence.size=10Gi \
  --set adminPassword=mysecurepass

Key K8s features: PersistentVolumeClaim for dashboard storage, Ingress for external access, ServiceMonitor for Prometheus scraping, and sidecar container for dashboard provisioning from ConfigMaps.

c) Linux Installation (APT/YUM)

bash
# Ubuntu/Debian
sudo apt-get install -y software-properties-common
sudo add-apt-repository "deb https://packages.grafana.com/oss/deb stable main"
sudo apt-get update && sudo apt-get install grafana
sudo systemctl start grafana-server
sudo systemctl enable grafana-server

# Config: /etc/grafana/grafana.ini
# Data:   /var/lib/grafana/
# Logs:   /var/log/grafana/grafana.log

d) macOS Installation (Homebrew)

bash
brew install grafana
brew services start grafana
# Access at http://localhost:3000

e) Windows Installation

Download the MSI installer from grafana.com/grafana/download. Install as a Windows service. Configuration at C:\Program Files\GrafanaLabs\grafana\conf\grafana.ini.

2.3 Configuration File Deep Dive (grafana.ini)

The grafana.ini file (located at /etc/grafana/grafana.ini on Linux) controls EVERY aspect of Grafana. Every setting can also be overridden by an environment variable using the pattern GF_<SECTION>_<KEY>.

KEY grafana.ini SECTIONS (EXAM TOPIC)
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ SECTION          KEY SETTINGS                ENV VAR EXAMPLE       │
  │ ───────          ────────────                ───────────────       │
  │ [server]         http_port, domain,          GF_SERVER_HTTP_PORT   │
  │                  protocol, cert_file,        =8080                  │
  │                  root_url                                          │
  │                                                                   │
  │ [database]       type (sqlite3/postgres/     GF_DATABASE_TYPE      │
  │                  mysql), host, name,         =postgres              │
  │                  user, password, ssl_mode                          │
  │                                                                   │
  │ [security]       admin_user, admin_password, GF_SECURITY_ADMIN_    │
  │                  secret_key, cookie_secure,  PASSWORD=secure123    │
  │                  disable_gravatar                                  │
  │                                                                   │
  │ [auth]           disable_login_form,         GF_AUTH_GITHUB_       │
  │ [auth.github]    enabled, client_id,         ENABLED=true          │
  │ [auth.google]    client_secret, allowed_                           │
  │ [auth.ldap]      organizations                                     │
  │                                                                   │
  │ [smtp]           enabled, host, user,        GF_SMTP_ENABLED=true  │
  │                  password, from_address                            │
  │                                                                   │
  │ [alerting]       enabled, execute_alerts,    GF_ALERTING_ENABLED   │
  │                  evaluation_timeout          =true                  │
  │                                                                   │
  │ [paths]          data, logs, plugins,        GF_PATHS_DATA         │
  │                  provisioning                =/data/grafana        │
  │                                                                   │
  │ [users]          allow_sign_up,              GF_USERS_ALLOW_       │
  │                  auto_assign_org             SIGN_UP=false         │
  │                                                                   │
  │ [log]            mode (console/file),        GF_LOG_LEVEL          │
  │                  level (debug/info/warn/     =debug                │
  │                  error)                                            │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
📖 Environment Variable Override Pattern

Any grafana.ini setting can be overridden with GF_<SECTION>_<KEY>. Underscores in section names become underscores: [auth.github] → GF_AUTH_GITHUB_. This is critical for Docker/K8s deployments where you don't want to edit config files. Example: GF_SERVER_HTTP_PORT=8080 overrides [server].http_port.

2.4 Provisioning — Infrastructure as Code for Grafana

Provisioning allows you to define dashboards, data sources, and alert rules as code — stored in Git, deployed via CI/CD, and automatically loaded by Grafana at startup. No more clicking in the UI to set up a new environment!

yaml — provisioning/datasources/prometheus.yaml
apiVersion: 1
datasources:
  - name: Prometheus-Production
    type: prometheus
    url: http://prometheus:9090
    access: proxy
    isDefault: true
    editable: false  # Prevents UI edits (GitOps!)
yaml — provisioning/dashboards/anihpj.yaml
apiVersion: 1
providers:
  - name: 'anihpj-dashboards'
    folder: 'anihpj'
    type: file
    options:
      path: /etc/grafana/provisioning/dashboards/anihpj
💡 Provisioning Benefits for the Exam

The exam tests provisioning concepts: (1) Provisioned dashboards are read-only in the UI (prevents config drift), (2) Files are watched — Grafana auto-reloads on change, (3) editable: false enforces GitOps, (4) Provisioning is the PREFERRED method for production — not manual UI setup.

2.5 anihpj Docker + Grafana Setup

🐳 anihpj COMPLETE DOCKER COMPOSE STACK
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  docker-compose.yml                                                 │
  │                                                                     │
  │  Services:                                                          │
  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ │
  │  │ django   │ │postgres  │ │ redis    │ │prometheus│ │ grafana  │ │
  │  │ (anihpj) │ │ :5432    │ │ :6379    │ │ :9090    │ │ :3000    │ │
  │  │ :8000    │ │          │ │          │ │          │ │          │ │
  │  └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ │
  │                                          ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ │
  │                                          │ loki     │ │ promtail │ │
  │                                          │ :3100    │ │(log coll)│ │
  │                                          └──────────┘ └──────────┘ │
  │                                                                     │
  │  Networks: monitoring (internal) — all metrics/logs traffic         │
  │  Volumes: grafana-data, prometheus-data, postgres-data (persist)    │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
💡 The anihpj Dockerfile — Production-Ready Multi-Stage Build

Use a multi-stage Dockerfile: Stage 1 (builder) — install Python dependencies, collect static files. Stage 2 (runtime) — copy only what's needed, run with gunicorn as non-root user. This minimizes image size (~150MB vs 800MB for single-stage).

yaml — docker-compose.yml (anihpj full stack)
version: '3.8'
services:
  django:        # anihpj Django app
    build: ./anihpj
    ports: ["8000:8000"]
    environment:
      - DATABASE_URL=postgres://anihpj:secret@postgres:5432/anihpj
    depends_on: [postgres, redis]

  postgres:      # Business data & Grafana's own DB (production)
    image: postgres:15
    environment:
      POSTGRES_DB: anihpj
      POSTGRES_USER: anihpj
    volumes: [postgres-data:/var/lib/postgresql/data]

  redis:         # Cache & Celery broker
    image: redis:7-alpine

  prometheus:    # Metrics collection
    image: prom/prometheus
    volumes: [./prometheus.yml:/etc/prometheus/prometheus.yml]
    command: ['--config.file=/etc/prometheus/prometheus.yml']

  grafana:       # Visualization platform
    image: grafana/grafana
    ports: ["3000:3000"]
    environment:
      GF_SECURITY_ADMIN_PASSWORD: admin
      GF_INSTALL_PLUGINS: grafana-clock-panel
    volumes:
      - grafana-data:/var/lib/grafana
      - ./provisioning:/etc/grafana/provisioning

  loki:          # Log aggregation
    image: grafana/loki:latest

  promtail:      # Log collector (reads Django logs → Loki)
    image: grafana/promtail
    volumes: [./promtail-config.yml:/etc/promtail/config.yml]

volumes:
  postgres-data:
  grafana-data:
  prometheus-data:

🎯 Chapter 2 Exam Focus — Top 7 Things to Memorize
  • ✅ GF_SECTION_KEY pattern: Every grafana.ini setting can be overridden via env var
  • ✅ Default database = SQLite: Switch to PostgreSQL for production (>10 users, HA needed)
  • ✅ Provisioning = GitOps: Data sources, dashboards, alerts as YAML/JSON in provisioning/ directory
  • ✅ Provisioned resources = read-only in UI: Prevents config drift. Edit files, not the UI.
  • ✅ Docker env var for plugins: GF_INSTALL_PLUGINS installs at startup — no restart needed
  • ✅ CLI plugins need restart: grafana-cli plugins install → systemctl restart grafana-server
  • ✅ Default paths: Config=/etc/grafana/grafana.ini, Data=/var/lib/grafana/, Logs=/var/log/grafana/

📝 Chapter 2 — Grafana Practice Questions

Q1

Which environment variable changes the default Grafana HTTP port from 3000 to 8080?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

GF_SERVER_HTTP_PORT=8080 — This follows the pattern GF_<SECTION>_<KEY> where SECTION is SERVER and KEY is HTTP_PORT.

💡 EXPLANATION

The override pattern is critical for the exam: (1) prefix GF_, (2) convert section name to uppercase, (3) convert key to uppercase, (4) replace dots/hyphens with underscores. So [server].http_port → GF_SERVER_HTTP_PORT.

Q2

Where is the grafana.ini configuration file located on a Linux system?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

/etc/grafana/grafana.ini — This is the default location when installed via APT/YUM package manager.

💡 EXPLANATION

Key paths to know for the exam: config = /etc/grafana/grafana.ini, data = /var/lib/grafana/, logs = /var/log/grafana/, plugins = /var/lib/grafana/plugins, provisioning = /etc/grafana/provisioning/.

Q3

What is provisioning in Grafana and why is it the preferred production method?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Provisioning is defining Grafana resources (data sources, dashboards, alert rules) as YAML/JSON files in the provisioning directory. It's preferred because: (1) Version-controlled in Git, (2) Deployed via CI/CD, (3) Prevents config drift (provisioned resources can't be edited in UI), (4) Reproducible across environments.

💡 EXPLANATION

Provisioning files go in: /etc/grafana/provisioning/datasources/ (YAML), /etc/grafana/provisioning/dashboards/ (YAML config + JSON dashboards), /etc/grafana/provisioning/alerting/ (YAML). Files are watched for changes and auto-reloaded.

Q4

How do you install Grafana plugins in a Docker container at startup?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Use the GF_INSTALL_PLUGINS environment variable with a comma-separated list of plugin IDs: -e GF_INSTALL_PLUGINS="grafana-clock-panel,grafana-worldmap-panel"

💡 EXPLANATION

Alternatively, you can: (1) Use grafana-cli plugins install <plugin-id> inside the container, (2) Mount a plugins volume, (3) Extend the Docker image with a Dockerfile that pre-installs plugins. The env var method is simplest for Docker.

Q5

What database does Grafana use by default? When should you switch to PostgreSQL?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

SQLite (file-based) is the default. Switch to PostgreSQL or MySQL when: (1) More than 10 concurrent users, (2) High Availability needed (multiple Grafana instances sharing one DB), (3) You need proper backup/replication, (4) Production deployment.

💡 EXPLANATION

SQLite is embedded — no separate DB server needed. Perfect for dev/small deployments. PostgreSQL supports concurrent writes (multiple Grafana instances), replication, and better performance at scale. Configure via: GF_DATABASE_TYPE=postgres, GF_DATABASE_HOST=..., GF_DATABASE_NAME=grafana.

Q6

What Helm command installs Grafana on Kubernetes with persistent storage?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER
helm repo add grafana https://grafana.github.io/helm-charts
helm install grafana grafana/grafana \
  --set persistence.enabled=true \
  --set persistence.size=10Gi
💡 EXPLANATION

The Grafana Helm chart also supports: --set service.type=LoadBalancer for cloud, --set ingress.enabled=true for Ingress, --set adminPassword=.... Use helm show values grafana/grafana to see all configurable values.

Q7

What does the [server].root_url setting in grafana.ini control?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

root_url defines the full public URL where Grafana is accessible, including protocol, domain, and port. Example: https://grafana.example.com:3000. This is used for generating links in alert notifications, email invitations, and OAuth redirect callbacks.

💡 EXPLANATION

If Grafana is behind a reverse proxy (nginx), root_url should reflect the EXTERNAL URL users see, not the internal one. Example: Grafana runs on port 3000 internally, but nginx proxies https://monitoring.company.com → http://localhost:3000. Set root_url = https://monitoring.company.com.

Q8

How does Docker handle Grafana data persistence? What happens if you don't configure it?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Use Docker volumes: -v grafana-storage:/var/lib/grafana. Without a volume, ALL Grafana configuration (dashboards, users, data sources, API keys) is lost when the container is removed. With a volume, data survives container restarts and updates.

💡 EXPLANATION

The critical paths to persist: /var/lib/grafana/ (database, plugins), /etc/grafana/ (config files), /etc/grafana/provisioning/ (provisioning files). For production Docker, mount all three as volumes or bind mounts.

Q9

What is the purpose of the [security].secret_key in grafana.ini?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

The secret_key is used to sign authentication tokens, cookies, and other security-sensitive data. It MUST be a random, unique string. If changed, all existing user sessions are invalidated. In production, always set this — never use the default.

💡 EXPLANATION

Generate with: openssl rand -base64 32. This is the Grafana equivalent of Django's SECRET_KEY. Without a strong secret_key, session hijacking becomes possible. Environment variable: GF_SECURITY_SECRET_KEY.

Q10

You want to provision a Prometheus data source via YAML. Write the complete YAML file.

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER
# /etc/grafana/provisioning/datasources/prometheus.yaml
apiVersion: 1

datasources:
  - name: Prometheus
    type: prometheus
    url: http://prometheus:9090
    access: proxy
    isDefault: true
    editable: false
    jsonData:
      timeInterval: "15s"
💡 EXPLANATION

Key fields: apiVersion: 1 (required), name (display name), type (plugin ID), url (endpoint), access: proxy (Grafana proxies queries — most secure), isDefault: true (pre-selected in query editors), editable: false (prevents UI modifications — GitOps).

2.7 🛠️ Grafana CLI Command Reference — Every Command You'll Need

🛠️ GRAFANA-CLI — COMMAND REFERENCE
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  COMMAND: grafana-cli plugins install <plugin-id>                            │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────                                    │
  │  SYNTAX:   grafana-cli plugins install <plugin-id> [--pluginsDir <path>]    │
  │  PURPOSE:  Install a Grafana plugin from the official plugin repository       │
  │  EXAMPLE:  grafana-cli plugins install grafana-clock-panel                    │
  │  NOTE:     Requires Grafana server restart. Use GF_INSTALL_PLUGINS env var   │
  │            for Docker containers to auto-install at startup.                  │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  COMMAND: grafana-cli plugins list-remote                                     │
  │  SYNTAX:   grafana-cli plugins list-remote                                    │
  │  PURPOSE:  List all available plugins from the Grafana plugin repository      │
  │  EXAMPLE:  grafana-cli plugins list-remote | grep -i "worldmap"              │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  COMMAND: grafana-cli plugins ls                                              │
  │  SYNTAX:   grafana-cli plugins ls                                             │
  │  PURPOSE:  List all currently INSTALLED plugins on this Grafana instance      │
  │  EXAMPLE:  grafana-cli plugins ls                                             │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  COMMAND: grafana-cli admin reset-admin-password <new-password>              │
  │  SYNTAX:   grafana-cli admin reset-admin-password <new-password>             │
  │  PURPOSE:  Reset the admin user's password (when locked out of Grafana)       │
  │  EXAMPLE:  grafana-cli admin reset-admin-password myNewP@ssw0rd               │
  │  CAUTION:  Only works when Grafana is NOT running (shut down first!)          │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  COMMAND: grafana-server -config <file>                                      │
  │  SYNTAX:   grafana-server -config /path/to/custom.ini                         │
  │  PURPOSE:  Start Grafana with a custom configuration file                     │
  │  EXAMPLE:  grafana-server -config /etc/grafana/grafana.ini                    │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  COMMAND: systemctl start|stop|restart|status grafana-server                  │
  │  SYNTAX:   sudo systemctl <action> grafana-server                            │
  │  PURPOSE:  Manage the Grafana systemd service (Linux only)                    │
  │  EXAMPLE:  sudo systemctl restart grafana-server                              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🐳 DOCKER COMMANDS FOR GRAFANA
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  COMMAND: docker run -d -p 3000:3000 grafana/grafana                          │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────                                │
  │  SYNTAX:   docker run [OPTIONS] IMAGE [COMMAND]                              │
  │  OPTIONS:  -d         Run container in background (detached mode)             │
  │            -p H:P     Map host port H to container port P                     │
  │            --name N   Assign container name N                                 │
  │            -v H:C     Mount host path H to container path C                   │
  │            -e K=V     Set environment variable K=V                            │
  │            --restart  Restart policy (no|always|unless-stopped)               │
  │  PURPOSE:  Start a Grafana Docker container                                   │
  │  EXAMPLE:  docker run -d --name=grafana -p 3000:3000 \                       │
  │              -v grafana-data:/var/lib/grafana \                               │
  │              -e GF_SECURITY_ADMIN_PASSWORD=admin \                            │
  │              grafana/grafana                                                  │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  COMMAND: docker compose up -d                                                │
  │  SYNTAX:   docker compose up [OPTIONS] [SERVICE...]                          │
  │  OPTIONS:  -d           Detached mode (background)                            │
  │            --build      Rebuild images before starting                        │
  │            --force-recreate  Recreate containers even if unchanged            │
  │  PURPOSE:  Start all services defined in docker-compose.yml                   │
  │  EXAMPLE:  docker compose up -d grafana prometheus                           │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  COMMAND: docker exec -it grafana bash                                        │
  │  SYNTAX:   docker exec [OPTIONS] CONTAINER COMMAND                           │
  │  OPTIONS:  -i    Keep STDIN open (interactive)                                │
  │            -t    Allocate a pseudo-TTY (terminal)                              │
  │  PURPOSE:  Run a command inside a running Grafana container                   │
  │  EXAMPLE:  docker exec grafana grafana-cli plugins ls                         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

3.1 The Grafana UI Tour — Every Element Explained

🖥️ GRAFANA UI LAYOUT — ANNOTATED MAP
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  🔍 Ctrl+K Search    [⭐ Starred] [❓ Help] [👤 User Menu]    ← TOP BAR  │
  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │          │  │  🏠 Home > Dashboards > anihpj Production Overview     │  │
  │  │ SIDEBAR  │  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │  │
  │  │          │  │  │ ⏱️ Last 6 hours  🔄 30s  [📊 Variables: prod ▾] │  │  │

📊
Dashboards Menu

Starred: Bookmarked dashboards. Recent: Last viewed. Browse: All dashboards with search, tag filter, folder tree. The primary navigation hub.

🔍
Explore

Ad-hoc querying WITHOUT creating a dashboard. Perfect for debugging, investigating anomalies, or trying new queries before adding them to a panel.

🔔
Alerting

Manage alert rules, silences (maintenance mute), contact points (Slack/Email/PagerDuty), and notification policies (routing tree).

🔌
Connections

Add/manage Data Sources (Prometheus, Loki, PostgreSQL, etc.) and browse/install plugins. The "wiring" section of Grafana.

⚙️
Administration

Users, Teams, Organizations, Service Accounts, Settings (server config), Plugins management, and (Enterprise) Audit Logs.

👤
User Menu

Profile, Preferences (theme/timezone), Switch Organization, Sign Out. Also shows your assigned organization and role.

3.2 Dashboard Navigation & Organization

Folder Structure — anihpj Example

  📁 General (default folder)
  📁 anihpj/
     ├── 📁 00-Overview/        → Main production overview dashboard
     ├── 📁 01-Application/     → Django-specific metrics (HTTP, middleware, ORM)
     ├── 📁 02-Infrastructure/  → K8s nodes, pods, resource usage
     ├── 📁 03-Business/        → Jobs, users, skills, locations
     ├── 📁 04-Logs/            → Loki log dashboards by service
     └── 📁 99-SRE/             → On-call dashboards, alert history, SLO tracking
💡 Organization Best Practices

Use descriptive folder names with numeric prefixes for sorting. Add tags to dashboards (e.g., "production", "django", "sre") for cross-folder filtering. Star the 3-5 dashboards you check daily. Never put ALL dashboards in the "General" folder — it becomes unmanageable.

3.3 User Profile & Preferences

🎨
Theme

Dark (default), Light, or System (follows OS). Grafana's dark theme is iconic — but light is better for well-lit rooms.

🕐
Timezone

Default (browser), UTC, or local. Important: dashboard time ranges always display in your local timezone.

🏠
Home Dashboard

Set a default dashboard to see when you log in. Use your most-checked dashboard (e.g., "anihpj Overview").

🔑
API Keys / Service Accounts

Manage machine-to-machine auth tokens. Service Accounts (newer) replace API keys for automation.

3.6 Search & Discovery Features

🔍
Global Search (Ctrl+K)

Search dashboards, panels, alert rules, data sources, and commands from ONE search bar. The fastest way to navigate Grafana. Supports partial text matching.

🏷️
Dashboard Search

Filter by tag (tag:production), folder, or text. Sort by name, last updated, or alphabetical. Use tag:anihpj tag:django to narrow results.

⭐
Starred Dashboards

Bookmark frequently used dashboards. Stars are per-user. Your starred list is your "quick access" panel — keep it focused!

🕐
Recent Dashboards

Auto-tracked list of dashboards you've viewed. Convenient for returning to work you were doing earlier. Persistent across sessions.
