8.3 Time Range Controls (CRITICAL FOR EXAM)

Time Controls Are Heavily Tested

The exam expects you to know ALL time range options, time shift (compare today vs yesterday), refresh intervals (for NOC displays), and how to zoom on graphs. Time ranges can be passed via URL parameters.

⏱️ TIME RANGE CONTROLS — Every Option Explained
  ⏱️ PRESET RANGES:           🔄 REFRESH INTERVALS:       📐 CUSTOM OPTIONS:
  Last 5 minutes              Off (no auto-refresh)       Absolute: YYYY-MM-DD HH:mm:ss
  Last 15 minutes             5 seconds (NOC/TV)          From: 2026-06-08 00:00:00
  Last 30 minutes             10 seconds                  To:   2026-06-08 23:59:59
  Last 1 hour                 30 seconds (default)
  Last 3 hours                1 minute                    🔀 TIME SHIFT:
  Last 6 hours                5 minutes                   Compare current period to
  Last 12 hours               15 minutes                  previous: "today vs yesterday"
  Last 24 hours               30 minutes                  Shift: 1h, 1d, 1w, 1M
  Last 2 days                 1 hour                      Overlays older data (lighter
  Last 7 days                                              color) on current period
  Last 30 days                🌐 URL Parameters:
  Last 90 days                ?from=now-6h&to=now         🔍 ZOOM CONTROLS:
  Yesterday                   ?from=now-1d&to=now         Click-drag on graph to zoom IN
  Today so far                ?refresh=30s                Double-click to reset zoom
  Last 5 years                                             Shift+drag: pan/scroll
💡 Time Range in URLs

You can share a specific time range by adding ?from=now-6h&to=now to the dashboard URL. Combine with variables: ?from=now-1d&to=now&var-environment=prod. This is tested on the exam — know that URL parameters can set both time and variables.

8.5 Dashboard Sharing & Export — Updated

🔗
Share Link

URL with current time range + variable values. Recipient must have Grafana login. Includes ?from=, ?to=, and ?var-* parameters.

📸
Snapshot

Static, publicly accessible rendering. NO Grafana login needed. Optional expiration time. Great for sharing a specific moment's data with external stakeholders.

🌐
Public Dashboard (v9+)

Live, read-only dashboard viewable without login. Queries run in real-time. Perfect for public status pages. Can be toggled on/off anytime.

📄
Export JSON

For version control (Git), provisioning, or migrating between Grafana instances. The JSON contains panels[], templating{}, annotations{}, links[] sections.

🖼️
Embed (iframe)

Embed panel or dashboard in external websites. anihpj: embed "Jobs Posted Today" stat on company intranet homepage.

📊
Report (Enterprise)

Schedule PDF reports of dashboards. Email to stakeholders on a schedule (daily, weekly). Enterprise feature only.

8.5b Dashboard Links — Connect Dashboards Together

🔗 Dashboard Links vs Share Links — Don't Confuse Them!

Dashboard Links (this section) = navigation from one dashboard TO another dashboard. Share Links (section 8.5) = URL you send to someone to VIEW a dashboard. Different concepts, both called "links"!

🔗 DASHBOARD LINKS — Navigation Between Dashboards
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │           anihpj Production Overview (Main Dashboard)         │
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌─────────────────────┐  ┌─────────────────────┐           │
  │  │ 🔗 Links (Top Bar)  │  │ 🔗 Links (Panel)    │           │
  │  │ "Django Details"    │  │ "View Pod Logs"     │           │
  │  │ "K8s Nodes"         │  │ "Trace Request"     │           │
  │  │ "Business Metrics"  │  │ "DB Query Analysis" │           │
  │  └─────────┬───────────┘  └─────────┬───────────┘           │
  └────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┘
               │                          │
     ┌─────────▼──────────┐    ┌──────────▼─────────────────┐
     │ Django HTTP Details│    │ Loki Log Explorer          │
     │ (Filtered to       │    │ (Pre-filtered to           │
     │  selected service) │    │  selected pod + time)      │
     └────────────────────┘    └────────────────────────────┘

Two types of Dashboard Links:

🔝
Dashboard-Level Links

Appear in the top-right corner of the dashboard. Configured in Dashboard Settings → Links. Can pass current time range and variable values. Use for: Related dashboards, documentation links, runbook URLs.

📊
Panel-Level Links

Appear in panel context menu (click panel title → "More..."). Can use panel data as link parameters. Use for: Drill-down from summary panel → detailed panel, linking to external systems (AWS console, PagerDuty incident).

Link Configuration Options: Type: Dashboard (internal) or Link (external URL). URL: Can include template variables — ${__url} for current dashboard URL, $variableName for dashboard variables, ${__value.raw} for panel data value. Time Range: Pass current time range to linked dashboard. Tags: Filter linked dashboards by tags. Open in new tab: Toggle.

📖 Real-World anihpj Example

Dashboard Link 1 (dashboards): "anihpj Overview" → Dashboard Link "K8s Pod Details" — passes selected namespace and pod name via variables, so clicking shows only the relevant pod. Dashboard Link 2 (URL): "anihpj Overview" → Link "PagerDuty On-Call" → https://anihpj.pagerduty.com/incidents. Panel Link (drill-down): "Error Rate Panel" → Link "View Error Logs in Loki" — uses ${__data.fields.endpoint} to filter Loki to the specific endpoint that's erroring.

8.7 Tags & Organization — anihpj Folder Structure

📁 anihpj DASHBOARD FOLDER STRUCTURE
  📁 /anihpj/00-Overview/
     └── "anihpj Production Overview" — Main SRE dashboard (all metrics at a glance)
  📁 /anihpj/01-Application/
     └── "Django HTTP Metrics" — Request rate, latency, status codes by endpoint
     └── "Django Middleware" — Authentication, CSRF, session middleware performance
     └── "Django ORM Performance" — DB query counts, slow queries, connection pool
  📁 /anihpj/02-Infrastructure/
     └── "Kubernetes Nodes" — Node CPU, memory, disk, network per node
     └── "K8s Pods Health" — Pod status, restarts, CrashLoopBackOff detection
     └── "Container Resources" — Per-container CPU/Memory usage and limits
  📁 /anihpj/03-Business/
     └── "Job Board KPIs" — Total jobs, new jobs today, applications per job
     └── "Skills Demand" — Top skills, growing skills, salary trends by skill
     └── "User Analytics" — Registrations, active users, geographic distribution
  📁 /anihpj/04-Logs/
     └── "Application Errors" — Django ERROR logs from Loki
     └── "Access Logs" — Nginx access patterns, 404 analysis, user agents
  📁 /anihpj/99-SRE/
     └── "On-Call Dashboard" — Current alert state, recent incidents, runbook links
     └── "Alert History" — All alert transitions (Normal→Pending→Alerting→Resolved)
     └── "SLO Tracking" — Service Level Objectives: 99.9% uptime, P95<500ms
💡 Organization Best Practices

Use numeric prefixes (00-, 01-, 02-) for automatic sorting. Add tags to dashboards (e.g., "production", "django", "sre", "anihpj") for cross-folder filtering. Star the 3-5 dashboards you check daily. Playlists rotate through dashboards for NOC/TV displays. Never put ALL dashboards in the "General" folder — it becomes unmanageable at scale.

8.8 Dashboard JSON Model — Understanding the Format

🧬 DASHBOARD JSON STRUCTURE — What's Inside
  {
    "dashboard": {
      "title": "anihpj Production Overview",
      "tags": ["anihpj", "production"],
      "timezone": "browser",
      "schemaVersion": 39,
      "panels": [           ← All visualization panels
        {
          "id": 1,
          "title": "Request Rate",
          "type": "stat",
          "datasource": { "type": "prometheus", "uid": "abc123" },
          "targets": [ { "expr": "rate(requests[5m])" } ],
          "fieldConfig": { "defaults": { "unit": "reqps" } },
          "gridPos": { "x": 0, "y": 0, "w": 6, "h": 4 }
        }
      ],
      "templating": {       ← Variables ($environment, $service, etc.)
        "list": [ { "name": "environment", "type": "custom", ... } ]
      },
      "annotations": {      ← Event markers on graphs
        "list": [ { "name": "Deployments", "datasource": "..." } ]
      },
      "links": [],          ← Navigation links between dashboards
      "time": { "from": "now-6h", "to": "now" }
    }
  }

Understanding the JSON model is essential for: provisioning (GitOps), API automation (create/update dashboards programmatically), debugging (find configuration issues), and bulk editing (search/replace across many dashboard JSON files). The gridPos uses a 24-column grid system — plan your layouts accordingly.

🎯 Chapter 8 Exam Focus — Top 7 Things to Memorize
  • ✅ Dashboard hierarchy: Organization → Folder → Dashboard → Row → Panel → Query
  • ✅ Sharing options: Link (same Grafana), Snapshot (static, no login), Public Dashboard (live, no login)
  • ✅ Provisioned dashboards = read-only in UI: Edit JSON files, not the UI. Prevent config drift.
  • ✅ 3 user roles: Viewer (read), Editor (create/edit), Admin (permissions + delete)
  • ✅ Folder permissions apply to ALL dashboards inside: Set once, inherited by children
  • ✅ Time Shift: Overlay previous period (1d ago) on current data for comparison
  • ✅ Dashboard versions: Every save = version. Diff viewer. Restore any version. Like Git for dashboards.

📝 Chapter 8 — Practice Questions

Q1

How do you share a dashboard with someone who doesn't have Grafana access?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Use Snapshot (Dashboard → Share → Snapshot) for a static rendering, or Public Dashboard (v9+) for a live read-only view. Neither requires Grafana login.

💡 EXPLANATION

Snapshot = static (captures current data). Public Dashboard = live (queries run in real-time). Use snapshots for sharing a specific time window. Use public dashboards for ongoing status pages.

Q2

Where do you find and restore previous dashboard versions?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Dashboard Settings (gear icon) → Versions. View any version, compare diffs, and click "Restore" to roll back. Each restore creates a new version (old version preserved).

💡 EXPLANATION

Every save = one version. The diff viewer shows exactly what changed between versions. This is like Git for dashboards. Key exam point: versions are in Dashboard Settings, NOT in Administration.

Q3

What is the difference between Dashboard Viewer, Editor, and Admin roles?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Viewer: Can only VIEW dashboards. Editor: Can create/modify panels and dashboards. Admin: Full control — can change permissions, delete, manage settings. Folder-level permissions override individual dashboard permissions.

💡 EXPLANATION
  Permissions Hierarchy:
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Grafana Admin (server-wide)         │
  │  └── Org Admin (per organization)   │
  │       └── Folder Admin (per folder) │
  │            ├── Editor (create/edit) │
  │            └── Viewer (read only)   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────┘
  For anihpj: SRE=Admin · Devs=Editor · Managers=Viewer
Q4

What does the Refresh Interval do on a dashboard?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Controls how often Grafana re-queries data sources and updates the visualization. Options: Off, 5s, 10s, 30s, 1m, 5m, 15m, 30m, 1h. Essential for NOC/TV displays that show real-time data.

💡 EXPLANATION
  Refresh Impact on Data Source Load:
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 5s refresh:  172,800 queries/day (10-panel)│
  │ 30s refresh:  28,800 queries/day           │
  │ 1m refresh:   14,400 queries/day           │
  │ 5m refresh:    2,880 queries/day           │
  │ Off:               10 queries/day           │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  Match refresh to need: NOC=10s · Ops=1m · Business=Off
Q5

What is an Annotation in Grafana and how do you add one?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

An Annotation is an event marker overlaid on dashboard graphs. Add via Dashboard settings → Annotations, or POST to /api/annotations. Use for: deployments, incidents, config changes.

💡 EXPLANATION

anihpj CI/CD adds annotations on every deployment: "v2.1 Deployed". When latency spikes, you can instantly correlate with deployments. Annotations provide context to your time-series data.

Q6

How do you export a dashboard for Git version control?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Dashboard → Share → Export → Save to file. Save the JSON in provisioning/dashboards/. Grafana auto-loads provisioned dashboards on startup. Provisioned dashboards are read-only in the UI.

💡 EXPLANATION

GitOps workflow: export JSON → commit to Git → CI/CD deploys to provisioning directory → Grafana auto-loads. The JSON contains panels[], templating{}, annotations{}, and links[] sections.

Q7

What is the purpose of dashboard folders?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Folders organize dashboards AND enable folder-level permissions. Set permissions once per folder — all dashboards inside inherit them. Grafana folders are flat (no nesting).

💡 EXPLANATION
  anihpj Folder Structure:
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 📁 /anihpj/00-Overview              │
  │ 📁 /anihpj/01-Application (Django)  │
  │ 📁 /anihpj/02-Infrastructure (K8s)  │
  │ 📁 /anihpj/03-Business (Jobs/Users) │
  │ 📁 /anihpj/04-Logs (Loki panels)    │
  │ 📁 /anihpj/99-SRE (On-call alerts)  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────┘
  Each folder = one permission set. Use numeric prefixes for sort.
Q8

What is a dashboard playlist?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

A Playlist auto-rotates through multiple dashboards at configurable intervals (10s, 30s, 1m). Used for NOC displays and office TV screens. Open in kiosk mode for full-screen: ?kiosk=tv.

💡 EXPLANATION

Create: Dashboards → Playlists → New Playlist. Add dashboards, set rotation interval, save. The playlist cycles through dashboards showing key metrics without manual intervention.

Q9

How does Time Shift help with analysis?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Time Shift overlays a previous period's data on current data. "Last 6 hours" with shift "1d" shows today AND yesterday. Answers: "Is traffic higher than yesterday?"

💡 EXPLANATION
  Time Shift Visual Comparison:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ ██ Today's traffic (solid line)          │
  │ ░░ Yesterday's traffic (dashed/shifted) │
  │                                           │
  │ Spikes at 9AM both days → normal pattern │
  │ Spike at 3PM today only → investigate!   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────┘
  Shifts: 1h · 1d · 1w · 1M — overlay for instant comparison
Q10

What happens to provisioned dashboards in the UI?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

With editable: false, provisioned dashboards are read-only. Grafana shows: "This dashboard is provisioned and cannot be saved from the UI." Modify via JSON file + restart/reload.

💡 EXPLANATION

GitOps prevents config drift. Source of truth = Git, not UI. Dev workflow: set editable:true temporarily → make changes → export JSON → set back to editable:false for production.

Chapter 9: Panel Types & Visualizations 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 9: Every Panel Type — When and How to Use Each

Grafana offers 13+ panel types — each designed for specific data visualization needs. Master all of them, learn when to use which, and build the perfect anihpj dashboard with the right visualizations for every metric.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~2 hours📊 Panels

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ All 13+ panel types and their best-use scenarios
  • ✅ Time Series, Stat, Gauge, Bar Gauge, Table — the "Big 5"
  • ✅ Panel overrides and field configuration
  • ✅ Decision matrix: which panel for which data type
Panel Types on the Exam

Expect questions like: "Which panel is best for showing a single important number?" → Stat. "Which panel for parts-of-a-whole?" → Pie Chart. "Which panel for service dependencies?" → Node Graph. Know the Big 5 and their use cases.

⚡ Panel Selection Speed Tip — The Time-Axis Rule

When asked "Which panel for X?", FIRST ask: "Does my data have TIME on the X-axis?" If YES → Time Series, State Timeline, or Heatmap. If NO → Stat, Gauge, Pie, Histogram, Table, Geomap. Wrong answer trap: Choosing Gauge when the question describes a metric over time — Gauge shows a point-in-time value. Time Series shows it over time. This single distinction answers 40% of panel-type exam questions.

9.1 Time Series (Graph) — The Most Common Panel

📊 TIME SERIES PANEL — Configuration Deep Dive
  VISUALIZATION MODES:              STACKING:
  • Line (default) — smooth/straight • Off — Each series independent
  • Bar — Vertical bars              • Normal — Stacked values (sum)
  • Points — Scatter/dots only       • 100% — Percentage stack

  GRADIENT FILL:                    AXIS CONFIG:
  • Opacity: 0-100 (fills area)     • Linear (default)
  • Fill below/below+above          • Logarithmic (log2 / log10)
  • Gradient mode: Opacity/Scheme   • Custom units: ms, bytes, %, ops/s
  • Line width: 1-10px              • Left AND Right Y-axes possible
  • Dash style: Solid/Dash/Dot      • Soft/hard min/max values
  • Connect nulls: Always/Never/Threshold

  THRESHOLDS:                       TOOLTIP:
  • Color-coded regions             • Single — One tooltip at a time
  • >80% = 🔴 Red (danger)          • All — All series tooltips shown
  • >60% = 🟡 Yellow (warning)      • Hidden — No tooltip
  • <60% = 🟢 Green (normal)
  • Absolute OR percentage-based    LEGEND:
                                    • Mode: List (classic) or Table
  TRANSFORM: Negative Y → Below X   • Placement: Bottom or Right
  axis (useful for error bars)      • Values shown: Min/Max/Avg/Total/Current
                                    • Custom: {{method}} - {{endpoint}}

9.2 Stat Panel — Big Numbers That Matter

🔢
Color Thresholds

Set thresholds for: absolute values (e.g., >100 = red) or percentage-based (top 20% = red). Colors: Green/Yellow/Red/Blue/Orange/Purple.

📈
Sparkline

Mini trend chart behind the big number. Shows recent history at a glance. Area or line style. Configurable time range independent of dashboard time.

🔄
Value Mapping

Map values to text: 1→"UP", 0→"DOWN", null→"NO DATA". Also: range-to-text mapping (0-10→"Low", 11-50→"Medium", 51+→"High").

📝
Text Mode

Display: Value only, Name only, or Name + Value. Auto/percent/bytes/custom unit formatting. Text alignment: Auto or Center.

9.3 Gauge Panel — At-a-Glance Health Status

Variants: Semi-circle (most common, like a car speedometer), Full circle (360°), Horizontal bar, Vertical bar. Threshold marks define color bands: set exact values for green→yellow→red transitions. Show/hide threshold labels and markers. Min/max values set the display range. Best for: resource utilization (CPU 0-100%), health scores (0-100), progress toward goals.

9.4 Bar Gauge Panel — Compact Multi-Value Status

Display modes: Basic (solid fill), Gradient (color transition), Retro LCD (segmented display). Horizontal or vertical orientation. Perfect for: multi-instance health (CPU across 10 servers), disk usage comparison (all nodes in one compact view), microservice health status (one bar per service). Much more space-efficient than individual Gauge panels for fleet views.

9.5 Table Panel — Structured Data Display (EXAM HEAVY)

📋 TABLE PANEL — Cell Display Modes
  CELL DISPLAY MODES (7 types):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Auto             → Grafana picks best mode based on data type        │
  │ Color text       → Text color changes (green=good, red=bad)          │
  │ Color background → Cell background changes (heatmap effect)          │
  │ Gauge            → Mini bar gauge inside each cell                   │
  │ Sparkline        → Mini trend chart (24h history in one cell!)       │
  │ JSON View        → Formatted JSON display for structured data        │
  │ Image            → Render image URL as actual image                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ADDITIONAL TABLE FEATURES:
  • Column sorting (click header, ascending/descending)
  • Client-side filtering (per-column value filters)
  • Pagination (rows per page: 10/25/50/100)
  • Footer calculations: Sum, Avg, Min, Max, Count per numeric column
  • Column width: Auto, fixed pixel, or hidden
  • Data links: Click cell → navigate to another dashboard (drill-down!)
  • anihpj: "Recent Job Posts" table with salary sparklines per job

9.6 All 13 Panel Types — Complete Reference

📊
1. Time Series

Trend over time. Lines/bars/points. The #1 most-used panel.

🔢
2. Stat

Single big number. With sparkline and thresholds. Hero metrics.

🎚️
3. Gauge

Health/utilization. Semi/full circle or bar. At-a-glance status.

📊
4. Bar Gauge

Multi-value comparison. Compact fleet views. Horizontal/vertical.

📋
5. Table

Structured data. Sortable/filterable. Rich cell display modes.

🥧
6. Pie Chart

Parts of a whole. Max 6-8 slices. Donut option. Distribution.

⏱️
7. State Timeline

Discrete state changes. Deployments, alerts, pod lifecycle.

🗂️
8. Status History

Grid version of State Timeline. Colored cells over time.

🟧
9. Heatmap

Data density over time. Latency distribution, error frequency.

📶
10. Histogram

Value distribution shape. NOT time-series. Statistical view.

🕸️
11. Node Graph

Service topology. Dependencies with metrics on edges.

🗺️
12. Geomap

Geographic data. User locations, server regions. Interactive.

🎨
13. Canvas

Free-form layout. Combine images, text, metrics. NOC displays.

Additional panels: Text/Markdown (documentation, runbooks), Logs (live Loki log tail in-dashboard), News (RSS/Atom feed for NOC), Trend (sparkline with current value), Dashboard List (searchable dashboard directory).

9.7 Choosing the Right Visualization — Decision Framework

🎨 PANEL TYPE DECISION MATRIX — Pick the Perfect Visualization
  YOUR QUESTION             BEST PANEL        WHY?
  ─────────────             ──────────        ─────
  How is X changing?       📊 Time Series    Shows trend over time
  What is X right now?     🔢 Stat           Single big number
  Is X healthy?            🎚️ Gauge          At-a-glance green/red
  What's the breakdown?    🥧 Pie/Donut      Parts of a whole
  Show me the raw data     📋 Table          Sortable, filterable
  When is X worst?         🟧 Heatmap        Color intensity over time
  What's the distribution? 📶 Histogram      Value buckets
  What happened & when?    ⏱️ State Timeline Discrete state changes
  How are services linked? 🕸️ Node Graph     Dependency visualization
  Where are my users?      🗺️ Geomap         Geographic coordinates
  Multiple instances OK?   📊 Bar Gauge      Compact multi-value
  Need documentation?      📝 Text/Markdown  Rich text, links, images

  🎯 anihpj DASHBOARD PANEL LAYOUT:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ ROW 1: 🔢Stat(Req/s) │ 🔢Stat(Err%) │ 🔢Stat(P95) │ 🔢Stat(Users)  │
  │ ROW 2: 📊 Time Series — Request Rate by Endpoint (multi-line)    │
  │ ROW 3: 🎚️Gauge(CPU) │ 🎚️Gauge(Mem) │ 📊BarGauge(Disk per pod)   │
  │ ROW 4: 📋 Table — Recent 500 Errors (sortable by endpoint)       │
  │ ROW 5: 🥧 Pie — Status Code Distribution (2xx/3xx/4xx/5xx)      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

9.8 Panel Overrides & Field Configuration

Field Overrides let you change visualization properties for SPECIFIC data fields without modifying the query. Override properties include: display name (rename series), color, unit, min/max values, decimals, thresholds, value mapping, data links, and "no value" display text. Example: rename "Value #A" → "P95 Latency (ms)" using regex /(.*) - (.*)/ → $2. Data links: Make any data point clickable — navigate to another dashboard with the clicked value as a parameter. Essential for building drill-down dashboard hierarchies.

🎯 Chapter 9 Exam Focus — Top 7 Things to Memorize
  • ✅ Time-Axis Rule: TIME on X? → Time Series/State Timeline/Heatmap. No TIME? → Stat/Gauge/Pie/Table/Geomap
  • ✅ Big 5 panels: Time Series (trends), Stat (single number), Gauge (health), Table (records), Pie (distribution)
  • ✅ Stat panel: Single value + sparkline + color thresholds + value mapping (1→UP, 0→DOWN)
  • ✅ Table panel: Sort, filter, paginate, footer calculations (sum/avg), cell display modes, data links
  • ✅ Heatmap vs Histogram: Heatmap = bucketed OVER TIME. Histogram = value distribution (no time axis)
  • ✅ State Timeline: Discrete state changes over time — deployments, pod states, alert states
  • ✅ 2 special panels: Node Graph (service topology), Geomap (lat/lon coordinates)

📝 Chapter 9 — Practice Questions

Q1

Which panel type is best for displaying a single important number like "Total Active Users"?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Stat Panel — designed for single values with optional sparkline, color thresholds, and value mapping. The Stat panel is optimized to display one key metric prominently.

💡 EXPLANATION

Stat panel features: big number display, sparkline (mini trend behind the number), color thresholds (green/yellow/red based on value), value mapping (1→"UP"), and text modes (value only, name only, or both).

Q2

When would you use a Heatmap panel instead of a Time Series?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Use Heatmap when you want to visualize data DENSITY over time — like "when do most errors occur?" or "what's the latency distribution across the day?" Heatmaps use color intensity to show concentration, while Time Series shows individual lines.

💡 EXPLANATION

Heatmap example: X-axis = time (24h), Y-axis = latency buckets (0-100ms, 100-500ms, 500ms-1s, 1s+), Color = count of requests in each bucket. Dark color = many requests at that latency. This reveals patterns invisible in line graphs.

Q3

What is the purpose of field overrides in a panel?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Field overrides let you customize visualization properties for SPECIFIC data fields without changing the query. You can: rename series, change colors, set custom units, configure thresholds per-field, hide fields, and add data links.

💡 EXPLANATION

Example: A query returns "Value #A" and "Value #B". Use field override to rename "Value #A" → "P95 Latency" and set unit to milliseconds. The query stays the same — only the display changes. Overrides are per-panel, not global.

Q4

What is the Gauge panel best used for?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Gauge is best for at-a-glance HEALTH STATUS — showing a value within a defined range with colored thresholds. Examples: CPU usage (0-100%), disk space (0-100%), target progress (0-target).

💡 EXPLANATION

Gauge variants: semi-circle (most common), full circle, horizontal bar, vertical bar. Configure min/max values and threshold marks (green < 60%, yellow < 80%, red >= 80%). The gauge clearly shows "is this value OK?"

Q5

Which panel would you use to show service dependencies?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Node Graph — visualizes nodes (services) and edges (connections). Shows which services call which, with request rates and error rates on connections.

💡 EXPLANATION

For anihpj: Django → PostgreSQL, Django → Redis, Django → External API. Color edges by latency (green=fast, red=slow) to spot bottlenecks.

Q6

Gauge vs Bar Gauge — what's the difference?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Gauge: Single value (semi-circle). Bar Gauge: Multiple values compared (horizontal/vertical bars). Gauge = "What's THIS server's CPU?" Bar Gauge = "CPU across ALL 10 servers."

💡 EXPLANATION

Bar Gauge is space-efficient for multi-instance monitoring. Both support thresholds and color mapping. Use Gauge for single-target views, Bar Gauge for fleet views.

Q7

What cell display modes does the Table panel support?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Auto, Color text, Color background, Gauge (mini bar), Sparkline (mini chart), JSON view, Image. This creates rich, information-dense tables with visual indicators inline.

💡 EXPLANATION

Example: Error Rate column uses Color background (green/red). Request Trend column uses Sparkline (24h mini chart in each cell). Combine multiple modes for powerful data presentations.

Q8

When would you use a State Timeline panel?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

For DISCRETE state changes: deployment status (deploying→running→failed), alert states (normal→pending→firing), pod lifecycle, CI/CD stages. Each state = colored bar.

💡 EXPLANATION

Unlike Time Series (continuous values), State Timeline shows categories. anihpj: 5 environments × deployment states = single-view deployment health dashboard.

Q9

How do you customize the legend in a Time Series?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Panel settings → Legend: Mode (List/Table), Placement (Bottom/Right), Values (Min/Max/Avg/Total/Current). Format with {{method}} - {{endpoint}} template variables.

💡 EXPLANATION

Add "Avg" to see averages, "Total" for cumulative, "Current" for latest. Table mode allows sorting. Template variables create descriptive legend entries from labels.

Q10

What is the Canvas panel?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Canvas is a free-form layout panel — place text, images, metrics, shapes anywhere. For: custom status boards, architecture diagrams with live metrics, branded NOC displays.

💡 EXPLANATION

anihpj: Canvas with company logo, live "Total Jobs" stat, system health gauges, and on-call engineer name — custom branded layout for office TV.

Chapter 10: Transformations — Shaping Data Before Display 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 10: Transformations — The Data Processing Pipeline

Transformations process data BETWEEN query execution and visualization rendering. Learn all 12 transformation types, chaining patterns, and how to combine Prometheus + PostgreSQL data into unified views.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~1 hour🔄 Data

10.1 What Are Transformations?

  Query A (Prometheus) ─┐
                          ├── Transform 1 (Merge) → Transform 2 (Filter) → Visualization
  Query B (PostgreSQL) ─┘

10.2 All 12 Transformation Types (EXAM TOPIC)

🔀
1. Merge

Combine results of MULTIPLE queries into one table. Essential for mixing data sources. Series or table mode.

📉
2. Reduce

Aggregate ALL rows into one result: sum, avg, min, max, count, last, first, range, difference.

🔍
3. Filter by Value

Include/exclude rows based on =, !=, <, >, between, contains, regex matches. Combines with AND/OR.

📊
4. Group By

Group rows by field(s), calculate aggregate PER GROUP. Keeps categories separate.

🔗
5. Join by Field

SQL-style inner/outer join between two query results on a common field. Merge different data sources.

🧮
6. Calculate Field

Add computed columns: binary (A+B, A/B), unary (-A, |A|), row number, cumulative sum, percentage.

📝
7. Rename by Regex

Rename fields/columns using regex pattern matching and replacement. Clean up auto-generated names.

🗂️
8. Organize Fields

Hide, reorder, and rename columns selectively. Drag to reorder. Only selected fields appear.

🔗
9. Concatenate Fields

Combine multiple fields into one with configurable separator. "Method" + "Path" → "GET /api/jobs".

🔄
10. Convert Field Type

Change field type: string→number, number→string, string→boolean, etc. Fix data type mismatches.

🔍
11. Extract Fields

Parse key=value pairs from a string field into separate columns. "env=prod,region=us" → two fields.

📈
12. Prepare Time Series

Convert tabular data to time-series format. Select Time column + Metric column. Enable graphing.

10.3 Transformation Chaining Patterns (4 Real Examples)

🔗 TRANSFORMATION PIPELINE PATTERNS
  PATTERN 1: Merge + Filter + Sort
  ────────────────────────────────
  Query A (Prometheus: CPU %) ─┐
                                ├── [MERGE] → [Filter: CPU>80%] → [Sort: CPU desc]
  Query B (Prometheus: Mem%) ──┘
  Result: "Top nodes by resource usage" — ONLY nodes that need attention

  PATTERN 2: Group By + Reduce
  ────────────────────────────
  Raw logs → [Group By: endpoint] → [Reduce: avg(latency), count(errors)]
  Result: Per-endpoint summary from thousands of raw log entries

  PATTERN 3: Join by Field + Organize
  ──────────────────────────────────
  Prometheus: {endpoint, error_rate} ─┐
                                       ├── [JOIN on "endpoint"] → [Organize: hide internal cols]
  PostgreSQL: {endpoint, revenue_impact}┘
  Result: "Endpoint | Error Rate | Revenue Impact" — Business+Tech unified!

  PATTERN 4: Calculate Field + Filter by Value
  ───────────────────────────────────────────
  Raw metrics → [Calculate: error_pct = errors/total * 100] → [Filter: error_pct > 5]
  Result: "Only endpoints with error rate above 5%"

  🎯 anihpj REAL EXAMPLE:
  Join Prometheus (technical metrics) + PostgreSQL (business metrics)
  on "endpoint" to create a unified "Business Impact of Errors" table

🧑‍🏫 Core Concept — Transformations as a Cooking Recipe

Think of transformations as cooking steps for your data. Your query brings raw ingredients from the store (data source). Transformations are your kitchen prep:

1. Merge = combining ingredients from TWO stores. You bought tomatoes from Prometheus Store and cheese from PostgreSQL Market. Merge puts them on the same cutting board so you can see everything together in one meal (panel).

2. Filter by Value = picking out the bad tomatoes. You have 50 tomatoes. Filter: "only keep ripe ones (redness > 80%)". Now you have 20 perfect tomatoes. Filter runs AFTER you've brought everything to the kitchen — you're not reducing what you bought, just what goes into the final dish.

3. Group By = sorting by recipe. You have 20 tomatoes. Group By "size" → 5 small, 10 medium, 5 large. Now you know exactly how many of each you have for different sauces. Group By keeps categories separate — unlike Reduce, which would blend everything into one soup.

4. Order MATTERS — follow the recipe! You can't season (Filter) before you've chopped (Group By) or combined (Merge). The pipeline runs left to right. Wrong order = wrong result. Exam trap: Filtering BEFORE Group By gives different results than Filtering AFTER. Think: "Do I want to discard tomatoes before or after sorting them by size?"

5. Transformations run in YOUR kitchen (browser), not the store (server). All ingredients come to you first — even the ones you'll discard later. If you need to reduce what you buy, use better queries (server-side WHERE). If you need to prepare what you have, use transformations (client-side pipeline).

📝 Chapter 10 — Practice Questions

Q1

When should you use a Transformation instead of modifying the query?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Use transformations when: (1) You need to combine data from MULTIPLE data sources (Prometheus + PostgreSQL), (2) The data source can't return data in the required shape, (3) You want non-destructive processing (original query unchanged), (4) You're combining multiple queries from the same source.

💡 EXPLANATION

Transformations are applied AFTER queries return. They don't reduce load on data sources — the full query still runs. Use query-level optimization for performance, transformations for data shaping and combining.

Q2

What does the Merge transformation do and when is it essential?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Merge combines results from MULTIPLE queries into a single table. It's essential when you have queries from different data sources (Prometheus + PostgreSQL) that need to appear in one visualization. Without Merge, each query would render separately.

💡 EXPLANATION

anihpj example: Query A = Prometheus (HTTP error rate per endpoint). Query B = PostgreSQL (business impact per endpoint — revenue affected). Merge them into one table showing "Endpoint | Error Rate | Revenue Impact" — a unified business+technical view.

Q3

What is the difference between Group By and Reduce transformations?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Group By: Groups by a field, aggregates per group (per-endpoint average). Reduce: Aggregates ALL rows into one result (global total). Group By = per-category. Reduce = overall.

💡 EXPLANATION

100 rows, 5 endpoints. Group By "endpoint" → 5 rows. Reduce (sum) → 1 row. Group By for breakdowns, Reduce for totals.

Q4

What does the Join by Field transformation do?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Join by Field performs SQL-style inner/outer joins between two query results based on a common field. Example: Join Prometheus data (endpoint, error_rate) with PostgreSQL data (endpoint, business_impact) on "endpoint" — creating a unified view.

💡 EXPLANATION

Join types: Inner (only matching rows), Outer (all rows from both). This is how you create dashboards that combine technical AND business data from different sources in one visualization.

Q6

What is the "Prepare time series" transformation?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Prepare time series converts non-time-series data (tabular) into time-series format by selecting a Time column and Metric column. Essential when your data source returns rows instead of series.

💡 EXPLANATION

Example: PostgreSQL returns (timestamp, endpoint, latency) as rows. Prepare time series: Time=timestamp, Metric=endpoint, Value=latency → Grafana can now graph it as time series.

Q7

How do you reorder columns in a Table panel using transformations?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Use Organize Fields — hide unwanted columns and reorder remaining ones. Drag fields in the UI or toggle visibility. Only selected fields appear in the final table.

💡 EXPLANATION

Combine with Rename by Regex for clean headers. Organize Fields is always applied AFTER data-shaping transformations (Group By, Merge, Join).

Q8

What is "Filter by value"? How do you use it?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Filter by value includes/excludes rows based on numeric conditions: >, <, =, !=, between, contains. Example: show only endpoints where error_rate > 5% — filter out healthy endpoints.

💡 EXPLANATION

Multiple conditions can be combined with AND/OR. Use regex for string filters. This runs AFTER queries and Group By — use it to highlight only what matters.

Q9

Can you apply multiple transformations in a pipeline?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Yes. Transformations are applied sequentially in a pipeline — output of one becomes input to the next. Order matters. Typical: Reduce (shrink)→ Organize Fields (reorder)→ Rename (clean names)→ Filter.

💡 EXPLANATION

Drag to reorder pipeline steps. Each step shows a preview. Common exam scenario: "After Group By, you want to rename columns and filter — what transformations do you use?" Answer: Rename by Regex + Filter by Value.

Q10

Where are transformations executed — browser or server?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Browser (client-side). Transformations run in the user's browser, processing data returned from Grafana server. This means: full data transferred, client CPU used for processing, no additional database load.

💡 EXPLANATION

Exam implication: If you need to filter large datasets, filter in the QUERY (server-side), not in Transformations (client-side). Transformations should complement queries, not replace WHERE clauses.

Chapter 11: Variables & Template Dashboards 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 11: Variables — One Dashboard, Infinite Flexibility

Variables are what make Grafana dashboards truly powerful. One dashboard can serve all environments, all services, all instances — just by changing a dropdown. Master all 7 variable types, chaining, and repeat panels.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~1.5 hours🎛️ Variables

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ 7 variable types: Query, Custom, Text Box, Constant, Data Source, Interval, Ad Hoc
  • ✅ Variable configuration: multi-value, include All, refresh, sort, regex filter
  • ✅ Chaining variables with parent-child dependencies
  • ✅ Repeat panels and rows for dynamic dashboards
  • ✅ anihpj: one dashboard for prod/staging/dev with $environment variable
Variables — Heavily Tested

The exam tests: (1) Variable types and when to use each, (2) Chaining syntax ($parent_var in child query), (3) Multi-value handling, (4) "Include All" option and its wildcard value. Expect 3-5 variable-specific questions.

11.1 What Are Variables?

Dynamic placeholders ($variable_name) used in queries, panel titles, and text panels. Benefits: ONE dashboard serves ALL environments/services/instances. Syntax: $var or ${var} (braces needed when adjacent to text).

11.2 Variable Types Deep Dive (7 Types — KNOW ALL FOR EXAM)

📊
a) Query

Values from data source query. Most common, most powerful. Example: label_values(metric, job) returns all job label values. query_result(up) returns all targets from Prometheus. Automatically picks up new hosts/services.

✏️
b) Custom

Manually defined comma-separated list. Example: prod, staging, dev. For fixed, known values that don't change. Simple and predictable.

⌨️
c) Text Box

Free-form user text input. Example: User types a specific pod name, IP address, or search term. No predefined values — completely flexible.

🔒
d) Constant

Hidden, hardcoded single value. Users CANNOT change it. For: provisioned dashboards (GitOps), hiding implementation details, consistent values across environments. Example: $datasource_name = "Prometheus-Production".

🔌
e) Data Source

Dropdown of available data sources by type. Filter: show only Prometheus/Loki/PostgreSQL sources. Switch entire dashboard between prod and staging data sources with one click.

⏱️
f) Interval

Time interval selection: 1m, 5m, 15m, 30m, 1h, 6h, 1d. Used as $__interval in PromQL queries for dynamic step resolution. Controls query granularity.

🔍
g) Ad Hoc Filters

Auto-generated key=value filters. User selects a field → Grafana queries distinct values → user selects value. Dynamic discovery. Example: filter by "status_code" = "500" on the fly.

11.3 Variable Configuration Options — Each Variable Has These

🏷️
Name & Label

Name: Used as $name in queries. Letters, numbers, underscores only. Label: Human-readable display name shown in the UI dropdown.

👁️
Hide Options

None: Full dropdown visible. Label: Only label shown, no dropdown (displays "$environment"). Variable: Completely hidden — for constant-like behavior.

☑️
Multi-Value

Allow selecting multiple values simultaneously. Needed for: "Show metrics for pods A, B, AND C at the same time."

🌐
Include All Option

Adds "All" that passes a wildcard (default: .*). Custom All value: prod|staging. Effectively removes that filter when "All" is selected.

🔄
Refresh

On Dashboard Load: Query runs once when dashboard opens. On Time Range Change: Re-queries when time range changes. For Query-type variables.

🔽
Sort & Regex

Sort: None, Alphabetical (asc/desc), Numerical (asc/desc), Case-insensitive. Regex filter: Filter query results — only values matching prod-.* appear.

11.4 Chaining Variables — Parent-Child Dependencies (EXAM TOPIC)

🔗 VARIABLE CHAINING — anihpj Complete Walkthrough
  $datasource ──────▶ $environment ──────▶ $namespace ────▶ $pod
  (Data Source         (Custom:            (Query:          (Query:
   type selector)      prod/staging/dev)   label_values)    label_values)

  ┌──────────┐       ┌──────────┐       ┌──────────┐    ┌───────┐
  │Prometheus│       │  ▾ prod  │       │▾ anihpj  │    │▾pod-1 │
  │• Prod    │       │  staging │       │▾ default │    │ pod-2 │
  │• Staging │       │  dev     │       │  kube-   │    │ pod-3 │
  └──────────┘       └──────────┘       │  system  │    └───────┘
                                        └──────────┘

  HOW IT WORKS (Step by Step):
  1️⃣ User selects $datasource = "Prometheus-Prod"
     → $environment values stay the same (Custom type, no dependency)
  2️⃣ User selects $environment = "prod"
     → $namespace re-queries: label_values(kube_namespace_created{}, namespace)
       Shows ALL namespaces in production cluster
  3️⃣ User selects $namespace = "anihpj"
     → $pod re-queries: label_values(kube_pod_info{namespace="$namespace"}, pod)
       Shows only pods in the anihpj namespace
  4️⃣ All panels re-query with final values:
     {namespace="anihpj", pod=~"$pod"} ← All 4 variables resolved!

  🎯 EXAM KEY POINT: Each child variable's query MUST reference its
  parent using $variable_name syntax. If you forget $ in the child
  query, the chain breaks. No special configuration needed — Grafana
  auto-detects the dependency from the $parent reference.

11.5 Repeat Panels/Rows with Variables

One panel definition automatically repeats for EACH value of a variable. Panel title: $pod — CPU Usage becomes "pod-abc123 — CPU Usage", "pod-def456 — CPU Usage" for each pod. Repeat direction: Horizontal or Vertical. Max per row: Controls layout. Repeat row: An entire row with all its panels repeats per variable value. For anihpj: repeat CPU/Memory/Disk panels for each pod in the deployment — new pods automatically get their own panel set!

11.6 anihpj Variable-Driven Dashboard — Full Example

Variable 1 — $datasource: Data Source type. Values: Prometheus-Production, Prometheus-Staging. Lets SRE switch entire dashboard between environments.
Variable 2 — $environment: Custom type. Values: prod, staging, dev. Used for label filtering: {env="$environment"}.
Variable 3 — $namespace: Query type from Kubernetes metrics. Depends on $datasource. Shows available namespaces.
Variable 4 — $service: Query type. Depends on $namespace. Shows services in selected namespace.
All panels use: {namespace="$namespace", job="$service"} — ONE dashboard serves ALL environments and ALL services. No more copying dashboards per environment!

🎯 Chapter 11 Exam Focus — Top 7 Things to Memorize
  • ✅ 7 variable types: Query (from DS), Custom (manual list), Text Box (free input), Constant (hidden), Data Source (type selector), Interval (time), Ad Hoc (key=value filters)
  • ✅ Syntax: $variable or ${variable}. Multi-value: =~ regex matching
  • ✅ Chaining: Parent variable's query is used by child variable. $datasource → $namespace → $pod
  • ✅ Repeat panels: One panel definition → repeated for each variable value. Use $var in panel title
  • ✅ Include All option: Passes wildcard .* or custom value when "All" is selected
  • ✅ Refresh timing: On Dashboard Load vs On Time Range Change — choose based on data volatility
  • ✅ Variable in queries: {job=~"$service"} — regex match for multi-value variables

📝 Chapter 11 — Practice Questions

Q1

Which variable type would you use to let users switch between production and staging data sources?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Data Source variable type — it creates a dropdown of available data sources filtered by type (e.g., only Prometheus data sources). Users select "Prometheus-Production" or "Prometheus-Staging" and all panels re-query the selected source.

💡 EXPLANATION

Data Source variable is powerful: one dashboard serves all environments. Configure: Type = Data Source, Type filter = Prometheus. Panels use $datasource as their data source selector. When user switches, all panels re-query automatically.

Q2

How do you create a chained variable where $service depends on $namespace?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Create $namespace first (Query type). Then create $service as a Query type with: label_values(kube_service_info{namespace="$namespace"}, service). The $namespace reference in the query creates the parent-child dependency. When $namespace changes, $service re-queries.

💡 EXPLANATION

Chaining requires: (1) Parent variable defined FIRST, (2) Child variable's query REFERENCES the parent with $parent_name, (3) Child's "Refresh" set to "On Dashboard Load" or "On Time Range Change". The dependency is automatic — Grafana detects the $parent reference.

Q3

What happens when you enable "Include All option" on a variable?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

An "All" option is added to the variable dropdown. When selected, the variable's value becomes a configurable wildcard (default: .* for regex or $__all). This makes queries match ALL possible values — effectively disabling the filter for that variable.

💡 EXPLANATION

Example: $environment with values "prod, staging, dev" and "Include All" enabled. When "All" is selected: {env=~".*"} matches ALL environments. The "Custom all value" field lets you change the wildcard: {env=~"prod|staging"} would only match prod+staging.

Q4

What is a Constant variable used for?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

A Constant variable has a single, hidden, hardcoded value. Users CANNOT change it. Used for: (1) Provisioned dashboards where a value should be fixed (e.g., datasource name), (2) Hiding implementation details from users, (3) Values that should be consistent across environments.

💡 EXPLANATION

anihpj example: $datacenter = "us-east-1" as a Constant. All panels reference it, but users can't accidentally change it. If you move to a new datacenter, update the provisioning file — all dashboards update automatically.

Q5

How do you repeat a panel for each value of a variable?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Panel settings → Repeat options: select variable + direction (horizontal/vertical). Title includes $variable_name. One panel definition → auto-scales to ALL values. New value = new panel appears automatically.

💡 EXPLANATION

Repeat by $pod: 5 pods → 5 panels. Pod added → 6th panel appears. Max per row controls layout. Combine with "Include All" for flexible views.

Q6

What is the difference between Custom and Query variable types?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Custom: Fixed, manually-entered values (prod, staging, dev). Query: Dynamic values from a data source query. Custom = static. Query = dynamic (automatically picks up new hosts/services).

💡 EXPLANATION

Use Custom for stable, known values (environments, regions). Use Query when values change (pod names, hostnames). Query variables auto-update — new instances appear in the dropdown automatically.

Q7

How do you hide a variable from the dashboard UI?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

In variable settings, set Hide to: Label (only name shown, no dropdown) or Variable (completely hidden). Use for: fixed values users shouldn't change, internal references, Constant-like behavior from Query variables.

💡 EXPLANATION

Hide options: None (full dropdown visible), Label (shows "$environment" text but no dropdown), Variable (completely invisible). Hide=Variable is useful when the value is set by URL parameter or another mechanism.

Q8

What does "Include All option" do? What value does "All" pass?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Adds an "All" option that passes a wildcard (default: .*). When selected, the variable matches ALL values — effectively removing that filter. Custom All value can be configured (e.g., prod|staging).

💡 EXPLANATION

Query behavior: $env="All" → {env=~".*"} matches everything. Custom All value: {env=~"prod|staging"} matches only those two. The "Custom all value" field overrides the default wildcard.

Q9

How do Ad Hoc Filters differ from Query variables?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Ad Hoc Filters: Auto-generated key=value filters. User selects a field → Grafana queries distinct values → user selects values. Query variables: Pre-defined query that runs on load. Ad Hoc = dynamic discovery. Query = pre-defined.

💡 EXPLANATION

Ad Hoc filters are powerful for exploration: user picks "status_code" field → sees values 200, 404, 500 → selects 500 → all panels filter to 500 errors only. No pre-configuration needed — Grafana discovers fields dynamically.

Q10

How do you use a variable in a panel title or text panel?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Use $variable_name or ${variable_name} in panel titles and text panels. Panel title: "$environment — HTTP Overview" becomes "Production — HTTP Overview". Text panel: use Markdown with $var for dynamic text.

💡 EXPLANATION

Braces ${var} are needed when variable is adjacent to text: ${environment}Metrics → "ProductionMetrics". Without braces: $environmentMetrics would look for a variable named "environmentMetrics".

Chapter 12: Grafana Alerting Fundamentals 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 12: Alerting — Your System's Early Warning System

Grafana Alerting is a unified alerting engine that works with ALL data sources — Prometheus, Loki, PostgreSQL, and more. Understand how it differs from Prometheus Alertmanager, master the alert rule anatomy, alert states and lifecycle, and the three alert rule types. This chapter is critical — alerting is 15% of the exam.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~2 hours🔔 Alerting🔥 15% of Exam

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Grafana Alerting architecture — Scheduler → Evaluator → State Manager → Notifier
  • ✅ How Grafana Alerting differs from Prometheus Alertmanager
  • ✅ Alert rule anatomy: every field explained with examples
  • ✅ Alert states and lifecycle: Normal → Pending → Firing → Resolved (with state machine diagram)
  • ✅ Three alert rule types: Grafana-managed, Data source-managed, Recording rules
  • ✅ Key terminology: Alert Instance, Contact Point, Notification Policy, Silence, Evaluation Group
Alerting — 15% of Exam Questions

Know: (1) "Evaluate For" (pending period) prevents alert flapping, (2) Alert states: Normal→Pending→Firing→Resolved, (3) Grafana-managed vs Data source-managed rules, (4) NoData and Error alert states are critical, (5) Multi-dimensional alerting: one rule → many alert instances via labels. The alert lifecycle state machine diagram below is a MUST-MEMORIZE for the exam.

12.1 The Alerting Landscape — How Grafana Alerting Works

🔔 GRAFANA ALERTING ARCHITECTURE — Complete Data Flow
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    GRAFANA ALERTING ENGINE (Unified)                       │
  │                                                                          │
  │  ┌────────────────┐    ┌────────────────┐    ┌────────────────┐          │
  │  │ 📊 SCHEDULER   │───▶│ 🧠 EVALUATOR   │───▶│ 📋 STATE       │          │
  │  │ Runs on cadence│    │ Checks if      │    │    MANAGER     │          │
  │  │ (e.g., every   │    │ condition is   │    │ Tracks Normal  │          │
  │  │  1m, 5m, etc.) │    │ met (query →   │    │ →Pending→Firing│          │
  │  │                │    │  reduce→math)  │    │ →Resolved      │          │
  │  └────────────────┘    └───────┬────────┘    └───────┬────────┘          │
  │                                │                     │                   │
  │                       ┌────────▼────────┐   ┌───────▼────────┐           │
  │                       │ CONDITION MET?  │   │ STATE CHANGE?  │           │
  │                       │ Yes → Pending   │   │ Firing→Resolved│           │
  │                       │ Yes+For→Firing  │   │ →Notify        │           │
  │                       └─────────────────┘   └───────┬────────┘           │
  │                                                     │                   │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────▼────────────────┐  │
  │  │                      📨 NOTIFICATION ENGINE                        │  │
  │  │  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐             │  │
  │  │  │ NOTIFICATION │  │ CONTACT      │  │ NOTIFICATION │             │  │
  │  │  │ TEMPLATES    │  │ POINTS       │  │ POLICIES     │             │  │
  │  │  │ (Format msg) │  │ (Where to    │  │ (Route: match │             │  │
  │  │  │              │  │  send)       │  │  labels→CP)   │             │  │
  │  │  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘             │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
📖 Grafana Alerting vs Prometheus Alertmanager

Grafana Alerting: Unified, works with ANY data source (Prometheus, Loki, PostgreSQL, etc.). Evaluated by Grafana's own engine. Best for multi-source alerts.
Prometheus Alertmanager: Prometheus-only. Evaluated by Prometheus server. Handles deduplication, grouping, inhibition, and routing of Prometheus alerts only. Can be used alongside Grafana Alerting.

📋
Alert Rule

"IF error rate > 5% for 5 minutes THEN fire." Defines the condition, evaluation schedule, and notification routing.

🔢
Alert Instance

One specific firing of a rule. One rule can create MULTIPLE instances — one per unique label combination (multi-dimensional alerting).

📧
Contact Point

WHERE the notification goes: Email, Slack, PagerDuty, Webhook, Kafka, Telegram, Discord.

🌲
Notification Policy

ROUTING rules: match labels → send to contact point. Tree structure, first match wins.

🔕
Silence

Temporarily suppress alerts (maintenance window, known outage). Auto-expires.

📝
Evaluation Group

Rules evaluated together on the same schedule. Organize by frequency: fast (1m), slow (5m), daily.

12.2 Alert Rule Anatomy — Every Field Explained

🧬 COMPLETE ALERT RULE ANATOMY — anihpj Example
  Rule Name: "anihpj High Error Rate"
  Folder:    /anihpj/Alerts
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ QUERIES & EXPRESSIONS:                                              │
  │   Query A: sum(rate(django_http_requests_total{status=~"5.."}[5m])) │
  │   Query B: sum(rate(django_http_requests_total[5m]))                │
  │   Expression C (Math): $A / $B                                      │
  │                                                                      │
  │ Condition: C > 0.05    (error rate > 5%)                            │
  │                                                                      │
  │ ⏱️ Evaluate Every: 1 minute       (how often to check)               │
  │ ⏱️ Evaluate For:   2 minutes      (how long before firing!)          │
  │                                                                      │
  │ 🏷️ LABELS (for routing):                                            │
  │   severity=critical, team=sre, service=anihpj, env=production       │
  │                                                                      │
  │ 📝 ANNOTATIONS (for context):                                        │
  │   summary="anihpj error rate at {{ humanize $values.C.Value }}"     │
  │   description="5xx error rate exceeded 5% threshold"                │
  │   runbook_url="https://wiki.anihpj.com/runbooks/high-error-rate"    │
  │                                                                      │
  │ ⚠️ NoData State: Alerting   (if query returns nothing — ALERT!)     │
  │ ❌ Error State:  Alerting   (if query fails — ALERT!)               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
⚠️ "Evaluate For" — THE Most Important Alert Setting

Evaluate For: 2m means the condition must be true for 2 consecutive minutes before firing. This prevents alert flapping (on/off/on/off) from transient spikes. Example: CPU spikes to 95% for 30 seconds, then drops. With "Evaluate For: 5m" → NO alert fires (correct!). Without it → false alarm wakes the on-call engineer at 3 AM. For production: use 5-15 min for most alerts.

🚫 CRITICAL: NoData & Error States

NoData: What if your query returns nothing? Metrics stopped flowing? Service crashed? Set NoData to "Alerting" — you're BLIND, that IS an alert!
Error: What if the query itself fails? Data source down? Set Error to "Alerting" — if you can't evaluate, you can't monitor. Both NoData and Error are frequently overlooked but CRITICAL for production alerting.

12.3 Alert States & Lifecycle — MUST KNOW FOR EXAM

🔔 COMPLETE ALERT LIFECYCLE STATE MACHINE
                         ┌──────────┐
            ┌───────────▶│  NORMAL  │◀──────────┐
            │            │ (Healthy)│           │
            │            └────┬─────┘           │
            │                 │                  │
            │     Condition   │  Condition       │  Condition
            │     becomes     │  stays false     │  becomes false
            │     TRUE        │  (everything OK) │  (self-healed!)
            │                 │                  │
            │                 ▼                  │
            │            ┌──────────┐           │
            │            │ PENDING  │           │
            │            │(Wait for │           │
            │    Condition│ Evaluate │           │
            │    becomes  │  For)    │           │
            │    FALSE    └────┬─────┘           │
            │    (quick         │                  │
            │     spike)   Evaluate For │  Evaluate For
            │            elapsed +     │  elapsed +
            │            still TRUE    │  still TRUE
            │                 ▼                  │
            │            ┌──────────┐           │
            └────────────│ FIRING   │───────────┘
                         │(🔔 Alert!│
                         │ Notify)  │
                         └────┬─────┘
                              │
                              │  Notification Timing:
                              │  • Group Wait: 30s (batch alerts)
                              │  • Group Interval: 5m (between batches)
                              │  • Repeat Interval: 4h (remind if still on)
                              ▼
                    ┌──────────────────┐
                    │ 📧 Email          │
                    │ 💬 Slack           │
                    │ 📟 PagerDuty       │
                    │ 🔗 Webhook         │
                    └──────────────────┘

  🎯 KEY INSIGHT: "Evaluate For" is your shield against alert fatigue!
  Without it, every transient spike triggers a false alert.
  Example: CPU spikes to 95% for 10s → Normal (no alert)
           CPU stays at 95% for 6 min → Firing (real problem!)
✅
NORMALCondition false
→
⏳
PENDINGWaiting "For" duration
→
🔔
FIRINGNotifications sent
→
✅
RESOLVEDCondition cleared

12.4 Alert Rule Types — Three Approaches

FeatureGrafana-ManagedData Source-ManagedRecording Rules
Who evaluates?Grafana Alerting EngineData source (e.g., Prometheus)Data source (Prometheus)
Multi-source?✅ Yes (any DS)❌ Single source only❌ Prometheus only
HA if Grafana down?❌ Alerts stop✅ ContinuesN/A (not alerting)
PurposeAlertingAlertingPerformance optimization
Sends notifications?✅ Yes✅ Via Alertmanager❌ No (precomputes queries)
Best forMulti-source alerts, teams without AlertmanagerExisting Prometheus setupsSpeeding up dashboards
🔗 Grafana Alerting with External Alertmanagers (Exam-Relevant)

The official Grafana docs confirm: Grafana Alerting includes a built-in Alertmanager (extended Prometheus Alertmanager). However, you can ALSO configure Grafana to send alerts to external Alertmanagers — Prometheus Alertmanager, Cortex Alertmanager, or Mimir Alertmanager. This is useful when: (1) You already have Alertmanager running with existing routing/config, (2) You want HA — if Grafana goes down, the external Alertmanager still handles dedup/grouping, (3) You're migrating gradually from Prometheus Alertmanager to Grafana Alerting. Configuration: grafana.ini → [alerting] → external_alertmanagers. Exam tip: If a question mentions "using Grafana with an existing Alertmanager setup," the answer is external Alertmanager integration — not replacing it.

📅 A DAY IN THE LIFE OF AN ALERT — Complete Journey
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  T+0:00 — CONDITION TRIGGERED                                                │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
  │  │ Scheduler runs evaluation. Query A returns error_rate=7.2%.              ││
  │  │ Expression C: $A / $B = 0.072. Condition: C > 0.05 → TRUE!               ││
  │  │ Alert enters PENDING state. Clock starts on "Evaluate For: 2m".          ││
  │  │ Grafana UI shows: ⏳ anihpj High Error Rate [PENDING]                     ││
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
  │                                                                              │
  │  T+2:00 — EVALUATE FOR ELAPSED                                               │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
  │  │ Condition still TRUE. Alert transitions: PENDING → FIRING.                ││
  │  │ State Manager records: instance_1 = FIRING, severity=critical.            ││
  │  │ Notifier invoked with: {severity:critical, team:sre, service:anihpj}     ││
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
  │                                                                              │
  │  T+2:00 — GROUP WAIT (30s)                                                   │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
  │  │ Notifier waits 30s. During this time:                                     ││
  │  │ • Alert instance_2 (P95 latency high) also fires                          ││
  │  │ • Alert instance_3 (disk space warning) also fires                        ││
  │  │ All 3 alerts are GROUPED into one notification batch.                     ││
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
  │                                                                              │
  │  T+2:30 — NOTIFICATION SENT                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
  │  │ Notification Policy routing tree traversal:                               ││
  │  │ 1. Check: {severity=critical}? → YES → Route to PagerDuty + Slack        ││
  │  │ 2. Check: {team=sre, service=anihpj}? → YES → Route to SRE Slack channel ││
  │  │                                                                          ││
  │  │ 📟 PagerDuty: "CRITICAL: anihpj error rate 7.2%"                         ││
  │  │ 💬 Slack #alerts-sre: Rich message with runbook link, graph screenshot    ││
  │  │ Dashboard annotation created: ▼ red marker "Alert: anihpj High Error Rate"││
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
  │                                                                              │
  │  T+7:30 — GROUP INTERVAL ELAPSED (5m later)                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
  │  │ Alert still FIRING. Group Interval (5m) has passed since last batch.      ││
  │  │ No new notification sent (unless new alerts joined the group).            ││
  │  │ This prevents flooding — one notification every 5 min at most.            ││
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
  │                                                                              │
  │  T+15:00 — CONDITION RESOLVES                                                │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
  │  │ Error rate drops to 3.1%. Condition: C > 0.05 → FALSE.                    ││
  │  │ Alert transitions: FIRING → RESOLVED.                                     ││
  │  │ Resolution notification sent: "anihpj error rate back to 3.1%"            ││
  │  │ Dashboard annotation: ✅ green marker "Alert: anihpj High Error Rate [OK]"││
  │  │ PagerDuty incident auto-resolved.                                         ││
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
  │                                                                              │
  │  T+4:15:00 — REPEAT INTERVAL TRIGGER (if still firing after 4h)              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
  │  │ If alert was STILL firing after 4 hours:                                  ││
  │  │ "REMINDER: anihpj error rate has been above 5% for 4 hours!"             ││
  │  │ This prevents "forgotten alerts" — periodic reminders.                    ││
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
⚠️ TOP 8 ALERTING MISTAKES — These WILL Appear on the Exam
  • MISTAKE 1: No "Evaluate For" duration. Every transient spike triggers a false alert. Always set at least 2-5 minutes.
  • MISTAKE 2: No NoData handling. If metrics stop, no other alert fires — you're blind and don't know it. Set NoData=Alerting.
  • MISTAKE 3: Alerting on causes, not symptoms. "CPU 80%" vs "P95 latency 500ms". Users experience symptoms. Causes may be harmless.
  • MISTAKE 4: Missing runbook_url in annotations. On-call engineer receives alert at 3 AM with no idea what to do. Always include a runbook.
  • MISTAKE 5: Wrong severity classification. Everything is "critical" → everything gets ignored. Use critical/warning/info tiers.
  • MISTAKE 6: No grouping in notification policies. 50 pods crash → 50 separate PagerDuty calls. Use Group Wait to batch.
  • MISTAKE 7: Disabling alert rules instead of using silences. You WILL forget to re-enable. Silences auto-expire.
  • MISTAKE 8: Using rate() on gauges instead of counters. rate() is for counters only. Gauges use raw values or avg_over_time().
🎯 Chapter 12 Exam Focus — Top 7 Things to Memorize
  • ✅ 4 alert states in order: Normal → Pending → Firing → Resolved
  • ✅ Evaluate For = anti-flap shield: How long condition must persist before firing. Prevents false alarms.
  • ✅ Labels route, Annotations describe: Labels → notification policies. Annotations → human-readable context.
  • ✅ Multi-dimensional alerting: One rule creates many instances — one per unique label combination
  • ✅ NoData = Alerting for critical services: Missing metrics = you're blind = emergency
  • ✅ Grafana-managed vs Data Source-managed: DS-managed survives Grafana being down (evaluated by Prometheus)
  • ✅ 3 timing controls: Group Wait (30s batch), Group Interval (5m between batches), Repeat Interval (4h remind)

📝 Chapter 12 — Practice Questions

Q1

What is the purpose of the "Evaluate For" duration in an alert rule?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

"Evaluate For" defines how long the condition must be TRUE before firing. If condition is met at T=0 and "Evaluate For"=5m, Grafana waits until T+5m before sending a notification. This prevents false alarms from transient spikes.

💡 EXPLANATION
  Without "For": CPU 95% for 10s → FALSE ALARM 🔔
  With "For:5m": CPU 95% for 10s → Stays Pending → CPU drops → Normal
                  CPU 95% for 6 min → Pending→Firing → REAL ALERT 🔔
Q2

What is the difference between Labels and Annotations in an alert rule?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Labels: Identify and ROUTE alerts (used in notification policies, grouping). Annotations: Provide human-readable CONTEXT (summary, description, runbook URL). Labels = machine routing. Annotations = human understanding.

💡 EXPLANATION

Route by labels: {severity="critical", team="sre"} → PagerDuty. {severity="warning", team="dev"} → Slack #alerts-dev. Annotations appear in alert messages: "Error rate is {{ $values.C.Value }}% on /api/jobs/. See runbook: https://wiki/anihpj/alerts/error-rate".

Q3

What happens when an alert enters the "NoData" state? Why is this critical?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

NoData means the alert query returned NO data (no time series matched). This usually means: monitoring agent stopped, service crashed, metric name changed, or Prometheus is down. Configure NoData to "Alerting" — it's often MORE urgent than a threshold breach because you're BLIND.

💡 EXPLANATION

CRITICAL: Without NoData alerting, if anihpj crashes, metrics stop flowing. Your error rate alert doesn't fire (no data = no condition met). But your service IS down! Always set NoData → Alerting for critical services. This is the "dead man's switch" of monitoring.

Q4

What is multi-dimensional alerting? How does one rule create many alert instances?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

One alert rule creates multiple alert instances, one per unique label combination. Example: rate(errors[5m]) by (endpoint) > 5 — if /api/jobs/ and /api/users/ both exceed 5%, TWO separate alerts fire. No need to create separate rules per endpoint!

💡 EXPLANATION
  Rule: sum by (endpoint) (rate(errors[5m])) > 5

  /api/jobs/  → 3.2% → Normal (no alert)
  /api/users/ → 7.1% → FIRING 🔔
  /api/admin/ → 12.4% → FIRING 🔔

  One rule → 2 alert instances (users + admin)
Q5

Grafana-managed vs Data source-managed alerts — what's the key trade-off?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Grafana-managed: Works with ANY data source (multi-source), but stops if Grafana is down. Data source-managed: Evaluated by the data source (e.g., Prometheus) — continues even if Grafana is down, but only works with that one data source. Trade-off: flexibility vs. high availability of alert evaluation.

💡 EXPLANATION

Production recommendation: Use Data source-managed for critical Prometheus alerts (HA even if Grafana is down). Use Grafana-managed for multi-source alerts (e.g., "if error rate > 5% AND database has zero connections"). Best practice: use both types where appropriate.

Q6

Describe the complete alert lifecycle: what states does an alert go through?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Normal (condition false) → Pending (condition true, "Evaluate For" not yet elapsed) → Firing (condition true AND "Evaluate For" elapsed → notification sent) → Resolved (condition becomes false → resolution notification sent) → Normal. The cycle repeats if the condition triggers again.

💡 EXPLANATION

Key nuance: "Pending" is the buffer state. If the condition becomes false during Pending, the alert returns to Normal — NO notification was ever sent. Only when "Evaluate For" elapses while still true does it transition to Firing. This prevents alert storms from transient issues.

Q7

What is the difference between an Alert Rule and an Alert Instance?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Alert Rule: The definition/template — "IF error rate > 5% for 5 minutes THEN fire." Alert Instance: One specific occurrence — "error rate for /api/jobs is 7.2%". One rule can create MANY instances (multi-dimensional alerting). Each instance has its own state and can fire independently.

💡 EXPLANATION

Think of it like: Rule = the blueprint. Instances = each house built from that blueprint. In Grafana, you see alert instances in the Alerting → Alert Rules view, where each instance shows its own state (Normal/Pending/Firing) and labels.

Q8

How should you configure "NoData" handling in production alerts?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Always set NoData to "Alerting" for critical services. If metrics stop flowing, that IS an emergency — your monitoring is blind. Configure a separate NoData-specific contact point with a message like: "No metrics received from anihpj for 10 minutes. Check if service is running and Prometheus is scraping."

💡 EXPLANATION

NoData Options: OK (ignore — dangerous!), NoData (separate NoData-specific handling), Alerting (treat as alert — recommended for critical), Keep Last State (if it was firing, stays firing). Best practice: NoData=Alerting for critical, NoData=OK for non-critical.

Q9

What are Evaluation Groups in Grafana Alerting?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Evaluation Groups organize alert rules that share the same evaluation schedule. Rules in the same group are evaluated together. Use separate groups for different evaluation frequencies: "fast-checks" (every 1m for critical), "standard" (every 5m), "daily-slos" (every 24h). Groups also help with permissions — you can assign folder-level access to groups.

💡 EXPLANATION

Evaluation Groups live inside Folders. Structure: Folder > Evaluation Group > Alert Rules. Example: Folder "anihpj-Alerts" > Group "critical-1m" (CPU, memory, error rate) and Group "warning-5m" (disk space, certificate expiry). This keeps your alerting organized and maintainable.

Q10

Recording rules vs Alerting rules — what's the difference and when do you use each?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Alerting rules: Evaluate conditions → trigger notifications. Used for monitoring and alerting. Recording rules: Pre-compute expensive PromQL expressions → create new metrics. Used for PERFORMANCE optimization. Recording rules save computation — dashboards and alert rules can reference the pre-computed metric instead of re-computing.

💡 EXPLANATION

Recording rules are defined in Prometheus config (not Grafana). Example: 20 dashboards and 5 alerts all query rate(errors[5m]) by (endpoint). That's 25 identical computations. A recording rule pre-computes job:errors:rate5m once → all 25 consumers read the cached result. This drastically improves dashboard load times.

🧠 Alerting Self-Check — Can You Answer These?
  • ✅ What are the 4 alert states in order? (Normal → Pending → Firing → Resolved)
  • ✅ What does "Evaluate For" protect against? (Transient spikes / alert flapping)
  • ✅ Labels vs Annotations: which one routes alerts? (Labels route, Annotations describe)
  • ✅ What's the difference between Contact Point and Notification Policy? (WHERE vs WHICH goes WHERE)
  • ✅ Why set NoData to "Alerting" for critical services? (Dead monitoring = you're blind to problems)
  • ✅ What's multi-dimensional alerting? (One rule → multiple instances per label combination)
  • ✅ Grafana-managed vs Data Source-managed: which survives Grafana being down? (Data Source-managed — evaluated by Prometheus)
  • ✅ What are the Group Wait / Group Interval / Repeat Interval defaults? (30s / 5m / 4h)

Alerting is 15% of the exam (~9 questions). If you can answer all 8 checkpoints without hesitation, you're ready. If not, re-read Sections 12.1-12.4 and the Gotchas above.

Chapter 13: Creating Effective Alert Rules 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 13: Building Production Alert Rules for anihpj

Learn the art and science of alert rule design. Master the RED method (Rate, Errors, Duration), USE method (Utilization, Saturation, Errors), and Google's Four Golden Signals. Build 6 real, production-grade alert rules for the anihpj Django platform — from error rate detection to dead monitoring checks.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~2 hours🚨 Alerts🎯 Design

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Alert on SYMPTOMS, not causes — the golden rule of alerting
  • ✅ RED Method (Rate, Errors, Duration) — for every service endpoint
  • ✅ USE Method (Utilization, Saturation, Errors) — for infrastructure
  • ✅ Google's Four Golden Signals: Latency, Traffic, Errors, Saturation
  • ✅ 6 real anihpj alert rules with complete PromQL and configuration
  • ✅ Standard labels, template variables, and humanize functions
Alert Design Principles on the Exam

Expect scenario-based questions: "An alert fires every time there's a brief CPU spike. What should you change?" → Increase "Evaluate For" duration. "You need to alert on slow endpoints. Which method?" → RED Method (Duration = P95 latency). Know the difference between USE (infrastructure) and RED (services).

13.1 Alert Rule Design Principles — The Art of Alerting

🎯
RED Method (Services)

Rate — requests per second. Errors — failed requests %. Duration — P95/P99 latency. Every service endpoint should have all three. Used for: HTTP APIs, gRPC services, microservices.

⚙️
USE Method (Resources)

Utilization — % of resource used (CPU, memory, disk). Saturation — queue depth, runnable threads. Errors — hardware errors, packet drops. Used for: servers, disks, networks, K8s nodes.

🌟
Four Golden Signals (Google SRE)

Latency — time to serve a request. Traffic — demand on your system. Errors — rate of failed requests. Saturation — how "full" your service is. Used for: user-facing systems.

🔕
Signal-to-Noise Ratio

Alert on SYMPTOMS users experience (slow page loads, errors), not CAUSES (high CPU, memory usage). Every alert MUST have an actionable runbook. If there's nothing a human can do, don't alert. This prevents alert fatigue.

⚠️ The Golden Rule: Alert on Symptoms, Not Causes

Good alert (symptom): "P95 latency for /api/jobs/ > 500ms for 5 minutes" — users are experiencing slowness. Bad alert (cause): "CPU > 80%" — might be fine if CPU is being used efficiently. A symptom alert leads you to investigate causes. A cause alert may wake you up for nothing. Users care about symptoms.
