🧮 Classic Condition vs Expressions — Two Ways to Define Alert Conditions

The official Grafana docs describe two approaches for building alert rule conditions:

AspectClassic ConditionExpressions (Recommended)
ComplexitySimple: single query + thresholdAdvanced: multi-query, math, reduce, resample
QueriesOne query onlyMultiple queries (A, B, C...)
Condition LogicBuilt-in: avg(), min(), max(), sum() of queryExpression pipeline: Reduce → Math → Resample → Threshold
ExampleWHEN avg() OF query(A) IS ABOVE 100$C = $A / $B; WHEN $C > 0.05 (error rate as percentage)
Multi-source?❌ Single data source✅ Queries can come from different data sources
TransformationsLimited (only avg/min/max/sum)Full pipeline: Reduce (aggregate), Math (combine queries), Resample (normalize time)
StatusLegacy — still supportedCurrent — recommended for all new rules

Expression Pipeline Explained: Reduce — aggregates time series (e.g., avg of all pod instances); Math — combines queries (e.g., $A / $B for error rate); Resample — aligns different query intervals; Threshold — the actual alert condition (e.g., > 0.05). Exam tip: If you see a question about "creating an alert that compares error count to total requests," you need EXPRESSIONS (Math expression), not Classic Condition.

13.2 Building Alert Rules for anihpj — 6 Real Production Alerts

a) High Error Rate Alert (RED: Errors)

PromQL — Alert Condition
# Query A: 5xx error rate
sum(rate(django_http_requests_total{status=~"5.."}[5m]))

# Query B: Total request rate
sum(rate(django_http_requests_total[5m]))

# Expression C (Math): $A / $B
# Condition: C > 0.05  (error rate > 5%)
# Evaluate Every: 1m | Evaluate For: 2m

# Labels: severity=critical, team=sre, service=anihpj
# Summary: "anihpj error rate {{ humanize $values.C.Value }}"
# Runbook: 1. Check Loki logs 2. Check DB connectivity 3. Check recent deploys

b) P95 Latency Spike (RED: Duration / Four Golden Signals: Latency)

# Query A: P95 request duration
histogram_quantile(0.95,
  sum(rate(django_http_request_duration_seconds_bucket[5m])) by (le))

# Condition: A > 2  (P95 > 2 seconds)
# Evaluate Every: 5m | Evaluate For: 5m
# Summary: "P95 latency {{ $values.A.Value }}s for anihpj"

c) Pod CrashLoopBackOff (K8s: USE — Errors)

# Query A: Pod restart rate (anomaly detection)
rate(kube_pod_container_status_restarts_total{namespace="anihpj"}[10m]) * 600

# Condition: A > 3  (more than 3 restarts in 10 min window)
# Labels: severity=critical, team=sre, component=kubernetes
# Summary: "Pod {{ $labels.pod }} is crash-looping in anihpj namespace"

d) No Data / Dead Monitoring (CRITICAL — If metrics stop, no other alerts fire!)

# Query A: Check if anihpj target is up
up{job="anihpj"} == 0
# OR: absent(up{job="anihpj"}) == 1

# Condition: A == 1  (target is down or absent)
# Evaluate Every: 1m | Evaluate For: 2m
# Labels: severity=critical, team=sre
# Summary: "anihpj monitoring is DEAD — no metrics received!"
# WHY THIS MATTERS: If anihpj crashes, all other alerts go silent.
# This is your "dead man's switch" — alert when monitoring itself fails.

e) Certificate Expiry (Infrastructure Health)

# Query A: Days until TLS certificate expires
grafana_tls_cert_expiry_days < 30

# Condition: A == 1  (less than 30 days)
# Evaluate Every: 24h | Evaluate For: 0m (instant)
# Summary: "Grafana TLS cert expires in {{ $values.A.Value }} days"

f) Disk Space Running Out (USE — Saturation)

# Query A: Disk free percentage
(node_filesystem_avail_bytes{fstype!="tmpfs"} /
 node_filesystem_size_bytes{fstype!="tmpfs"}) < 0.1

# Condition: A == 1  (less than 10% free)
# Evaluate Every: 15m | Evaluate For: 15m
# Summary: "Disk usage > 90% on {{ $labels.instance }} ({{ $labels.mountpoint }})"
# Labels: severity=warning, team=sre

13.3 Labels & Annotations Best Practices

Label/AnnotationPurposeExample
severityRouting: critical→PagerDuty, warning→Slack, info→Emailcritical, warning, info
teamOwning team for routing and escalationsre, backend, frontend, platform
serviceAffected service nameanihpj-django, anihpj-postgres
environmentProd vs staging (only prod pages on-call)production, staging, development
summary (annotation)Short alert description (required)"Error rate {{ humanize $values.C.Value }}"
runbook_url (annotation)Link to resolution procedurehttps://wiki.anihpj.com/runbooks/errors

Template variables in annotations: {{ $values.A.Value }} — raw numeric value, {{ humanize $values.A.Value }} — human-readable (e.g., "1.5K ms"), {{ humanizePercentage $values.A.Value }} — percentage format (e.g., "5.2%"), {{ $labels.instance }} — label values from the alert instance.

13.4 Alert Rule Evaluation Groups

Evaluation groups control how often rules are evaluated and how they're organized. Rules in the same group share an evaluation interval. Organize by frequency: "critical-1m" group (every 1m) — error rate, dead monitoring, crash loops. "standard-5m" group (every 5m) — latency spikes, resource warnings. "daily" group (every 24h) — certificate expiry, SLO compliance reports. Groups also inherit folder permissions for team-based access control.

📝 Chapter 13 — Practice Questions

Q1

What does the RED method stand for and which metrics should you alert on?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Rate (requests/sec), Errors (failed requests %), Duration (P95/P99 latency). Every service endpoint should have alerts on all three. Rate tells you if traffic is normal, Errors tell you if something is broken, Duration tells you if users are experiencing slowness.

💡 EXPLANATION

RED was created by Tom Wilkie (Grafana Labs). Combine RED (service-level) with USE (resource-level) for complete coverage. For anihpj: RED monitors the Django API endpoints. USE monitors the K8s nodes running Django.

Q2

Why should you alert on SYMPTOMS rather than causes? Give concrete examples.

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Symptoms = what users experience (slow page loads, errors). Causes = why (high CPU, memory leak, network congestion). Alert on symptoms because: (1) Symptoms impact users directly — you know there's a real problem, (2) One symptom may have multiple causes — you need to investigate anyway, (3) Causes may be false alarms — CPU at 80% during batch processing is normal.

💡 EXPLANATION
  ✅ GOOD: "P95 latency > 500ms for /api/jobs/" (symptom)
  ❌ BAD:  "CPU > 80%" (cause — might be fine!)

  ✅ GOOD: "Error rate > 5% on checkout endpoint" (symptom)
  ❌ BAD:  "PostgreSQL connections > 80" (cause)
Q3

How do you use the USE method to design infrastructure alerts?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Utilization — % of resource used. Alert when > 80-90%. Saturation — queue depth, runnable threads. Alert when work is queueing. Errors — hardware/software errors. Alert on any non-zero rate. For anihpj K8s nodes: Utilization = CPU > 80%, Memory > 85%, Disk > 90%. Saturation = load average > CPU cores. Errors = disk read/write errors, network packet drops.

💡 EXPLANATION

USE is designed by Brendan Gregg. It's for RESOURCES (things you can run out of). RED is for SERVICES (things users interact with). Together they provide complete coverage. USE monitors "is my infrastructure healthy?" RED monitors "are my users happy?"

Q4

What is the dead monitoring problem and how do you solve it?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

If your application crashes, metrics stop flowing. ALL your alert rules (error rate, latency, etc.) go silent because there's no data. You think everything is fine — but your service is DOWN. Solution: Create a "dead man's switch" alert using up{job="anihpj"} == 0 or absent(up{job="anihpj"}). This alerts when monitoring itself fails.

💡 EXPLANATION

This is arguably the MOST important alert in your system. Without it, you have a silent failure scenario. Always pair it with NoData=Alerting on critical alert rules. The up metric is special — Prometheus auto-generates it for every scrape target. up=1 means target is reachable; up=0 means scrape failed.

Q5

How do you write an alert rule that fires when any pod in a namespace is crash-looping?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER
rate(kube_pod_container_status_restarts_total{
  namespace="anihpj"
}[10m]) * 600 > 3

This calculates the restart rate over 10 minutes and scales to per-10-minutes. If a pod restarts more than 3 times in a 10-minute window, it fires. Labels should include {{ $labels.pod }} and {{ $labels.container }} so you know exactly which pod is crashing.

💡 EXPLANATION

The kube_pod_container_status_restarts_total metric comes from kube-state-metrics. It's a counter of total restarts. rate(...[10m]) * 600 converts to "restarts per 10 minutes". A healthy pod has 0. A CrashLoopBackOff pod restarts every few seconds — easily detected by this alert.

Q6

What are the Google SRE Four Golden Signals and how do they map to Grafana alerts?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Latency: P95/P99 request duration → Alert: histogram_quantile(0.95, ...) > 2
Traffic: Requests per second → Alert: rate(requests[5m]) < 10 (abnormal drop)
Errors: Failed request rate → Alert: rate(errors[5m]) / rate(total[5m]) > 0.05
Saturation: How "full" the service is → Alert: queue depth, goroutines, connection pool usage

💡 EXPLANATION

The Four Golden Signals are from the Google SRE book. They're designed for user-facing systems. Latency = how fast, Traffic = how much, Errors = how many failures, Saturation = how close to capacity. Together they give you a complete picture of service health from the user's perspective.

Q7

What is alert fatigue and how do you prevent it?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Alert fatigue is when people stop paying attention to alerts because there are too many false/non-actionable ones. Prevention: (1) Use proper "Evaluate For" durations to filter transient spikes, (2) Only alert on actionable conditions (require a runbook), (3) Classify severity (critical→page, warning→Slack, info→email digest), (4) Use silences during maintenance, (5) Regularly review and tune alert thresholds.

💡 EXPLANATION

If your on-call engineer gets 50 alerts per night and 45 are false alarms, they'll start ignoring ALL alerts — including the 5 real ones. Alert fatigue is the #1 cause of missed incidents. The fix is not more alerts — it's BETTER alerts with higher signal-to-noise ratio.

Q8

How do you use humanize and humanizePercentage in alert annotations?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

{{ humanize $values.A.Value }} converts raw numbers to human-readable format: 1500 → "1.5K", 0.0532 → "53.2m". {{ humanizePercentage $values.A.Value }} formats as percentage: 0.052 → "5.2%". {{ humanizeDuration $values.A.Value }} formats seconds: 3661 → "1h 1m 1s". Always use these for readable alert messages.

💡 EXPLANATION

Without humanize: "Error rate: 0.072345854" — confusing. With humanizePercentage: "Error rate: 7.2%" — clear. These Go template functions make alert notifications actually useful. The exam may ask: "Which function formats a value as percentage in an alert annotation?" → humanizePercentage.

Q9

What evaluation interval should you use for critical vs non-critical alerts?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Critical alerts (every 1m): Error rate, dead monitoring, CrashLoopBackOff — issues that need IMMEDIATE attention. Warning alerts (every 5m): Latency spikes, resource warnings, slow queries — issues that need investigation soon. Info alerts (every 15m-1h): Disk space trending, certificate expiry — issues that need action within hours/days.

💡 EXPLANATION

Don't evaluate everything at 1 minute — it creates unnecessary load. Use evaluation groups to organize by frequency. If a disk fills over hours, you don't need to check it every minute. Match your evaluation interval to the urgency of the condition. Fast evaluation = fast detection but higher system load.

Q10

Write a complete PromQL alert for "more than 50 errors in any 5-minute window for anihpj".

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER
# Query A: Error count over 5 minutes (absolute, not rate)
increase(django_http_requests_total{
  job="anihpj",
  status=~"5.."
}[5m]) > 50

# Labels: severity=critical, team=sre, service=anihpj
# Summary: "{{ $value }} 5xx errors in last 5 min for anihpj"
# Evaluate Every: 1m, Evaluate For: 1m

Note: increase() gives absolute error count, not rate. Use this when you want "at least N errors" rather than "error percentage > X%".

💡 EXPLANATION

increase() vs rate() for alerts: increase() = "more than 50 errors in 5 minutes" (absolute threshold). rate() = "more than 5 errors per second" (rate threshold). Choose based on what makes sense for your service. For low-traffic services, absolute counts are better. For high-traffic services, percentages/rates are better.

Chapter 14: Contact Points, Notification Policies & Silences 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 14: Routing Alerts to the Right People, Right Channel, Right Time

Contact Points define WHERE alerts go (Slack, Email, PagerDuty, Webhook, Kafka, Telegram). Notification Policies define HOW they get there — a routing tree with label matching. Silences define WHEN alerts don't fire (maintenance windows). Master the complete alert routing triad for production alerting.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~2 hours📧 Routing🌲 Policies

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ All 8 contact point types: Email, Slack, PagerDuty, Webhook, Discord, Telegram, Kafka, AWS SNS
  • ✅ Notification Templates: Go templating with labels, annotations, and values
  • ✅ Notification Policies: The routing tree — match labels → route to contact point
  • ✅ Timing controls: Group Wait, Group Interval, Repeat Interval
  • ✅ Silences: Temporarily mute alerts during maintenance with auto-expiration
  • ✅ Alert State History: Track alert transitions and overlay on dashboards
Contact Points & Policies — 4-6 Exam Questions

Know: (1) Contact Point vs Notification Policy — CP = WHERE, NP = WHICH alerts go WHERE, (2) Routing tree: top-down label matching, first match wins, (3) Group Wait/Interval/Repeat interval timing, (4) Silences vs disabling alert rules — silences are temporary and auto-expire, (5) Go template variables in annotations.

14.1 Contact Points — Where Alerts Go (8 Types)

📧
a) Email (SMTP)

Universal. Requires SMTP config in grafana.ini [smtp] section. Set host, user, password, from_address. Best for: daily digests, non-urgent alerts, external stakeholders who don't have Slack.

💬
b) Slack / MS Teams

Most popular for dev teams. Webhook URL-based — create Slack App → Incoming Webhooks → paste URL. Rich message formatting with colors, fields, buttons. Best for: team notifications.

📟
c) PagerDuty / Opsgenie

On-call management platforms. Integration keys, severity mapping, on-call schedules, escalation policies. Best for: critical alerts that wake people up at 3 AM.

🔗
d) Webhook (Custom HTTP)

Custom HTTP POST to any endpoint. JSON payload with full alert data. Best for: auto-remediation scripts, custom integrations, triggering CI/CD pipelines.

💬
e) Discord / Telegram

Community/dev chat platforms. Bot token-based. Quick setup — just add bot token. Best for: community alerts, dev environment notifications.

📡
f) Kafka / AWS SNS

Stream alerts to event bus for downstream processing. AWS SNS for fan-out to multiple subscribers. Best for: large-scale data pipelines, cloud-native architectures.

14.2 Notification Templates — Go Templating

Customize alert message content using Go templates. Access alert data: {{ .Labels.severity }} — alert labels, {{ .Annotations.summary }} — alert annotations, {{ .Values.A.Value }} — query expression values, {{ .Status }} — "firing" or "resolved". Humanize functions: {{ humanize $value }} → "1.5K ms", {{ humanizePercentage $value }} → "5.2%". Template examples: Slack rich message with color-coded severity, Email HTML with alert table, PagerDuty severity-mapped payload.

14.3 Notification Policies — The Routing Tree (EXAM TOPIC)

🌲 NOTIFICATION POLICY ROUTING TREE — anihpj Example
                        ┌─────────────────────┐
                        │    ROOT POLICY       │
                        │  (Default Catch-All) │
                        │  → Email: oncall@co  │
                        └──────────┬──────────┘
                                   │
              ┌────────────────────┼────────────────────┐
              │                    │                    │
     ┌────────▼────────┐  ┌───────▼────────┐  ┌───────▼────────┐
     │ severity=       │  │ severity=      │  │ severity=info  │
     │ critical        │  │ warning        │  │                │
     │                 │  │                │  │                │
     │ 📟 PagerDuty    │  │    (continue)  │  │ 📧 Daily Email │
     │ (Wake someone!) │  └───────┬────────┘  │   Digest       │
     └─────────────────┘          │           └────────────────┘
                         ┌────────▼────────┐
                         │ team=backend    │
                         │ 💬 Slack        │
                         │ #alerts-backend │
                         └─────────────────┘
                         ┌────────▼────────┐
                         │ team=frontend   │
                         │ 💬 Slack        │
                         │ #alerts-frontend│
                         └─────────────────┘

  ⏱️ TIMING CONTROLS:
  Group Wait:     30s  (wait to batch alerts before first notification)
  Group Interval: 5m   (minimum time between notification batches)
  Repeat Interval: 4h  (resend if alert is still firing after 4h)

  🔑 KEY: Alerts traverse the tree TOP-DOWN. First matching policy WINS.
  If no match, falls to Root (default). Policies match on alert LABELS.
  This is why labels like severity and team are critical!
📖 Timing Controls Explained

Group Wait (30s): After first alert fires, wait 30s to group any additional alerts before sending. Prevents 50 individual Slack messages when 50 pods crash simultaneously. Group Interval (5m): Minimum time between notification batches for the same group. Prevents alert storms. Repeat Interval (4h): If an alert is still firing after 4 hours, resend as a reminder. Prevents "forgotten alerts."

14.4 Silences — "I Know, Don't Tell Me"

Silences mute alerts during planned events. Create via: Alerting → Silences → New Silence. Configure: Matchers (labels that identify WHICH alerts to silence — =, !=, =~, !~), Duration (start/end time), Comment (why? — visible to team). Use cases: planned maintenance (2-hour database migration), known outages being actively worked on, deployment windows, noisy alert tuning. Types: Pending (scheduled, not yet active), Active (currently silencing), Expired (past end time). Silences auto-expire — much safer than disabling alert rules (you might forget to re-enable).

14.4b Mute Timings — Recurring Scheduled Silences

🆕 Mute Timings — Exam Gap Alert!

Mute Timings are often confused with Silences, but they are fundamentally different. The official Grafana docs list them as a SEPARATE feature alongside silences.

Aspect🔕 Silence⏰ Mute Timing
NatureOne-time / ad-hocRecurring / scheduled
TriggerManual creation by adminAutomatic (calendar schedule)
Example"Mute DB alerts for today's migration""Mute every Saturday 2am-4am for maintenance"
ExpiryFixed end time, then goneRepeats weekly/monthly/indefinitely
CreationAlerting → Silences → NewAlerting → Notification Policies → Mute Timings
ConfigLabel matchers + start/end timeName + time intervals (days + hours)
Use CaseEmergency maintenance, unplanned outageWeekly backup window, weekend deploys
Audit Trail✅ Comment visible in silence list⚠️ Less visible — document externally
⏰ MUTE TIMING — How Recurring Schedules Work
  Mute Timing: "Weekend Maintenance Window"
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Schedule: Every Saturday, 02:00 AM - 04:00 AM UTC  │
  │                                                     │
  │  Mon  Tue  Wed  Thu  Fri  ┌──Sat──┐  Sun           │
  │  ███  ███  ███  ███  ███  │█MUTED█│  ███           │
  │                           │█02-04█│                 │
  │                           └───────┘                 │
  │                                                     │
  │  During 2am-4am Saturday:                           │
  │  • All matched alerts → evaluation STILL happens    │
  │  • Notifications → SUPPRESSED                       │
  │  • At 4:01 AM → notifications resume normally       │
  │                                                     │
  │  ⚠️ KEY: Mute Timings don't stop evaluation —       │
  │  they only suppress NOTIFICATIONS on a schedule.    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Time Interval Definition: Mute timings use time intervals defined with: Days of week (Mon-Sun), Time range (HH:MM-HH:MM UTC), Months (optional — for monthly maintenance), Location (timezone). You can define multiple time intervals per mute timing (e.g., "Weekend 2am-4am" AND "Wednesday 8pm-9pm").

Example Config (JSON):

{
  "name": "Weekend Maintenance",
  "time_intervals": [
    {
      "weekdays": ["saturday"],
      "times": [{"start_time": "02:00", "end_time": "04:00"}]
    },
    {
      "days_of_month": ["15"],
      "times": [{"start_time": "01:00", "end_time": "03:00"}]
    }
  ]
}
// Matches: Every Saturday 2am-4am AND every 15th of month 1am-3am
⚠️ Critical Exam Distinction

Silence = one-off: "Don't tell me about the DB migration today." Mute Timing = recurring: "Don't tell me about weekend maintenance — every weekend, forever." If the exam question mentions "recurring," "weekly," "every Saturday," or "scheduled" — the answer is Mute Timing, not Silence. If the question mentions "one-time," "today's migration," or "planned maintenance for 4 hours" — the answer is Silence.

⚡ Quick Tip: When to Use Each

Use Silence: Emergency patching, unplanned outage response, ad-hoc maintenance, DB migration happening NOW. Use Mute Timing: Weekly backup windows, recurring deployment slots, scheduled maintenance every Sunday, monthly security patching window. Use BOTH: Mute timing for the known weekend window + a silence for the unexpected extra hour when the backup runs long.

14.5 Alert State History & Forensics

Grafana tracks all alert state transitions: Normal→Pending→Firing→Resolved. View the alert state timeline per rule — when did each instance fire? How long did it last? Alert annotations auto-generate dashboard markers when alerts fire — creating a visual record of incidents overlaid on your graphs. The Alert Instances table shows all current and recent alert instances with their state and labels. Admin view provides system-level alerting health and performance metrics.

📊 Part 4 Summary: Alerting & Notifications

🔔
Alert EngineScheduler→Evaluator→State Manager→Notifier. Unified for all data sources.
⏱️
Evaluate ForPrevents alert flapping. Condition must persist N minutes before firing.
🎯
RED + USERED for services (Rate, Errors, Duration). USE for infrastructure.
🌲
Routing TreeTop-down label matching. First match wins. Root = default catch-all.
🔕
SilencesTemporary one-off mute during maintenance. Auto-expires. Safer than disabling rules.
⏰
Mute TimingsRecurring scheduled mute (e.g., every Saturday 2-4am). Defined on Notification Policies.
⚠️
Dead MonitoringNoData alerts catch when metrics stop — the silent failure killer.

📝 Chapter 14 — Practice Questions

Q1

What is the difference between a Contact Point and a Notification Policy?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Contact Point: Defines WHERE alerts go (Slack, Email, PagerDuty — the destination). Notification Policy: Defines WHICH alerts go WHERE based on label matching (the routing rules). Contact Point = phone number. Notification Policy = "If Mom calls, ring. If spam calls, ignore."

💡 EXPLANATION

You need BOTH: configure the destinations (Contact Points: Slack webhook URL, PagerDuty integration key, email SMTP), then define the routing rules (Notification Policies: match label severity=critical → PagerDuty, severity=warning → Slack). Without policies, all alerts go to the default/root contact point.

Q2

When would you use a Silence instead of modifying or disabling the alert rule?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Use Silences for TEMPORARY muting: planned maintenance (2-4 hours), known outages being actively worked on, deployment windows. Silences are timed, auto-expire, and leave an audit trail (comment explaining why). Never disable alert rules for temporary events — you WILL forget to re-enable them.

💡 EXPLANATION
  Silence: {service="anihpj-postgres"}, 2:00 AM - 4:00 AM
  → Alerts won't fire during DB migration
  → At 4:01 AM, silence auto-expires
  → Monitoring resumes automatically
  ✅ Safe, auditable, temporary

  vs.

  Disabling rule: ❌ Might forget to re-enable
  → 3 days later, DB crashes. No alert fires.
  → Users notice before you do.
Q3

How does the Notification Policy routing tree work? What happens if no policy matches?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

The routing tree is a top-down label matching system. Grafana checks each policy from top to bottom — the FIRST policy whose label matchers match the alert's labels WINS. If no policy matches, the alert falls through to the Root policy (default catch-all). You MUST have a root policy configured.

💡 EXPLANATION

Example: Alert has labels {severity="critical", team="backend"}. Policy 1: severity=critical → MATCH! → Route to PagerDuty. Policy 2: team=backend → NEVER REACHED (Policy 1 already matched). Order matters — place most specific policies first, general policies later.

Q4

What are Group Wait, Group Interval, and Repeat Interval? Why are they important?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Group Wait (default 30s): Wait before sending first notification — allows batching multiple alerts into one message. Group Interval (default 5m): Minimum time between notification batches for the same group — prevents alert storms. Repeat Interval (default 4h): How often to resend if alert is still firing — prevents forgotten alerts.

💡 EXPLANATION
  Without Group Wait: 50 pods crash → 50 separate PagerDuty calls
  With Group Wait=30s: 50 pods crash → 1 batched Slack message
  "50 pods in anihpj namespace are crash-looping"

  Without Repeat Interval: Alert fires at 2 AM, still broken at 10 AM
  → On-call engineer went back to sleep, forgot about it
  With Repeat Interval=4h: Alert fires at 2 AM, repeats at 6 AM, 10 AM
  → Reminder: "This is STILL broken!"
Q5

How do you configure Slack as a contact point in Grafana?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

1) In Slack: Create a Slack App → Enable "Incoming Webhooks" → Create webhook → Copy URL. 2) In Grafana: Alerting → Contact Points → New Contact Point → Type: Slack → Paste Webhook URL. 3) Optional: Configure channel override, username, icon. 4) Test with "Test" button. 5) Reference in Notification Policy.

💡 EXPLANATION

The webhook URL looks like: https://hooks.slack.com/services/T00000000/B00000000/xxxxxxxxxxxx. Keep this URL secret — anyone with it can post to your Slack channel. For MS Teams, the process is similar using the Teams "Incoming Webhook" connector.

Q6

How would you route critical alerts to PagerDuty and warnings to Slack, with everything else going to email?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER
  Contact Points needed:
  1. "PagerDuty-OnCall" (PagerDuty type, with integration key)
  2. "Slack-Alerts" (Slack type, with webhook URL)
  3. "Email-Digest" (Email type, with SMTP configured)

  Notification Policies (in order):
  Root Policy → Email-Digest (default catch-all)
    ├── severity=critical → PagerDuty-OnCall
    └── severity=warning  → Slack-Alerts
💡 EXPLANATION

CRITICAL design: The Root is your safety net. Everything that doesn't match a specific policy goes to Root. If you forget to add a label to a new alert, it still notifies someone (via Root) rather than silently disappearing. Never leave the Root policy unconfigured.

Q7

What Go template variables can you use in notification templates? Give examples.

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

{{ .Labels.severity }} — alert labels (for routing context). {{ .Annotations.summary }} — human-readable summary. {{ .Values.A.Value }} — query expression A's numeric value. {{ humanize .Values.A.Value }} — formatted (1.5K). {{ .Status }} — "firing" or "resolved". {{ .StartsAt }} — when alert started.

💡 EXPLANATION

Full Slack template example: "*{{ .Labels.severity | ToUpper }}*: {{ .Annotations.summary }}\nValue: {{ humanize .Values.A.Value }}\nRunbook: {{ .Annotations.runbook_url }}". These templates make alerts informative and actionable. Customize per contact point type for optimal formatting.

Q8

What happens when a silence expires? Does the alert fire immediately if the condition is still true?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

When a silence expires, Grafana re-evaluates the alert. If the condition is still true AND has been true for ≥ "Evaluate For" duration, the alert transitions to Firing and notifications are sent. The alert doesn't "remember" the silenced period — it evaluates based on current state. If the condition is still true, you'll get a notification shortly after the silence expires.

💡 EXPLANATION

Silence expiration behavior: The alert was in "Firing" state but silenced. Silence expires → Grafana checks: "Is condition still true? Has it been true for ≥ Evaluate For?" → If yes, notification fires. This is why silences are safe — if your maintenance takes longer than expected, you'll still get alerted once the silence expires.

Q9

How do annotations from alert rules appear on dashboard graphs?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Grafana automatically creates annotations on dashboard graphs when alerts fire and resolve. These appear as vertical markers showing: "Alert: anihpj High Error Rate [FIRING]" in red, and "Alert: anihpj High Error Rate [RESOLVED]" in green. This provides visual context — when you see a latency spike, you can see if an alert was active during that spike.

💡 EXPLANATION

This auto-annotation is enabled by default. It uses the built-in "Grafana alerts" annotation query. You can also create custom annotations via the API: POST /api/annotations with tags like "deployment", "incident", "config-change". Annotations are the key to answering "why did this graph change at 2 PM?"

Q10

You need to mute all staging environment alerts without affecting production. How?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Create a Silence with matcher: environment=staging. This mutes ALL alerts with that label, regardless of severity or service. Set a duration (e.g., 4 hours for a testing window). Add comment: "Load testing on staging — do not page." This silences staging alerts while production alerts continue normally.

💡 EXPLANATION

Alternative approach: Create a Notification Policy for environment=staging that routes to a "Muted" contact point (e.g., a /dev/null webhook). However, silences are simpler and leave a clear audit trail. If you frequently need to mute staging, consider using a separate Notification Policy that routes staging alerts to Slack only (no PagerDuty).

Chapter 15: User & Organization Management 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 15: Multi-Tenancy — Organizations, Users, Teams & Service Accounts

Grafana Organizations provide hard tenant boundaries — each org has its own data sources, dashboards, users, and alert rules. Users and Teams simplify access management at scale. Service Accounts replace deprecated API Keys for machine-to-machine authentication. Master these concepts — user management is 15% of the exam.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~1.5 hours👥 Admin🏢 Multi-Tenant

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Organizations as tenant boundaries — isolated resources per org
  • ✅ User roles: Grafana Admin (global), Org Admin, Editor, Viewer
  • ✅ Teams for group-based permission management
  • ✅ Service Accounts for machine-to-machine API access (replaces API Keys)
  • ✅ LDAP/OAuth/SAML user directory sync for auto-provisioning
User Management — 3-5 Exam Questions

Know: (1) Organizations = hard boundaries (separate everything), Folders = soft boundaries (within one org), (2) Service Accounts replace API Keys (deprecated), (3) Four user roles: Grafana Admin, Org Admin, Editor, Viewer, (4) Teams group users for permission assignment, (5) LDAP/OAuth auto-sync for large organizations.

15.1 Organizations — Multi-Tenancy in Grafana

🏢 ORGANIZATIONS — Hard Tenant Boundaries
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    GRAFANA INSTANCE (Server)                          │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ┌──────────────────┐ ┌──────────────────┐ ┌──────────────────┐     │
  │  │ 🏢 Org 1:        │ │ 🏢 Org 2:        │ │ 🏢 Org 3:        │     │
  │  │ Engineering      │ │ Product          │ │ Analytics        │     │
  │  │                  │ │                  │ │                  │     │
  │  │ 📊 Dashboards    │ │ 📊 Dashboards    │ │ 📊 Dashboards    │     │
  │  │ 🔌 Prometheus    │ │ 🔌 PostgreSQL    │ │ 🔌 Snowflake     │     │
  │  │ 🔌 Loki          │ │ 🔌 GoogleAnalyt  │ │ 🔌 BigQuery      │     │
  │  │ 🔔 Alert Rules   │ │ 🔔 Alert Rules   │ │ 🔔 Alert Rules   │     │
  │  │ 👥 Users (SREs)  │ │ 👥 Users (PMs)   │ │ 👥 Users (Data)  │     │
  │  └──────────────────┘ └──────────────────┘ └──────────────────┘     │
  │                                                                      │
  │  🔑 KEY: Each org is COMPLETELY isolated                              │
  │  • Org A users can't see Org B dashboards (even if same server)      │
  │  • Data sources are org-scoped (each org configures its own)          │
  │  • A user can belong to MULTIPLE orgs — switch via User Menu          │
  │  • Org = HARD boundary. Folder = SOFT boundary (within one org)       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Engineering Org (SREs monitor infrastructure via Prometheus + Loki), Product Org (Product Managers track business KPIs via PostgreSQL), Analytics Org (Data team analyzes trends via Snowflake/BigQuery). Each org has its own data sources, dashboards, and users — completely isolated from each other. A Grafana Admin manages ALL orgs; an Org Admin manages only their org.

15.2 Users & Teams — Role-Based Access

👤
User Roles (Per-Org)

Viewer: Read dashboards only. No edits, no panel inspection.
Editor: Create/modify dashboards and panels. Cannot manage permissions.
Admin: Full org control — manage users, teams, data sources, settings.
Grafana Admin (Global): Server-level superuser. Manage ALL orgs, server settings.

👥
Teams — Group Permissions

Teams group users for bulk permission assignment. Instead of granting "Editor" to 20 individual users, create a "Backend-Devs" team and grant Editor to the team once. Teams can be assigned to folders and dashboards.
anihpj Teams: Backend-Devs, Frontend-Devs, SRE, Product-Analysts, Data-Scientists.

RoleScopeCan Create Dashboards?Can Manage Users?Can Add Data Sources?
ViewerPer-Org❌ No❌ No❌ No
EditorPer-Org✅ Yes❌ No❌ No
Org AdminPer-Org✅ Yes✅ In that org✅ Yes
Grafana AdminServer-wide✅ Yes✅ All orgs✅ Yes

User creation: Admin-created (Administration → Users → Invite) or self-registration (if allow_sign_up=true in grafana.ini). Auto-assign org: New users can be automatically added to a default organization on first login. User preferences (theme, timezone, home dashboard) are per-user, per-org.

15.3 Service Accounts — Machine-to-Machine API Access

🤖 SERVICE ACCOUNTS — Replace API Keys
  API Keys (DEPRECATED):                    Service Accounts (CURRENT):
  ─────────────────────                     ─────────────────────────
  • No role assignment                      • Role-based (Viewer/Editor/Admin)
  • No audit trail (which key did what?)    • Appears as user in audit logs
  • Manual key rotation                     • Token-based with expiration
  • Cannot be managed via API               • Full API management support
  • Legacy, will be removed                 • Current standard

  CREATE: Administration → Service Accounts → Add Service Account
  → Name: "anihpj-cicd-bot"
  → Role: Editor
  → Generate Token: Save immediately! (shown only ONCE)
  → Use: curl -H "Authorization: Bearer glsa_xxxxxxxxxxxx" /api/...

For anihpj CI/CD: A Service Account with Editor role auto-deploys dashboards on every release. Another Service Account with Viewer role allows the status page to query metrics. Service Account tokens should be stored in HashiCorp Vault, K8s Secrets, or CI/CD environment variables — NEVER commit to Git.

15.4 User Directory Sync — LDAP, OAuth, SAML

📂
LDAP / Active Directory

Auto-sync users and groups from corporate directory. Map LDAP groups to Grafana teams: "cn=sre-team,ou=groups" → SRE Team with Admin role. Configure in grafana.ini [auth.ldap] with TOML config file.

🔗
OAuth / OpenID Connect

Google, GitHub, GitLab, Okta, Azure AD, Auth0. Users log in with existing identity. Auto-provision on first login with role mapping: role_attribute_path = contains(groups[*], 'sre') && 'Admin' || 'Viewer'.

🔐
SAML (Enterprise)

Enterprise SSO with advanced team sync. Auto-map SAML group claims to Grafana teams. Okta, OneLogin, Azure AD, PingIdentity supported. Requires Enterprise license.

🔄
Auth Proxy

Nginx/Apache handles authentication, passes user info via HTTP headers (X-WEBAUTH-USER). Grafana trusts the proxy. No built-in login form needed. Used with SSO proxies.

🎯 Chapter 15 Exam Focus — Top 7 Things to Memorize
  • ✅ Organization = hard tenant boundary: Separate data sources, dashboards, users per org
  • ✅ Folder = soft boundary: Same org, shares data sources. Permission grouping for dashboards
  • ✅ 3 roles per org: Viewer (read), Editor (create/edit), Admin (permissions). Separate from Grafana Admin.
  • ✅ Grafana Admin = server-wide superuser: Creates orgs, manages server settings. Not tied to any org.
  • ✅ Teams = group users for permissions: Assign team to folder → all members get folder access
  • ✅ Service Accounts = machine auth: Replaces API keys. Tokens start with glsa_. For CI/CD automation.
  • ✅ LDAP/OAuth sync: Map directory groups → Grafana teams. Auto-provision users on first login.

📝 Chapter 15 — Practice Questions

Q1

What is the difference between an Organization and a Folder in Grafana?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Organization: HARD tenant boundary. Each org has its OWN data sources, dashboards, users, alert rules, and teams. A user in Org A cannot see Org B's resources. Folder: SOFT boundary within ONE organization. Groups dashboards and applies permissions — but shares the same data sources and users as the parent org.

💡 EXPLANATION
  Grafana Instance
  ├── 🏢 Org: Engineering (HARD boundary)
  │   ├── 📁 Folder: /Production (SOFT boundary)
  │   └── 📁 Folder: /Staging
  └── 🏢 Org: Product (HARD boundary)
      └── 📁 Folder: /Business-KPIs

  Orgs = separate everything. Folders = organize within an org.
Q2

What are the four user roles in Grafana and what can each do?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Viewer: Read-only access to dashboards. No edits.
Editor: Create/modify dashboards and panels. Cannot manage users or data sources.
Org Admin: Full control within their org — manage users, teams, data sources, settings.
Grafana Admin: Server-level superuser. Manage ALL orgs, server config, global settings.

💡 EXPLANATION

Key exam point: An Org Admin can manage everything WITHIN their organization, but cannot see or manage OTHER organizations. Only a Grafana Admin has cross-org visibility. The first user created (default admin/admin) is automatically a Grafana Admin.

Q3

What is a Service Account and why does it replace API Keys?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Service Accounts are role-based machine identities for API access. They replace API Keys (deprecated) because: (1) They have roles (Viewer/Editor/Admin) — API Keys had no role system, (2) They appear in audit logs — you can see which Service Account made changes, (3) Token management with expiration, (4) Full API support for CRUD operations.

💡 EXPLANATION

Create: Admin → Service Accounts → Add. Tokens are shown ONCE — store securely. Use Bearer auth: curl -H "Authorization: Bearer glsa_xxx" /api/dashboards/home. For anihpj: CI/CD pipeline uses a Service Account with Editor role to auto-deploy dashboards on release.

Q4

How do you set up Team-based dashboard permissions?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

1) Create a Team: Administration → Teams → New Team (e.g., "Backend-Devs"). 2) Add users to the team. 3) Go to Dashboard/Folder → Settings → Permissions → Add Team Permission → Select team + role (Viewer/Editor/Admin). All team members inherit the permission. Much more scalable than granting permissions to individual users one at a time.

💡 EXPLANATION

Best practice: NEVER assign permissions to individual users. Always use Teams. When a new developer joins, just add them to the "Backend-Devs" team — they immediately get access to all team dashboards. When someone leaves, remove from team — access revoked everywhere instantly.

Q5

How does LDAP directory sync work in Grafana?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Configure [auth.ldap] in grafana.ini with a TOML config file. Grafana connects to LDAP/AD server, searches for users matching the filter, and syncs them. LDAP groups can be mapped to Grafana teams: "cn=sre-team,ou=groups,dc=company" → SRE Team with Admin role. Users log in with LDAP credentials — Grafana doesn't store passwords.

💡 EXPLANATION

LDAP is ideal for enterprises with existing directory services. Key settings: host, port, bind_dn, search_filter, group_mappings. Sync happens on user login — new LDAP users are auto-created in Grafana. Group membership controls role assignment. No manual user management needed for large orgs.

Q6

Can a user belong to multiple Grafana organizations? How do they switch?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Yes. A user can belong to multiple organizations with potentially DIFFERENT roles in each. For example: Jane is an Admin in the Engineering org but only a Viewer in the Product org. Switch organizations via the User Menu (avatar) → Switch Organization. Each org has its own set of dashboards, data sources, and permissions.

💡 EXPLANATION

This is powerful for cross-functional teams: an SRE might need Admin access to Engineering org dashboards but only Viewer access to Product org dashboards. The active organization is shown in the user menu. All navigation (sidebar, dashboards, alerting) is scoped to the current org.

Q7

What happens when allow_sign_up is set to true vs false?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

allow_sign_up=true: Anyone can create an account via the login page "Sign Up" link. New users are auto-assigned to the default org (if auto_assign_org=true). allow_sign_up=false: Only admins can create accounts via Administration → Users → Invite. Recommended for production — prevents unauthorized access.

💡 EXPLANATION

Configuration in grafana.ini [users] section: allow_sign_up = false, auto_assign_org = true, auto_assign_org_role = Viewer. For OAuth/LDAP: sign-up control is in the auth provider config. Always disable public sign-up in production — use admin invitation or directory sync instead.

Q8

How do you implement Service Account authentication in a CI/CD pipeline?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER
# 1. Create Service Account (once, via UI)
# Administration → Service Accounts → "anihpj-cicd" (Editor role)
# Generate token: glsa_xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx

# 2. Store token in CI/CD secrets (GitHub Secrets, GitLab CI Variables)
# NEVER commit token to Git!

# 3. Use in CI/CD pipeline
curl -X POST /api/dashboards/db \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $GRAFANA_SERVICE_TOKEN" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d @dashboard.json
💡 EXPLANATION

Typical CI/CD flow: Developer commits dashboard JSON → CI/CD pipeline runs → Service Account updates dashboard in Grafana. The Service Account token should have Editor role (can modify dashboards). For read-only operations (e.g., status page), use a separate Service Account with Viewer role for least privilege.

Q9

What is the difference between a Grafana Admin and an Org Admin?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Grafana Admin (server-level): Can manage ALL organizations, create/delete orgs, manage server settings (grafana.ini), view server stats, manage global users. The ultimate superuser. Org Admin (org-level): Can manage everything WITHIN their own organization — users, teams, data sources, dashboards, alert rules. Cannot see or affect other organizations.

💡 EXPLANATION

Analogy: Grafana Admin = building manager (has keys to ALL apartments). Org Admin = apartment tenant (controls everything INSIDE their apartment, but not others). The first user created during Grafana setup (admin/admin) is a Grafana Admin. You can promote other users to Grafana Admin via Server Admin → Users.

Q10

Your company has 500 employees. How should you manage Grafana access at scale?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

1) Use LDAP/OAuth/SAML — never manually create 500 users. Configure directory sync so users auto-provision on first login. 2) Use Teams for all permission assignments — map LDAP/OAuth groups to Grafana teams. 3) Set auto_assign_org=true with auto_assign_org_role=Viewer as default. 4) Disable allow_sign_up — only directory-authenticated users get access. 5) Use folder-level permissions inherited by all dashboards inside.

💡 EXPLANATION

At scale, you manage 5-10 TEAMS, not 500 individual users. When a new engineer joins: IT adds them to the "Engineering" LDAP group → they automatically get Grafana access with the correct role. When they leave: IT removes from LDAP → access revoked everywhere. Zero manual Grafana user management.

Chapter 16: Authentication & Security 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 16: Securing Your Grafana Instance — Authentication, Authorization & Hardening

Authentication verifies WHO you are (Built-in, LDAP, OAuth, SAML, Auth Proxy). Authorization controls WHAT you can do (Roles, Teams, Permissions). Security hardening protects the instance itself. Master all authentication methods — security is a core exam domain.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~1.5 hours🔐 Security🔑 Auth

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Six authentication methods: Built-in, LDAP, OAuth/OIDC, SAML, Auth Proxy, Anonymous
  • ✅ OAuth configuration with GitHub as a real example
  • ✅ Role mapping: How OAuth claims map to Grafana roles
  • ✅ Security hardening: TLS, secret key, CSP headers, rate limiting
  • ✅ Data source security: Credential encryption, proxy mode, read-only access
Authentication — 3-5 Exam Questions

Know: (1) Six auth methods and when to use each, (2) OAuth configuration pattern (client_id, client_secret, auth_url, token_url, api_url), (3) Role mapping via role_attribute_path, (4) Anonymous access for public dashboards, (5) Auth Proxy when Grafana sits behind nginx/Apache with existing auth.

16.1 Authentication Methods — Complete Reference

🔑
a) Built-in (Grafana DB)

Username/password stored in Grafana's database. Simplest. Users created by admin or self-registration. Password policies: minimum length, complexity. Best for: small teams, quick setups.

📂
b) LDAP / Active Directory

Enterprise directory integration. Users authenticate with corporate credentials. Groups map to Grafana teams. Grafana never sees passwords — LDAP server verifies them. Best for: large enterprises.

🔗
c) OAuth 2.0 / OIDC

Google, GitHub, GitLab, Okta, Azure AD, Auth0. Users click "Sign in with X". No separate Grafana password. Role mapping via claims. Best for: modern teams using identity providers.

🔐
d) SAML (Enterprise)

Enterprise SSO with XML-based assertions. Advanced team sync based on SAML group claims. Used by Okta, OneLogin, Azure AD, PingIdentity. Best for: enterprises with SAML infrastructure.

🔄
e) Auth Proxy

External proxy (nginx/Apache) handles auth, passes authenticated username via HTTP header. Grafana trusts the header. No login form in Grafana. Best for: environments with existing SSO proxy.

👁️
f) Anonymous Access

Public dashboards without login. Read-only. Configurable in grafana.ini: [auth.anonymous] enabled=true. Org role for anonymous users. Best for: public status pages, TV displays.

16.2 OAuth Configuration — GitHub Example

🔐 OAUTH AUTHENTICATION FLOW — How GitHub Login Works in Grafana
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  1️⃣ USER CLICKS "Sign in with GitHub"                                    │
  │     Browser → GET /login/github → Grafana Server                         │
  │                                                                          │
  │  2️⃣ GRAFANA REDIRECTS TO GITHUB                                          │
  │     Browser → https://github.com/login/oauth/authorize?                  │
  │     client_id=XXX&redirect_uri=https://grafana.company.com/login/github  │
  │                                                                          │
  │  3️⃣ USER LOGS IN AT GITHUB (if not already)                              │
  │     GitHub shows: "Grafana wants to access your email and orgs"          │
  │     User clicks "Authorize"                                              │
  │                                                                          │
  │  4️⃣ GITHUB REDIRECTS BACK WITH CODE                                      │
  │     Browser → https://grafana.company.com/login/github?code=AUTH_CODE    │
  │                                                                          │
  │  5️⃣ GRAFANA EXCHANGES CODE FOR ACCESS TOKEN (server-to-server)           │
  │     Grafana → POST https://github.com/login/oauth/access_token           │
  │     {client_id, client_secret, code} → {access_token: "gho_xxx"}         │
  │                                                                          │
  │  6️⃣ GRAFANA FETCHES USER INFO FROM GITHUB API                            │
  │     Grafana → GET https://api.github.com/user                            │
  │     Authorization: Bearer gho_xxx                                        │
  │     ← {login: "janedoe", email: "jane@company.com", orgs: [...])}       │
  │                                                                          │
  │  7️⃣ GRAFANA APPLIES ROLE MAPPING                                         │
  │     role_attribute_path evaluates: "jane is in 'sre' org → Admin"        │
  │                                                                          │
  │  8️⃣ GRAFANA CREATES/UPDATES USER & CREATES SESSION                      │
  │     User "janedoe" created/updated in DB with Admin role                 │
  │     Session cookie set in browser → logged in! 🎉                        │
  │                                                                          │
  │  🔑 KEY: Grafana never sees the user's GitHub password.                   │
  │  The user authenticates at GitHub, Grafana only gets a token.            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
1
Create GitHub OAuth App

GitHub → Settings → Developer Settings → OAuth Apps → Register. Callback: https://grafana.company.com/login/github

2
Get Client Credentials

Copy Client ID + generate Client Secret. These identify your Grafana instance to GitHub.

3
Configure grafana.ini

Enable [auth.github], set client_id, client_secret, allowed_organizations.

4
Set Role Mapping

Map GitHub org membership to Grafana roles: "mycompany/sre" → Admin, "mycompany/dev" → Editor.

5
Restart & Test

Restart Grafana. Login page shows "Sign in with GitHub". Test with a GitHub user.

ini — grafana.ini [auth.github]
[auth.github]
enabled = true
client_id = YOUR_GITHUB_OAUTH_CLIENT_ID
client_secret = YOUR_GITHUB_OAUTH_CLIENT_SECRET
scopes = user:email,read:org
auth_url = https://github.com/login/oauth/authorize
token_url = https://github.com/login/oauth/access_token
api_url = https://api.github.com/user
allowed_organizations = mycompany
team_ids = 123456,789012
# Role mapping: org members in "sre" team → Admin in Grafana
role_attribute_path = contains(groups[*], 'mycompany/sre') && 'Admin' || 'Viewer'

16.3 Security Best Practices — Hardening Checklist

🔒
Change Default Admin Password

Set via GF_SECURITY_ADMIN_PASSWORD env var on first start. Never use admin/admin in production.

🔐
Enable HTTPS/TLS

Set protocol=https in grafana.ini. Provide cert_file + cert_key. Or terminate TLS at reverse proxy.

🔑
Set Strong Secret Key

Generate: openssl rand -base64 32. Set GF_SECURITY_SECRET_KEY. Used for signing auth tokens.

🚫
Disable Public Sign-Up

Set allow_sign_up=false. Control access via admin invites or directory sync only.

🛡️
Content Security Policy

Configure CSP headers to prevent XSS. Set content_security_policy = true in grafana.ini.

⏱️
Rate Limiting (Enterprise)

Prevent brute force attacks. Limit API requests per user/IP. Enterprise/Labs feature.

📋
Audit Logging (Enterprise)

Track who did what: user logins, dashboard changes, config modifications. Compliance requirement.

🔄
Regular Updates

Upgrade Grafana regularly for CVE patches. Check release notes for breaking changes. Test on staging first.

🔥
Network Firewall

Restrict Grafana to internal network. Use VPN or SSH tunneling for remote access. Never expose directly to internet.

16.4 Data Source Security — Protecting Your Data

🔒
Credential Encryption

Data source passwords are encrypted at rest (AES-256) in Grafana's database using the secret_key. If secret_key changes, encrypted credentials become unreadable — all data source connections fail. Keep secret_key backed up and rotated carefully.

🔄
Server (Proxy) Mode

Grafana backend proxies ALL queries to data sources. The user's browser never connects directly to your databases. Data sources can be on private networks — only Grafana needs network access. This is the DEFAULT and recommended mode for all production data sources.

👁️
Read-Only Credentials

Create dedicated read-only database users for Grafana: GRANT SELECT ON ALL TABLES TO grafana_reader. Never use admin/root credentials. Limits damage if credentials leak — data exposure without data destruction.

📏
Row Limits & Query Timeout

Set Max Rows in data source config to prevent accidental massive queries. Set Query Timeout (default 30s) to prevent runaway queries. Connection pooling limits protect your database from Grafana dashboard traffic.

📝 Chapter 16 — Practice Questions

Q1

What are the six authentication methods available in Grafana?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

1) Built-in (Grafana DB), 2) LDAP (Active Directory), 3) OAuth 2.0 / OIDC (Google, GitHub, Okta), 4) SAML (Enterprise SSO), 5) Auth Proxy (nginx/Apache), 6) Anonymous (public access). Multiple methods can be enabled simultaneously — users choose which to use on the login page.

💡 EXPLANATION

Best practice: For teams, use OAuth with your existing identity provider (GitHub/Google/Okta). For enterprises, use LDAP or SAML for directory integration. Disable built-in auth (disable_login_form=true) when using external auth to prevent backdoor logins. Anonymous access is for public dashboards only.

Q2

How does OAuth role mapping work in Grafana?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

role_attribute_path is a JMESPath expression that maps OAuth claims to Grafana roles. Example: contains(groups[*], 'sre-team') && 'Admin' || 'Viewer' means: "If user is in 'sre-team' group → Admin role, otherwise → Viewer." The expression evaluates against the OAuth provider's user info response.

💡 EXPLANATION

Complex example: contains(groups[*], 'admin') && 'Admin' || contains(groups[*], 'editor') && 'Editor' || 'Viewer'. This creates a three-tier role hierarchy based on OAuth group membership. If the user doesn't match any condition, they fall through to the last value (Viewer).

Q3

What is the Auth Proxy authentication method and when would you use it?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Auth Proxy delegates authentication to an external proxy (nginx, Apache, Traefik). The proxy authenticates the user (via SSO, LDAP, etc.), then passes the username and other attributes to Grafana via HTTP headers (X-WEBAUTH-USER). Grafana trusts these headers and creates/logs in the user. Use when you already have a centralized auth proxy in front of multiple applications.

💡 EXPLANATION

Configuration: [auth.proxy] enabled=true, header_name=X-WEBAUTH-USER, auto_sign_up=true. CRITICAL: The proxy MUST strip incoming auth headers from clients and set them itself. Otherwise, a malicious client could send X-WEBAUTH-USER: admin and gain admin access. Always configure the proxy to overwrite these headers.

Q4

How do you enable anonymous access for public dashboards?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

In grafana.ini: [auth.anonymous] enabled=true, org_name=Main Org., org_role=Viewer. This gives unauthenticated users Viewer access to dashboards in the specified organization. Combine with Public Dashboards (v9+) for fine-grained control over which dashboards are publicly accessible.

💡 EXPLANATION

Anonymous access is ORGANIZATION-WIDE — all dashboards in that org become viewable. For selective access, use the Public Dashboard feature (Dashboard Settings → General → Public Dashboard) which generates a unique URL for that specific dashboard only. Anonymous access is perfect for NOC/TV wall displays showing dashboards without requiring login.

Q5

What is the purpose of allowed_organizations in OAuth configuration?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

allowed_organizations restricts login to ONLY members of specified GitHub organizations (or Google domains, GitLab groups, etc.). If a user is NOT in the allowed org, they cannot log in — even with valid credentials. This is a critical security control: "Only mycompany employees can access Grafana."

💡 EXPLANATION

Example: allowed_organizations = mycompany means only GitHub users who are members of the "mycompany" GitHub organization can sign in. This prevents random GitHub users from accessing your Grafana instance. Combine with team_ids for even finer control: only specific teams within the org.

Q6

How does Grafana encrypt data source credentials at rest?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Grafana encrypts data source passwords using AES-256 encryption with the secret_key from grafana.ini as the encryption key. The encrypted credentials are stored in the database. When Grafana needs to connect to a data source, it decrypts the credentials in memory using the secret_key. If the secret_key changes, ALL encrypted credentials become unreadable.

💡 EXPLANATION

This is why the secret_key is SO critical: (1) Back it up — if lost, you must re-enter ALL data source passwords, (2) Never change it without re-entering passwords, (3) Use the same secret_key when migrating to a new server. Generate a strong one: openssl rand -base64 32. Store in a secrets manager.

Q7

What is the security risk of using Browser (Direct) access mode for data sources?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

In Browser (Direct) mode, the user's browser connects DIRECTLY to the data source — bypassing Grafana's proxy. This means: (1) The data source must be accessible from the user's browser (public network or VPN), (2) Data source credentials may be exposed in browser developer tools, (3) No query-level access control — Grafana can't filter or audit queries. Server (Proxy) mode is ALWAYS more secure.

💡 EXPLANATION

Browser mode exists for edge cases: when the data source is a local service on the user's machine, or when using CORS-restricted APIs that only work from a browser context. For ALL production deployments, use Server mode. Grafana proxies all queries — data sources can be on private networks, credentials stay on the server, and queries are auditable.

Q8

How do you configure multiple authentication methods simultaneously?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Enable multiple [auth.*] sections in grafana.ini simultaneously. Grafana shows ALL enabled methods on the login page. Example: [auth.github] enabled=true + [auth.google] enabled=true + built-in login gives users three options. The login page displays buttons/tabs for each method. You can disable built-in login with disable_login_form=true to force SSO only.

💡 EXPLANATION

Common setup: GitHub OAuth for developers + Google OAuth for non-GitHub users + disable built-in login for security. Users choose their preferred identity provider. Each provider has its own role mapping — a user logging in via GitHub might get Editor, while the same user via Google might get Viewer (based on the different role_attribute_path settings).

Q9

What is the significance of the Grafana secret_key for session security?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

The secret_key is used to: (1) Sign authentication tokens (cookies) — prevents session hijacking. (2) Encrypt data source credentials in the database. (3) Sign CSRF tokens — prevents cross-site request forgery. If an attacker learns the secret_key, they can forge session cookies and impersonate any user. Always use a strong, randomly generated key.

💡 EXPLANATION

Generate: openssl rand -base64 32. This is equivalent to Django's SECRET_KEY. Never use the default. Never commit to Git. Rotate periodically. If compromised: generate new key → all sessions invalidated → all users must re-login → all data source passwords must be re-entered. The secret_key is the "keys to the kingdom" — protect it accordingly.

Q10

How would you secure a Grafana instance exposed to the public internet?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

1) NEVER expose Grafana directly — place behind nginx/Apache reverse proxy with TLS termination. 2) Enable HTTPS with strong ciphers and HSTS. 3) Disable built-in login (disable_login_form=true) — use OAuth/SAML only. 4) Set allow_sign_up=false. 5) Configure rate limiting (Enterprise). 6) Set strong secret_key. 7) Enable CSP headers. 8) Use fail2ban to block brute force. 9) Restrict IP ranges if possible (VPN/bastion). 10) Keep Grafana updated for security patches.

💡 EXPLANATION

Layered security: Network layer (reverse proxy, TLS, firewall/IP restrictions) → Authentication layer (OAuth/SAML, no built-in login, MFA) → Application layer (strong secret_key, CSP, rate limiting, regular updates) → Data layer (read-only DB credentials, encrypted at rest, proxy mode). Defense in depth — if one layer fails, others still protect you.

Chapter 17: Backup, Restore & Maintenance 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 17: Keeping Grafana Healthy — Backup, Restore & Maintenance

Grafana stores critical configuration — users, dashboards, data sources, alert rules. Learn what to backup, how to restore, upgrade strategies, and how to monitor Grafana itself. A good backup strategy is the difference between a 5-minute recovery and starting from scratch.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~1.5 hours💾 Backup🔧 Ops

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ What Grafana stores — database, file system, provisioning, plugins
  • ✅ Backup strategies for SQLite, PostgreSQL, MySQL
  • ✅ Restore procedures — full recovery in minutes
  • ✅ Upgrading Grafana safely with rollback plans
  • ✅ Monitoring Grafana itself with Prometheus metrics

17.1 What Grafana Stores (and Where) — The Recovery Map

💾 GRAFANA DATA MAP — What to Backup
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ WHAT GRAFANA STORES         WHERE                     BACKUP METHOD  │
  │ ─────────────────           ─────                     ─────────────  │
  │ 🗄️ DATABASE:                /var/lib/grafana/        SQL dump /     │
  │   • Users, passwords        grafana.db (SQLite)      file copy      │
  │   • Organizations           OR PostgreSQL/MySQL       pg_dump/       │
  │   • Dashboards (JSON)                                 mysqldump     │
  │   • Data source configs                                               │
  │   • Alert rules & history                                           │
  │   • API keys / Service Accts                                         │
  │   • Starred dashboards                                               │
  │   • User preferences                                                 │
  │                                                                      │
  │ 📁 FILE SYSTEM:             /etc/grafana/            Git (provision- │
  │   • grafana.ini             provisioning/            ing) or tar     │
  │   • Provisioning YAML/JSON  /var/lib/grafana/                          │
  │   • Custom images/logos     plugins/                                   │
  │   • Installed plugins                                                 │
  │                                                                      │
  │ 📸 SNAPSHOTS:               Grafana DB or remote     Optional,       │
  │   • Dashboard snapshots     storage                  auto-expire     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

17.2 Backup Strategies — Complete Coverage

StrategySQLitePostgreSQLKubernetes
MethodStop Grafana → copy filepg_dump (online)Velero/PVC snapshot
Downtime?❌ Yes (~1-2 min)✅ Zero✅ Zero
Consistency⚠️ Must stop first✅ Transaction-consistent⚠️ Crash-consistent
Restore Speed⚡ Copy file back🟡 pg_restore🟡 PVC restore
HA Support❌ Single instance✅ Multiple instances✅ StatefulSet
Best ForDev, small (<10 users)ProductionCloud-native
bash — Backup Commands
# === SQLite Backup (Simple Copy — Grafana MUST be stopped first!) ===
systemctl stop grafana-server
cp /var/lib/grafana/grafana.db /backup/grafana-$(date +%Y%m%d-%H%M).db
systemctl start grafana-server

# === PostgreSQL Backup (Can run while Grafana is running) ===
pg_dump -h localhost -U grafana -d grafana -Fc > grafana-backup-$(date +%Y%m%d).dump
# OR: pg_dump -h localhost -U grafana grafana > grafana-backup.sql

# === Dashboard Export via API (Supplement, not replacement for DB backup) ===
curl -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" /api/search?type=dash-db \
  | jq -r '.[].uid' | while read uid; do
    curl -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" /api/dashboards/uid/$uid \
      > dashboards/$uid.json
  done

# === Provisioning Files (Git is your backup!) ===
cd /etc/grafana/provisioning/
git add -A && git commit -m "backup: $(date +%Y%m%d)"
git push origin main
💡 anihpj Backup Strategy

1) Nightly PostgreSQL dump → S3 bucket (retained 30 days). 2) Provisioning files in Git — every change is versioned. 3) Weekly full dashboard export via API as JSON. 4) Automation via cron job that runs pg_dump → uploads to S3 → notifies Slack on success/failure. Recovery Time Objective (RTO): <15 minutes.

17.3 Restore Procedures — Full Recovery

1
Stop Grafana

systemctl stop grafana-server / docker stop grafana

2
Restore Database

SQLite: copy backup file. PostgreSQL: pg_restore.

3
Restore Files

Provisioning from Git. Custom images. Plugins.

4
Start Grafana

systemctl start grafana-server. DB migrations run automatically.

5
Verify Recovery

Check dashboards load. Data sources connect. Alerts evaluate.

bash — Restore Commands
# === PostgreSQL Restore ===
pg_restore -h localhost -U grafana -d grafana --clean grafana-backup-20260608.dump
# OR: psql -h localhost -U grafana -d grafana < grafana-backup.sql

# === SQLite Restore ===
systemctl stop grafana-server
cp /backup/grafana-20260608.db /var/lib/grafana/grafana.db
chown grafana:grafana /var/lib/grafana/grafana.db
systemctl start grafana-server

17.4 Upgrading Grafana — Safe Process

⚠️ Always Backup Before Upgrading!

Grafana automatically runs database migrations on startup when it detects a new version. Once migrated, you CANNOT downgrade without restoring from backup. Always: 1) Backup database, 2) Read release notes for breaking changes, 3) Test upgrade on staging, 4) Check plugin compatibility, 5) Have a rollback plan.

🐳
Docker Upgrade

docker pull grafana/grafana:latest
docker stop grafana
docker rm grafana
docker run ... (same volumes)
DB auto-migrates on start.

🐧
Linux Upgrade

apt-get update && apt-get upgrade grafana
OR: yum update grafana
systemctl restart grafana-server
DB auto-migrates on start.

☸️
K8s Upgrade

helm upgrade grafana grafana/grafana --version NEW_VERSION
K8s rolling update replaces pods one by one with zero downtime.

17.5 Monitoring Grafana Itself — Meta-Monitoring

Grafana exposes its own Prometheus metrics at /metrics (enable via [metrics] enabled=true). Key metrics to monitor: grafana_active_users — how many users are active, grafana_api_request_duration_seconds — API latency, grafana_alerting_active_alerts — how many alerts are firing, grafana_dashboard_view_total — most viewed dashboards, grafana_db_queries_total — database load. Build a "Grafana Monitoring" dashboard using the built-in "Grafana" data source to watch Grafana itself.

📊 Part 5 Summary: Administration & Security

🏢
OrganizationsHard tenant boundaries. Each org has own data sources, dashboards, users.
👥
Users & Teams4 roles: Viewer, Editor, Org Admin, Grafana Admin. Teams = group permissions.
🤖
Service AccountsReplace API Keys. Role-based machine identities with audit trail.
🔐
6 Auth MethodsBuilt-in, LDAP, OAuth, SAML, Auth Proxy, Anonymous. Mix and match.
💾
Backup StrategyDB dump + provisioning Git + dashboard API export. RTO: 15 minutes.
📊
Meta-MonitoringMonitor Grafana with Grafana. /metrics endpoint + built-in data source.

📝 Chapter 17 — Practice Questions

Q1

What does Grafana's database store? What happens if you lose it?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Grafana's database (SQLite/PostgreSQL/MySQL) stores ALL configuration: users, passwords, organizations, dashboards (JSON), data source connection settings, alert rules, alert history, API keys, service accounts, starred dashboards, and user preferences. If lost: you lose ALL of this. Your monitoring data (Prometheus/Loki) is safe — only Grafana config is lost.

💡 EXPLANATION

This is why backup is critical. Without a database backup: you must recreate ALL users, data source connections, dashboards, and alert rules from scratch. With dashboard JSON exports in Git + provisioning files, you can partially recover dashboards. But users, data sources, and alert rules need the database. Back it up!

Q2

What is the safest way to backup a SQLite-based Grafana instance?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Stop Grafana first, then copy the SQLite file. SQLite uses file-level locking — copying while Grafana is running risks getting a corrupted/inconsistent backup. Commands: systemctl stop grafana-server && cp /var/lib/grafana/grafana.db /backup/ && systemctl start grafana-server. For zero-downtime, switch to PostgreSQL which supports online backups via pg_dump.

💡 EXPLANATION

SQLite is file-based. While SQLite itself supports concurrent reads, copying the file while writes are happening can result in a database in an inconsistent state. PostgreSQL solves this — pg_dump creates a consistent snapshot without stopping the database. For production, use PostgreSQL specifically for backup flexibility.

Q3

What happens when you upgrade Grafana? What should you check first?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Grafana automatically runs database migrations on first startup after upgrade — updating the database schema to match the new version. After migration, you CANNOT downgrade without restoring a pre-upgrade backup. Before upgrading: always (1) check release notes for breaking changes, (2) backup the database, (3) check plugin compatibility, (4) test on staging first, (5) have a rollback plan.

💡 EXPLANATION

Breaking changes typically involve: deprecated config options removed, plugin API changes (some plugins stop working), authentication config format changes. The release notes at grafana.com/docs/grafana/latest/whatsnew/ are your best friend before ANY upgrade. Read them carefully — 5 minutes of reading can save hours of debugging.

Q4

How do you monitor Grafana's own health and performance?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Enable [metrics] enabled=true in grafana.ini — this exposes Prometheus metrics at /metrics. Scrape this endpoint with Prometheus. Key metrics: grafana_active_users, grafana_api_request_duration_seconds, grafana_alerting_active_alerts, grafana_db_queries_total. Use the built-in "Grafana" data source (it connects to Grafana's own internal metrics) to build monitoring dashboards.

💡 EXPLANATION

Meta-monitoring: "Who watches the watchers?" You do — by monitoring Grafana with another Grafana instance, or by having Grafana monitor itself via the built-in Grafana data source. Most teams deploy a separate "infra-monitoring" Grafana instance that monitors the production Grafana instance. If your monitoring breaks, you need to know about it.

Q5

Why should you use PostgreSQL instead of SQLite for production Grafana?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

1) Online backups: pg_dump works while Grafana runs — no downtime. 2) Multiple Grafana instances: PostgreSQL supports concurrent connections from multiple Grafana servers (HA setup). 3) Better performance: at 10+ concurrent users, SQLite becomes a bottleneck. 4) Replication: PostgreSQL supports streaming replication for DR. 5) Proper authentication: database users and roles vs. file permissions.

💡 EXPLANATION

SQLite is perfect for dev, small deployments (<10 users), and quick setups. Grafana Cloud uses PostgreSQL under the hood. The switch is simple: change GF_DATABASE_TYPE=postgres and provide host/user/password. Migration from SQLite to PostgreSQL is possible but requires a manual export/import process.

Q6

How do you backup and restore a Grafana instance running on Kubernetes?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

1) Database: If using external PostgreSQL (recommended), use pg_dump as usual. 2) PVC backup: Use Velero or your storage provider's snapshot feature to backup the PersistentVolumeClaim. 3) Provisioning: Store in Git — no backup needed. 4) Restore: Restore PVC from snapshot, or restore PostgreSQL dump, then restart Grafana pod. K8s StatefulSet with PVC provides built-in persistence.

💡 EXPLANATION

K8s-native backup tools: Velero (formerly Heptio Ark) can backup entire namespaces including PVCs. Cloud-specific: AWS EBS snapshots, GCP Persistent Disk snapshots. Best practice: Use an external PostgreSQL (e.g., RDS, Cloud SQL) rather than a K8s-hosted database — this separates compute (Grafana pods) from state (database), simplifying backups.

Q7

What is the recovery process if Grafana's secret_key is lost?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

If you lose the secret_key (e.g., after restoring a backup to a new server): Generate a new one with openssl rand -base64 32 and set it in grafana.ini. All existing user sessions are invalidated (everyone must log in again). Encrypted data source credentials become unreadable — you must re-enter all data source passwords. API keys/Service Account tokens become invalid. This is why secret_key must be part of your backup strategy.

💡 EXPLANATION

The secret_key is used for: signing auth tokens, encrypting data source credentials, and securing cookies. Back it up separately from the database — store in a password manager or secrets vault. If moving Grafana to a new server, keep the SAME secret_key to avoid credential re-entry. The secret_key should never be committed to Git.

Q8

How do you set up an automated backup for Grafana using cron?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER
# /etc/cron.d/grafana-backup
# Run daily at 2 AM
0 2 * * * root pg_dump -h localhost -U grafana grafana \
  | gzip > /backup/grafana-$(date +\%Y\%m\%d).sql.gz

# Cleanup backups older than 30 days
0 3 * * * root find /backup/ -name "grafana-*.sql.gz" -mtime +30 -delete

# Sync to S3 for off-site backup
0 4 * * * root aws s3 sync /backup/ s3://my-backups/grafana/
💡 EXPLANATION

For anihpj: Cron runs pg_dump nightly at 2 AM → compresses with gzip → uploads to S3. Retention: 30 daily backups kept locally, 90 days in S3. Monitoring: A separate cron job verifies the latest backup is <24h old, and alerts if missing. Test restores quarterly — a backup you haven't tested is not a backup, it's a wish.

Q9

How does Grafana's plugin system affect backup and upgrade strategies?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Plugins are stored on disk in /var/lib/grafana/plugins/. They are NOT in the database. Backup strategy: either back up the plugins directory, or use GF_INSTALL_PLUGINS (comma-separated list) to auto-reinstall on container startup. For upgrades: always check plugin compatibility — major Grafana version bumps may break plugins that haven't been updated. Test on staging before upgrading production.

💡 EXPLANATION

Recommended approach: Use GF_INSTALL_PLUGINS=grafana-clock-panel,grafana-worldmap-panel as an environment variable. On container start, Grafana auto-installs these plugins — no need to back up the plugins directory. This is declarative and reproducible. If a plugin isn't available in the catalog, keep its .tar.gz in a Git repo or artifact store.

Q10

Design a complete disaster recovery plan for anihpj's Grafana instance.

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER
  anihpj GRAFANA DISASTER RECOVERY PLAN:

  BACKUP (Automated, Daily):
  ├── 2 AM: pg_dump grafana DB → /backup/
  ├── 3 AM: Sync /backup/ → S3 (off-site)
  ├── 4 AM: Git push provisioning files
  └── 5 AM: Health check — verify backup succeeded

  RECOVERY (Target RTO: < 15 minutes):
  1. Provision new server / K8s pod
  2. Install Grafana (same version)
  3. Restore PostgreSQL: pg_restore from latest S3 backup
  4. Git clone provisioning files → /etc/grafana/provisioning/
  5. Set GF_SECURITY_SECRET_KEY (same as old instance)
  6. Start Grafana → DB auto-migrates
  7. Verify: dashboards load, data sources connect, alerts evaluate

  TESTING: Perform a full DR test quarterly.
  "A backup you haven't tested is not a backup — it's a wish."
💡 EXPLANATION

Key metrics: RTO (Recovery Time Objective) = how fast you must recover. RPO (Recovery Point Objective) = how much data you can afford to lose. For anihpj: RTO=15 min, RPO=24h (daily backups). If you need RPO=1h, switch to PostgreSQL continuous archiving (WAL shipping) for point-in-time recovery. Remember: Grafana is STATELESS — it queries data sources live. Losing Grafana means losing dashboards, not data.

Chapter 18: Query Inspector & Debugging 🔴 Advanced

Chapter 18: The Query Inspector — Your #1 Diagnostic Tool

The Query Inspector is the diagnostic powerhouse of Grafana. Learn to inspect queries, analyze raw response data, debug performance issues, and optimize slow dashboards. Every Grafana expert relies on it daily — this is the tool that separates beginners from professionals.

🔴 Advanced⏱️ ~1.5 hours🔍 Debug⚡ Performance

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Query Inspector: Stats, Query, Data, JSON, Error tabs — when to use each
  • ✅ Debug workflow: Query Inspector → Data tab → find the anomaly
  • ✅ 5 common query problems and their exact fixes
  • ✅ Dashboard performance optimization techniques
  • ✅ PromQL-specific debugging: counter resets, stale markers, range vectors
Query Inspector — 2-3 Exam Questions

Know: (1) How to access Query Inspector (Panel menu → Inspect → Query), (2) Which tab shows timing info (Stats), (3) Which tab shows raw data (Data), (4) "No data" troubleshooting sequence, (5) Dashboard performance optimization — fewer panels, recording rules, longer refresh intervals.

🧑‍🏫 Novice's Corner — What the Query Inspector ACTUALLY Does (Plain English)

If you're new to debugging in Grafana, here's the simplest way to understand the Query Inspector:

1. Think of it as "Show My Work" from math class. The Query Inspector shows you EXACTLY what Grafana asked the database (Prometheus/PostgreSQL/Loki), what it got back, and how long it took. Without it, you're looking at a graph that says "No data" with zero clues about why. With it, you see the actual request and response — like watching the conversation between Grafana and the data source in real-time.

2. Every panel has one. Click any panel title → Inspect → Query. It's not a separate tool — it's built into every panel on every dashboard. Even Viewers (read-only users) can access it. This means you can debug dashboards even if you can't edit them.

3.
