Five tabs tell a story: Query Tab = "What did Grafana ask?" → Data Tab = "What did the database say?" → Stats Tab = "How long did it take?" → JSON Tab = "Raw evidence, no interpretation" → Error Tab = "What went wrong?" (only appears when there's an error). Follow this sequence and you'll find the problem 95% of the time.

4. Why anihpj developers love it: Imagine the "Active Job Posts" stat panel shows 0. Without Query Inspector, you'd think "maybe there are no jobs?" With it, you see the PostgreSQL query returned 47 rows — but a transformation filtered them all out. Two clicks and you've found the real problem instead of wasting an hour checking if the database is empty.

5. The "Copy to Explore" trick: Query Tab → copy the query → paste into Explore (Ctrl+K → Explore). Now you can test the query independently — change variables, adjust the time range, modify the query — without affecting the dashboard. When it works in Explore, paste it back into the panel. This single workflow solves 80% of "my panel doesn't work" issues.

🔍 The Query Inspector — How It Shows the Data Flow

Every time a panel renders, Grafana goes through this pipeline. The Query Inspector lets you peer into EACH step — like having X-ray vision into the dashboard rendering process.

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │              🔍 QUERY INSPECTOR — The Data Flow You Can NOW SEE               │
  │                                                                                │
  │  Step 1: 🌐 USER OPENS DASHBOARD                                               │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  Browser → GET /api/dashboards/uid/anihpj-overview                      │   │
  │  │  Grafana responds: Full dashboard JSON (panels, queries, variables)     │   │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                      │                                         │
  │                                      ▼                                         │
  │  Step 2: 📋 QUERY TAB — "What Grafana Asked"                                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  Grafana resolves variables: $environment → "prod"                       │   │
  │  │  Grafana sends: rate(django_http_requests_total{env="prod"}[5m])        │   │
  │  │  Query Inspector → QUERY TAB shows this EXACT string                     │   │
  │  │  💡 COPY THIS → Paste into Explore → Test independently!                 │   │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                      │                                         │
  │                                      ▼                                         │
  │  Step 3: ⏱️ STATS TAB — "How Long It Took"                                     │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  ┌───────────────────┬──────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐   │   │
  │  │  │ Grafana overhead  │ 12ms     │ Variable resolution, auth check  │   │   │
  │  │  │ Data source query │ 145ms    │ Prometheus query execution       │   │   │
  │  │  │ Data processing   │ 8ms      │ JSON parsing, type conversion    │   │   │
  │  │  │ TOTAL             │ 165ms    │ ⚡ Great! (target: <500ms)       │   │   │
  │  │  └───────────────────┴──────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘   │   │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                      │                                         │
  │                                      ▼                                         │
  │  Step 4: 🗂️ DATA TAB — "What Came Back"                                        │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  Prometheus Response:                                                    │   │
  │  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │   │
  │  │  │ {method="GET",  endpoint="/api/jobs/"} → [42.3, 43.1, 41.8, ...] │   │   │
  │  │  │ {method="POST", endpoint="/api/jobs/"} → [12.1, 11.5, 12.8, ...] │   │   │
  │  │  │ {method="GET",  endpoint="/api/users/"}→ [8.2,  7.9,  8.5,  ...] │   │   │
  │  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │   │
  │  │  💡 Check: Right number of series? Right labels? Expected values?        │   │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                      │                                         │
  │                                      ▼                                         │
  │  Step 5: 🔄 TRANSFORM TAB — "After Transformations" (if any)                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  Merge query results → Filter by value > 10 → Sort descending           │   │
  │  │  💡 "Before" vs "After" comparison — did transforms do what you expect? │   │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                      │                                         │
  │                                      ▼                                         │
  │  Step 6: 📊 PANEL RENDERS — Data → Visualization                               │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  Time series line chart appears in browser                              │   │
  │  │  User sees: Beautiful graph showing request rates by endpoint           │   │
  │  │  (If graph is blank → go back to Step 2 and debug!)                     │   │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                                │
  │  🎯 THE GOLDEN RULE: If a panel shows wrong/no data, the Query Inspector       │
  │     will ALWAYS show you why. Start at the Query Tab, work through to           │
  │     the Data Tab. The answer is in one of these 6 steps.                        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

18.1 The Query Inspector — Every Tab Explained

📊
Stats Tab — Performance

Shows: total query count, total request time, data processing time. Use to identify SLOW queries. If one query takes 8 seconds and others take 200ms — that's your bottleneck. Also shows Grafana-side processing time vs data source query time.

📋
Query Tab — The Raw Query

See the EXACT query sent to the data source AFTER all variables are substituted. Copy-paste into Explore for isolated testing. This is your "show me what Grafana actually sent" view — invaluable for debugging variable issues.

🗂️
Data Tab — Raw Response

Every data point returned by the query. Verify: correct labels? Expected values? Right number of series? This is where you discover "why is my graph showing 3 lines when I expected 5?" — check the actual data returned.

📈
JSON Tab — Full Payload

Complete JSON response from the data source. Debug API-level issues: missing fields, format problems, authentication errors. The most detailed view — shows everything Grafana received from the data source including metadata.

❌
Error Tab — When Things Break

Only visible when queries FAIL. Shows the exact error message from the data source. "expected type range vector" → missing [5m] on rate(). "connection refused" → data source is down. Read error messages — they tell you exactly what's wrong.

🔄
Transform Tab — Data Shaping

Shows data AFTER transformations are applied. See how your Merge/Filter/Group By transforms changed the data. This is the "before vs after" view — essential for debugging transformation chains that produce unexpected results.

18.2 Common Query Problems & Solutions — Troubleshooting Guide

SymptomLikely CauseDiagnostic StepFix
"No data"Query returns empty setQuery Inspector → Data tab → see if emptyCheck labels match, time range includes data, metric exists
Slow panel loadExpensive queryStats tab → check query durationAdd label filters, reduce time range, use recording rules
Wrong valuesUnit mismatch / raw counterData tab → check raw valuesUse rate() on counters, check unit conversion
Missing seriesLabel mismatchQuery tab → see actual query sentVerify label selectors, check variable values
Intermittent dataStaleness / scrape gapStats → check data freshnessCheck scrape interval, increase "for" duration
Graph drops to 0Counter reset (restart)Data tab → see sudden dropUse rate()/increase() — they handle resets automatically
"expected type range vector"Missing [range] on rate()Error tab → read error messageAdd [5m]: rate(metric[5m]) not rate(metric)
🔧 Debug Workflow — The Systematic Approach
  🐛 DEBUG CHECKLIST: "My panel shows No Data / Wrong Data"

  Step 1: QUERY INSPECTOR → Query Tab
           └─ See exact query text. Copy → paste into Explore.
              Does it work in Explore? YES → problem is panel config.
              NO → problem is query or data source.

  Step 2: QUERY INSPECTOR → Stats Tab
           └─ Is the query even reaching the data source?
              0ms duration → connection issue. Check data source health.
              >10s duration → query too slow. Optimize or add limits.

  Step 3: QUERY INSPECTOR → Data Tab
           └─ What data actually came back? Empty? Wrong labels?
              Compare labels in data vs labels in your panel legend.
              Mismatch = legend shows nothing.

  Step 4: QUERY INSPECTOR → JSON Tab
           └─ Full raw response. Any error messages? Missing fields?
              This is the "no assumptions" view — the ground truth.

  Step 5: DATA SOURCE → Save & Test
           └─ Is the data source itself healthy?
              "Connection refused" → server down or wrong URL.
              "Authentication failed" → wrong credentials.

  ⚡ THE 80/20 RULE: 80% of "no data" problems are:
     1. Wrong time range (looking at old data)
     2. Label mismatch (variable value ≠ metric label)
     3. Counter graphed raw (use rate()!)

18.3 Dashboard Performance Optimization

📊
Limit Panels

Split large dashboards into multiple focused ones. Each panel = one query to the data source. 50 panels = 50 simultaneous queries. Break into: Overview (10 panels), Details (15 panels), Business (10 panels).

🔄
Recording Rules

Pre-compute expensive PromQL in Prometheus config. 20 dashboards querying the same complex expression → 1 recording rule → 20x less query load. Example: job:errors:rate5m.

⏱️
Increase Refresh

Default 30s refresh means 120 queries/hour per panel. Change to 5m = 12 queries/hour. For business dashboards (updated daily), set refresh to "Off" or 1h. NOC displays need fast refresh; weekly reports don't.

🎛️
Use Variables Wisely

Template variables limit data scope. $environment=prod narrows dashboards to one cluster instead of querying ALL clusters. Variables reduce data volume → faster queries.

📏
Min Step & Resolution

Set appropriate min_step in query options. For a 30-day view, 1-minute resolution is wasteful — use 1h. Grafana auto-adjusts, but you can set lower bounds to prevent overly fine-grained queries.

🔍
Query Inspector Profiling

Use Stats tab to profile dashboard load. Sort by query duration — fix the slowest first. A single 10-second query blocks the entire dashboard. Target: every query under 500ms.

📝 Chapter 18 — Practice Questions

Q1

How do you access the Query Inspector in Grafana?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Panel menu (click panel title) → Inspect → Query. Alternatively, while hovering over a panel, press 'i' on the keyboard. The Query Inspector opens as a slide-out panel showing Stats, Query, Data, JSON, Error, and Transform tabs.

💡 EXPLANATION

The Query Inspector is available on EVERY panel — even Viewers can access it. It's the primary debugging tool. The shortcut 'i' is not commonly known but saves time. The inspector shows what Grafana actually sent to the data source and what came back — invaluable for troubleshooting.

Q2

A dashboard shows "No data" for a Prometheus panel. What are the first 3 things you check?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

1) Time range: Is the dashboard showing a recent time range? Data from last 6h won't show if you're viewing "Yesterday." 2) Query Inspector → Query tab: See the exact PromQL sent — copy to Explore and test. 3) Data source health: Connections → Data Sources → Prometheus → Save & Test. Is the green check showing?

💡 EXPLANATION

Debug sequence: Time Range → Query Text → Data Source Connectivity → Label Match → Metric Exists. 80% of "no data" issues are solved by checking the time range (user is viewing a period before data started) or label mismatch (variable value doesn't match any metric labels).

Q3

Which Query Inspector tab shows query execution time? Why is this important?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Stats tab. It shows total query count, total duration, data processing time, and per-query breakdown. This is how you identify slow queries — sort by duration and fix the bottleneck. Grafana dashboard load is only as fast as the SLOWEST query on the page.

💡 EXPLANATION

A dashboard with 20 panels where 19 load in 50ms but 1 takes 8 seconds will feel slow — users wait for ALL panels. The Stats tab reveals this immediately. Fix: simplify the slow query, add label filters, use recording rules, or increase the min_step for longer time ranges.

Q4

You see "expected type range vector in eval" error in a PromQL query. What's wrong?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

You're using rate() without a range vector [time]. rate(http_requests_total) is WRONG — it needs rate(http_requests_total[5m]). The [5m] tells rate() to look at the last 5 minutes of data to calculate the per-second rate. Without it, rate() gets only a single value and can't compute a rate.

💡 EXPLANATION
  ❌ rate(http_requests_total) → ERROR: expected type range vector
  ✅ rate(http_requests_total[5m]) → CORRECT

  rate() needs to see HOW the counter changed over time:
  With [5m]:  100→105→110→115→120 → rate=(120-100)/300s=0.067/s
  Without:    120 (just the latest value, can't calculate rate)
Q5

How do recording rules improve dashboard performance?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Recording rules pre-compute expensive PromQL expressions and store the result as a new metric. Instead of 20 dashboards each running rate(errors[5m]) by (endpoint) independently, a recording rule computes it ONCE as job:errors:rate5m. All 20 dashboards read the cached result — reducing Prometheus query load by 95%.

💡 EXPLANATION

Recording rules are defined in Prometheus rules.yml (not Grafana). They run on a schedule (e.g., every 1m) and their output is treated as a regular metric. For anihpj: pre-compute error rates, P95 latency, and request counts — dashboards load instantly because the heavy computation is already done.

Q6

What's the difference between the Query tab and the Data tab in the Query Inspector?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Query Tab: Shows the exact query TEXT sent to the data source (after variable substitution). This is what Grafana ASKED. Data Tab: Shows the raw RESPONSE from the data source — the actual data returned. Query = request. Data = response. Use both together: verify the query is correct (Query tab), then verify the data returned matches expectations (Data tab).

💡 EXPLANATION

Debug workflow: 1) Query tab → copy query to Explore → does it return data? If no → query problem. If yes → 2) Data tab → do the returned labels/values look right? If yes → panel config issue (visualization settings, transformations). If no → data source issue.

Q7

Your Prometheus graph suddenly drops to zero and stays flat. What likely happened?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

A counter reset. The application/service restarted, and the counter metric (e.g., http_requests_total) reset to 0. If you graphed the RAW counter, it drops to 0. Solution: Always use rate(metric[5m]) or increase(metric[5m]) — these functions automatically detect and handle counter resets, showing smooth graphs even across restarts.

💡 EXPLANATION
  Raw counter:  100→105→110 [RESTART] 0→5→10 (graph drops to 0!)
  rate(counter[5m]): Smooth line, handles resets automatically
  
  rate() detects: new_value < old_value → counter reset → calculates
  rate from new baseline. This is the #1 reason to NEVER graph raw counters.
Q8

Why would a panel work in Explore but show "No data" on a dashboard?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Likely causes: 1) Variable issue: The panel uses $environment and its value doesn't match any labels. Explore uses raw values — no variables. 2) Time range difference: Explore might show last 5 min, dashboard shows last 6h. 3) Data source selector: Panel is set to a different data source than Explore. 4) Transformation filtering: Panel transformations are filtering out all data.

💡 EXPLANATION

Debug: Query Inspector → Query tab → copy the query (with variables resolved) → paste into Explore with the SAME time range. If it works in Explore but not on dashboard, check transformations. If it fails in Explore, the resolved query is the problem — check variable values.

Q9

What is the optimal dashboard refresh interval for a business KPI dashboard viewed once daily?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

"Off" or 24h. A dashboard viewed once per day doesn't need auto-refresh. Setting refresh to "Off" means data is loaded once when the dashboard opens. Setting it to 5s would generate 17,280 unnecessary queries per day — wasting data source resources. Match refresh to viewing frequency: NOC=10s, Operations=1m, Business=Off.

💡 EXPLANATION

Refresh interval impact: 10-panel dashboard at 5s refresh = 172,800 queries/day. Same dashboard at 1m = 14,400 queries/day. Same at "Off" = 10 queries/day. For anihpj: Production dashboard = 30s, Business KPI dashboard = Off, SLO dashboard = 1h. Always use the slowest refresh that meets the need.

Q10

A dashboard with 50 panels loads slowly. How would you optimize it? (List 5 techniques)

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

1) Split into multiple dashboards: 50 panels → Overview(10) + Details(20) + Business(20). 2) Use recording rules: Pre-compute expensive PromQL expressions. 3) Increase refresh interval: 30s → 5m reduces queries by 90%. 4) Add label filters: {job="anihpj"} narrows scope. 5) Increase min_step: For 30-day views, query at 1h resolution instead of 1m. Profile first with Query Inspector Stats tab.

💡 EXPLANATION

Optimization priority: 1) Profile (find slow queries), 2) Reduce query scope (filters, variables), 3) Reduce query frequency (refresh interval), 4) Pre-compute (recording rules), 5) Architectural (split dashboards). Always measure before and after with the Stats tab — data-driven optimization beats guesswork.

Chapter 19: Plugins & Extending Grafana 🔴 Advanced

Chapter 19: The Power of Plugins — Extend Grafana Beyond Built-in Features

Plugins are what make Grafana infinitely extensible. Data source plugins connect to new backends, panel plugins add new visualization types, and app plugins create full applications within Grafana. Learn to install, manage, and discover essential plugins for the exam.

🔴 Advanced⏱️ ~1 hour🔌 Plugins

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Three plugin types: Data Source, Panel, App — and when to use each
  • ✅ Installing plugins via CLI, Docker env vars, and K8s
  • ✅ Plugin signing, verification, and security considerations
  • ✅ Essential plugins tested on the exam
  • ✅ Plugin management: listing, updating, disabling, removing
Plugins — 2-3 Exam Questions

Know: (1) grafana-cli plugins install <id> command, (2) GF_INSTALL_PLUGINS for Docker, (3) Three plugin types (Data Source, Panel, App), (4) Grafana must be RESTARTED after CLI plugin installation, (5) Unsigned plugins require explicit enabling for security.

🧑‍🏫 Novice's Corner — What Plugins ACTUALLY Are (Plain English)

1. Think of Grafana as a smartphone. Out of the box, it does a LOT — make calls (time series graphs), send texts (stat panels), take photos (gauges). But what if you need something it doesn't do? That's where plugins come in — they're like "apps" for Grafana. Need a clock on your NOC display? Install the Clock Panel plugin. Need to connect to a custom database? Install its data source plugin.

2. Three kinds of "apps": Panel Plugins = new ways to VISUALIZE data (like Instagram filters for your graphs). Data Source Plugins = new places to GET data FROM (like adding a new bank account to your banking app). App Plugins = full applications inside Grafana (like installing a whole new app on your phone — OnCall, Incident Management, etc.).

3. Plugins aren't magic — they're code running on your server. When you install a plugin, you're downloading code that runs WITHIN Grafana. This is why signed plugins matter — a signed plugin is verified by Grafana Labs as authentic, like an app from the official app store. Unsigned plugins are like downloading a random .exe from the internet — Grafana warns you about them for a reason.

4. The "built-in" stuff is ALSO plugins. Prometheus, PostgreSQL, Loki — these are technically data source plugins that ship WITH Grafana. Even the Time Series graph is a built-in panel plugin. "Installing a plugin" means adding one that ISN'T bundled. This is why the plugin architecture matters — Grafana can connect to ANYTHING because everything is a plugin.

5. Why anihpj uses plugins: anihpj has a custom internal metrics API. Instead of building a whole new visualization tool, they use the Infinity data source plugin → connect to their API → visualize in Grafana. Two hours of work instead of two months. That's the power of the plugin ecosystem.

🔌 The Grafana Plugin Ecosystem — How Everything Connects
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

  │  │ 🔢 Stat      │  │ 📊 Prometheus│  │              │                        │
  │  │ 🎚️ Gauge     │  │ 📋 Table     │  │              │                        │
  │  │ 🥧 Pie       │  │ 📈 Graphite  │  │              │                        │
  │  │ ...13 total  │  │ ...10+ total │  │              │                        │
  │  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘                        │
  │                                                                                │
  │  🔑 KEY POINTS:                                                                │
  │  • "Built-in" panels/datasources = plugins shipped WITH Grafana                │
  │  • "Installed" plugins = downloaded from Grafana Plugin Catalog                │
  │  • All plugins use the SAME interface — Grafana treats them identically        │
  │  • 100+ plugins available at grafana.com/plugins                               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

19.1 Plugin Types — Complete Reference

🔌
Data Source Plugins

Connect Grafana to external systems. 100+ available: Prometheus, Loki, PostgreSQL, Elasticsearch, InfluxDB, Tempo, Jaeger, CloudWatch, Azure Monitor. Each has its own query editor and language. Core to Grafana's "connect to anything" philosophy.

📊
Panel Plugins

New visualization types beyond the 13 built-in. World Map, Clock, Diagram, Plotly, Discrete, FlowCharting. Extend Grafana's visual capabilities for specific use cases like geographic data or flowcharts.

📱
App Plugins

Full applications inside Grafana with their own UI, pages, and workflows. Grafana OnCall (incident management), Grafana Incident, Performance Testing (k6), Machine Learning. Integrated experiences, not just visualizations.

19.2 Installing & Managing Plugins

bash
# === CLI Installation (Linux/macOS) ===
grafana-cli plugins install grafana-clock-panel
grafana-cli plugins ls              # List installed
grafana-cli plugins list-remote     # Browse available plugins
grafana-cli plugins update grafana-clock-panel  # Update
grafana-cli plugins remove grafana-clock-panel  # Uninstall

# === Docker Installation (at container startup) ===
docker run -e "GF_INSTALL_PLUGINS=grafana-clock-panel,grafana-worldmap-panel" grafana/grafana

# === Kubernetes (Helm values.yaml) ===
plugins:
  - grafana-clock-panel
  - grafana-worldmap-panel

# ⚠️ IMPORTANT: Restart Grafana after CLI installation!
# Docker auto-installs plugins before Grafana starts — no restart needed.
# Plugin signing: Unsigned plugins require allow_loading_unsigned_plugins setting.
⚠️ Plugin Signing & Security

Grafana Labs signs official plugins. Signed plugins are verified on load — you know they're authentic. Unsigned plugins (community, custom) require GF_PLUGINS_ALLOW_LOADING_UNSIGNED_PLUGINS=plugin-id in grafana.ini. Only enable unsigned plugins you trust. Check plugin ratings, downloads, and update frequency before installing. Plugins have access to your data — treat them like you'd treat any third-party code.

🔌 Plugin Lifecycle Management — Enable, Disable, Update, Remove
OperationCLI CommandDocker/K8sUI (Admin → Plugins)
List installedgrafana-cli plugins lsCheck container logsShows all with versions
Installgrafana-cli plugins install <id>GF_INSTALL_PLUGINS env varBrowse catalog → Install
Updategrafana-cli plugins update <id>Update env var + restartClick "Update" button
Update ALLgrafana-cli plugins update-allUpdate all in env varCheck each manually
DisableRename plugin folderRemove from env varToggle "Enable" switch
Uninstallgrafana-cli plugins remove <id>Remove from env var + restartClick "Uninstall"

Key points: Disabling ≠ Uninstalling. Disabled plugins keep files but are not loaded. Uninstalling removes files. After CLI uninstall, RESTART Grafana. Plugin dependencies: Some plugins depend on others — removing a dependency breaks dependent plugins. Always check the plugin's documentation for dependencies before removing.

19.3 Essential Plugins for the Exam

🗺️
World Map Panel

Geographic data visualization. Plot points on interactive maps. Use for: user locations, server regions, IoT sensor positions. Exam: "Which panel for geographic data?"

🕐
Clock Panel

Digital/analog clock display. Time zones, countdown, 12/24h format. Use for: NOC displays, showing server times across regions. One of the most downloaded plugins.

📊
Plotly Panel

Advanced charting: 3D scatter, contour, polar, ternary plots. Beyond built-in visualizations. Use for: scientific data, complex statistical visualizations.

🔀
Discrete Panel

Discrete time series display. Show on/off states, boolean values, categorical data over time. Use for: uptime tracking, feature flags, deployment status.

📐
Diagram Panel

Flowchart and diagram visualization. Mermaid.js-based. Use for: architecture diagrams, process flows, decision trees — all with live metric data.

📈
Infinity Data Source

Query JSON, CSV, XML from REST APIs, files, or inline data. Turns any HTTP API into a Grafana data source. Use for: custom APIs, SaaS integrations without native plugins.

19.4 Plugin Development (Exam Overview)

Plugins are built with React + TypeScript using the @grafana/toolkit or create-plugin package. Structure: plugin.json (metadata), module.ts (entry point), React components for UI. Signed plugins require signing via Grafana's plugin signing process. The exam tests installation and management ONLY — not plugin development. Know that plugins extend Grafana's capabilities beyond built-in features.

📝 Chapter 19 — Practice Questions

Q1

What command installs a Grafana plugin from the CLI?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

grafana-cli plugins install <plugin-id>. For example: grafana-cli plugins install grafana-clock-panel. After installation, you MUST restart the Grafana server for the plugin to become available. This is a common exam point — CLI-installed plugins don't appear until restart.

💡 EXPLANATION

Related commands: grafana-cli plugins ls (list installed), grafana-cli plugins list-remote (browse catalog), grafana-cli plugins update <id> (update), grafana-cli plugins remove <id> (uninstall). For Docker, use GF_INSTALL_PLUGINS env var instead — plugins are auto-installed at container startup.

Q2

What are the three types of Grafana plugins? Give an example of each.

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

1) Data Source Plugins: Connect to external systems. Example: Prometheus, PostgreSQL, Loki. 2) Panel Plugins: New visualization types. Example: Clock Panel, World Map, Diagram. 3) App Plugins: Full applications within Grafana. Example: Grafana OnCall, Grafana Incident, Performance Testing (k6).

💡 EXPLANATION

Even "built-in" data sources like Prometheus and PostgreSQL are technically plugins — they're just bundled with Grafana. Panel plugins extend beyond the 13 built-in visualizations. App plugins are the most powerful — they can add entire new sections to the Grafana sidebar with their own pages and workflows.

Q3

How do you install plugins in a Docker-based Grafana deployment?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Use the GF_INSTALL_PLUGINS environment variable with a comma-separated list of plugin IDs: -e GF_INSTALL_PLUGINS="grafana-clock-panel,grafana-worldmap-panel". Plugins are auto-installed at container startup — no restart needed. Alternatively, mount a plugins volume or extend the Docker image.

💡 EXPLANATION

Docker advantage: No manual CLI commands and no restart needed. Grafana checks GF_INSTALL_PLUGINS on every startup, installs missing plugins, and updates existing ones to the specified version (if version pinned). This is the recommended approach for Docker deployments — declarative and reproducible.

Q4

What is plugin signing and why does it matter?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Plugin signing verifies that a plugin comes from a trusted source (Grafana Labs or authorized developer). Signed plugins have a cryptographic signature that Grafana verifies on load. Unsigned plugins require explicit enabling via GF_PLUGINS_ALLOW_LOADING_UNSIGNED_PLUGINS. This prevents malicious plugins from being loaded — critical security measure.

💡 EXPLANATION

Grafana Labs signs official and community plugins. Custom/private plugins are typically unsigned. To load unsigned plugins: set allow_loading_unsigned_plugins = my-custom-plugin in grafana.ini. Only enable unsigned plugins you've built yourself or fully trust — they execute with Grafana's permissions and can access all your data.

Q5

After running grafana-cli plugins install, the plugin doesn't appear. What's wrong?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Grafana needs to be RESTARTED. CLI-installed plugins are loaded only when Grafana starts. Run systemctl restart grafana-server (Linux) or docker restart grafana (Docker). This is the #1 plugin installation pitfall — the CLI installs files to disk, but Grafana only discovers new plugins during startup.

💡 EXPLANATION

This is frequently tested on the exam. The difference: Docker's GF_INSTALL_PLUGINS installs plugins BEFORE Grafana starts (no restart needed). CLI installation happens while Grafana is RUNNING (restart required). Verify installation: grafana-cli plugins ls shows installed plugins regardless of Grafana running state.

Q6

Which plugin would you use to display geographic data on an interactive map?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

World Map Panel plugin. It plots data points on an interactive world map using latitude/longitude coordinates. Use for: user locations, server regions, IoT sensor positions, sales by country. Data format requires geohash or latitude+longitude fields. Built-in Geomap panel (newer) also handles geographic data — World Map is the legacy plugin version.

💡 EXPLANATION

Exam note: The Geomap panel is now BUILT-IN (since Grafana 8.x). The World Map Panel is the older plugin version. Both display geographic data. The exam may reference either. Key difference: Geomap supports more map layers and is actively maintained. World Map is community-maintained and simpler.

Q7

How do you list all installed plugins on a Grafana instance?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

grafana-cli plugins ls — lists all installed plugins with their IDs, versions, and status. In the UI: Administration → Plugins shows installed plugins with additional details like signatures, update availability, and dependencies. The CLI is faster; the UI provides more context.

💡 EXPLANATION

CLI output shows: plugin ID, type (panel/datasource/app), version. UI shows: description, screenshots, documentation links, update notifications, dependency information, and whether the plugin is signed. Use CLI for scripting/automation, UI for exploration and management.

Q8

What is the Infinity data source plugin and when would you use it?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Infinity is a data source plugin that queries JSON, CSV, XML, and GraphQL from REST APIs, local files, or inline data. Use it when you need to visualize data from an API that doesn't have a native Grafana plugin. Example: Pull data from your company's internal REST API → visualize in Grafana without writing a custom data source plugin.

💡 EXPLANATION

Infinity is incredibly versatile: query any HTTP endpoint, parse the response (JSONPath, XPath, CSV), and visualize. For anihpj: Query the Django REST API directly for business metrics without going through PostgreSQL. Authentication support: Basic, Bearer token, API key, OAuth2. This is the "Swiss Army knife" of data sources.

Q9

How does Grafana handle plugin updates and compatibility?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

CLI: grafana-cli plugins update <plugin-id> or grafana-cli plugins update-all. Docker: update the GF_INSTALL_PLUGINS version pin. UI: Administration → Plugins shows available updates. Compatibility: Major Grafana version bumps may break plugins. Always check plugin release notes before upgrading Grafana — some plugins lag behind Grafana releases. Test on staging first.

💡 EXPLANATION

Plugin compatibility is the #1 reason Grafana upgrades fail. A plugin built for Grafana 9.x may not work on 10.x. Before upgrading: 1) List all installed plugins, 2) Check each plugin's documentation for version support, 3) Upgrade in staging, 4) Verify all dashboards using plugin panels still render correctly. Unmaintained plugins are a risk.

Q10

You need to install plugins across 50 Grafana instances. What approach do you use?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

1) Docker/K8s: Set GF_INSTALL_PLUGINS env var in your deployment — every instance auto-installs on startup. 2) Configuration management: Use Ansible/Puppet/Chef to run grafana-cli plugins install + restart. 3) Custom Docker image: Build a Dockerfile that pre-installs plugins → consistent across all instances. 4) Shared volume: Mount a plugins directory on NFS — all instances share plugins.

💡 EXPLANATION

Best practice for 50+ instances: Custom Docker image with plugins baked in. Each instance starts identical and fast — no download delays. For K8s: Use an init container that runs grafana-cli plugins install before the main Grafana container starts. Never manually SSH into 50 servers to install plugins — automation is essential.

Chapter 20: Grafana API & Automation 🔴 Advanced

Chapter 20: The Grafana HTTP API — Automate Your Entire Grafana Workflow

The Grafana HTTP API is a comprehensive RESTful interface that lets you automate every Grafana operation — from creating dashboards to managing users. Learn the essential endpoints, authentication patterns, and build automation scripts for the anihpj platform using curl, Python, and Terraform.

🔴 Advanced⏱️ ~1.5 hours🤖 API🔄 Automation

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Grafana HTTP API overview — RESTful, JSON, /api/ prefix
  • ✅ Authentication: Bearer tokens (Service Accounts), Basic Auth
  • ✅ Essential endpoints: Dashboards, Data Sources, Annotations, Alerting, Admin
  • ✅ Real anihpj automation: CI/CD annotations, dashboard export, API-driven deployments
  • ✅ Terraform provider for full GitOps management
Grafana API — 3-4 Exam Questions

Know: (1) Base URL: /api, (2) Authentication: Authorization: Bearer <token> header, (3) Dashboard CRUD endpoints (GET/POST /api/dashboards/*), (4) Annotations API for automated event markers, (5) API version is independent of Grafana version — check /api/health for server status.

🧑‍🏫 Novice's Corner — What's a REST API? And Why Should I Care? (Plain English)

If you've never worked with APIs before, here's the simplest explanation:

1. An API is a "menu" for computers. When you go to a restaurant, you don't walk into the kitchen and cook your own food. You read the menu, tell the waiter what you want ("I'll have the pasta"), and the kitchen makes it. An API is exactly that — a menu of things Grafana can do, and you "order" by sending a request. Instead of clicking through the UI to create 50 dashboards, you send 50 API "orders" and Grafana's "kitchen" (backend) does the work automatically.

2. REST = a standard "language" for the menu. REST means the API uses standard HTTP methods: GET = "Show me what you have" (read), POST = "Make something new" (create), PUT = "Update this" (modify), DELETE = "Remove this" (destroy). These four "verbs" cover 99% of everything you'll ever need to do with Grafana programmatically.

3. Why not just use the UI? Because humans are slow. Creating one dashboard in the UI takes 5 minutes. Creating 50 takes 4 hours. An API script creates 50 in 30 seconds. When you're managing dozens of Grafana instances across environments (dev, staging, prod), automation via API isn't optional — it's essential. This is what separates "I use Grafana" from "I run Grafana at scale."

4. Authentication = your "ID card." The API needs to know WHO is making the request. Service Account tokens are like ID badges — they prove you're authorized. Without one, the API says "401 Unauthorized" (like a bouncer saying "you're not on the list"). Tokens are safer than passwords because you can create one per purpose (one for CI/CD, one for backups) and revoke them individually.

5. Why anihpj uses the API: Every time anihpj deploys new code, a GitHub Actions script POSTs an annotation to Grafana marking "v2.1.0 deployed." Now when engineers see a latency spike, they look for the nearest annotation — "Was it right after the deploy? Yes! Rollback!" Without API automation, this correlation would be manual guesswork.

🌐 API vs UI — When to Use Which
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    🖥️ UI (Click)  vs  🤖 API (Code)                           │

  │  └────────────────────────────┘            └────────────────────────────┘     │
  │                                                                                │

  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │                                                                           │ │

  │                                                                                │
  │  🎯 RULE OF THUMB: If you do it ONCE → use UI. If you do it MORE THAN ONCE →  │

20.1 Grafana HTTP API Overview

🌐 GRAFANA HTTP API — Architecture
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  API Base: http://localhost:3000/api                                  │
  │  All responses: JSON                                                  │
  │  Auth: Authorization: Bearer <service-account-token>                  │
  │                                                                      │
  │  API CATEGORIES:                                                     │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ 📊 Dashboards  → /api/dashboards/*   (CRUD dashboards)       │   │
  │  │ 🔌 Data Sources→ /api/datasources/*  (CRUD data sources)     │   │
  │  │ 📍 Annotations → /api/annotations/*  (Create event markers)  │   │
  │  │ 🔔 Alerting    → /api/alertmanager/* (Manage alerts)         │   │
  │  │ 👥 Admin       → /api/admin/*         (Users, orgs, settings)│   │
  │  │ 🔍 Search      → /api/search/*        (Find dashboards)      │   │
  │  │ ❤️ Health      → /api/health           (Server status)       │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🔑 API Authentication — Service Accounts vs API Keys

Service Accounts (RECOMMENDED): Machine-to-machine auth tokens. Create via Admin → Service Accounts → Add. Tokens start with glsa_. Can be assigned roles (Viewer, Editor, Admin) and permissions. Multiple tokens per account — rotate individually. Expiry configurable. Use: Authorization: Bearer glsa_xxxxx.

API Keys (DEPRECATED): Older auth method. One key per user. Cannot have multiple keys for different purposes. Replaced by Service Accounts. Still work but Grafana recommends migration.

Basic Auth: Authorization: Basic base64(user:pass). Works but NOT recommended for automation — credentials are long-lived. Use Service Accounts with token rotation.

📝 API Versioning & Rate Limiting

Versioning: Grafana's API version is INDEPENDENT of the Grafana server version. Check /api/health for server version. Individual API endpoints have their own versioning (e.g., /api/v1/, /api/v2/). Breaking changes are announced in release notes. Rate limiting: Not enabled by default in OSS. Enterprise/Labs features add rate limiting per user/IP. Be respectful: don't poll endpoints more than once per second. Use exponential backoff on 429 responses.

20.2 Essential API Endpoints — Complete Reference

a) Dashboards API — Full CRUD

Dashboards API
GET    /api/search?type=dash-db&query=anihpj     # Search dashboards
GET    /api/dashboards/uid/:uid                   # Get dashboard JSON
POST   /api/dashboards/db                         # Create/update dashboard
DELETE /api/dashboards/uid/:uid                   # Delete dashboard
GET    /api/dashboards/home                       # Get home dashboard
GET    /api/dashboards/tags                       # Get all dashboard tags

b) Data Sources API — Provisioning at Scale

Data Sources API
GET    /api/datasources                            # List all data sources
GET    /api/datasources/:id                        # Get data source by ID
GET    /api/datasources/name/:name                 # Get data source by name
POST   /api/datasources                            # Create new data source
PUT    /api/datasources/:id                        # Update data source
DELETE /api/datasources/:id                        # Delete data source
GET    /api/datasources/:id/health                 # Test data source connection

c) Annotations API — Event Markers for CI/CD

Annotations API
POST   /api/annotations                            # Create annotation
GET    /api/annotations?tags=deploy                # Query annotations by tag
GET    /api/annotations?dashboardUID=:uid          # Get annotations for dashboard
PUT    /api/annotations/:id                        # Update annotation
DELETE /api/annotations/:id                        # Delete annotation
💡 Annotation Use Case

Automatically POST an annotation after every CI/CD deployment. These markers appear as vertical lines on ALL dashboards — see instantly if a deployment caused a metric spike. For anihpj: POST /api/annotations in GitHub Actions after successful deploy with tags: ["deploy", "anihpj", "v2.1.0"].

d) Alerting API — Programmatic Alert Management

Alerting API
GET    /api/alertmanager/grafana/api/v2/alerts     # List active alerts
GET    /api/alertmanager/grafana/api/v2/alerts/:id  # Get specific alert
POST   /api/alertmanager/grafana/api/v2/silences   # Create silence
GET    /api/alertmanager/grafana/api/v2/silences   # List silences
DELETE /api/alertmanager/grafana/api/v2/silence/:id# Expire silence
GET    /api/v1/provisioning/alert-rules            # List provisioned alert rules

e) Admin API — Server & User Management

Admin API
GET    /api/admin/stats                            # Server statistics
GET    /api/admin/settings                         # Grafana settings
GET    /api/health                                 # Health check (no auth needed)
GET    /api/users                                  # List users (admin only)
GET    /api/orgs                                   # List organizations
POST   /api/serviceaccounts                        # Create service account

20.3 Automation Examples with anihpj

bash — CI/CD Integration
# === CI/CD: Add deployment annotation ===
curl -X POST http://grafana:3000/api/annotations \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $GRAFANA_SERVICE_TOKEN" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{
    "text": "anihpj v2.1.0 deployed to production",
    "tags": ["deployment", "anihpj", "production"],
    "dashboardUID": "anihpj-overview",
    "panelId": 2
  }'

# === Automation: Export dashboard for GitOps backup ===
curl -H "Authorization: Bearer $GRAFANA_SERVICE_TOKEN" \
  /api/dashboards/uid/anihpj-overview > dashboard-backup.json

# === Automation: Provision new data source via API ===
curl -X POST /api/datasources \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $GRAFANA_SERVICE_TOKEN" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"name":"Prometheus-Staging","type":"prometheus","url":"http://prom-staging:9090","access":"proxy"}'

20.4 Grafana Terraform Provider — GitOps at Scale

hcl — Terraform
# Configure the Grafana provider
provider "grafana" {
  url  = "http://localhost:3000"
  auth = var.grafana_service_token
}

# Dashboard as code
resource "grafana_dashboard" "anihpj_overview" {
  folder      = grafana_folder.anihpj.id
  config_json = file("dashboards/anihpj-overview.json")
}

# Data source as code
resource "grafana_data_source" "prometheus" {
  name = "Prometheus-Production"
  type = "prometheus"
  url  = "http://prometheus:9090"
}

# Folder as code
resource "grafana_folder" "anihpj" {
  title = "anihpj"
}

# Full GitOps: terraform apply provisions your ENTIRE Grafana setup
# Dashboards, data sources, folders, alert rules — all as code in Git.

📝 Chapter 20 — Practice Questions

Q1

What is the base URL for the Grafana HTTP API and how do you authenticate?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Base URL: http://localhost:3000/api. Authentication: Authorization: Bearer <token> header using a Service Account token. Alternatively, Basic Auth with username:password (deprecated for automation). All responses are JSON. Check health: GET /api/health returns "ok".

💡 EXPLANATION

Service Account tokens are the RECOMMENDED auth method for API access. Create: Admin → Service Accounts → Add → Generate Token. Tokens start with glsa_. API Keys are deprecated. Always use HTTPS in production: https://grafana.company.com/api. Never expose token in code — use env vars or secrets management.

Q2

How do you create a dashboard programmatically via the API?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER
POST /api/dashboards/db
Authorization: Bearer <token>
Content-Type: application/json

{
  "dashboard": {
    "title": "My Dashboard",
    "panels": [...],
    "schemaVersion": 39
  },
  "folderId": 0,
  "overwrite": true
}
💡 EXPLANATION

The dashboard field contains the full dashboard JSON model (same format as Export from UI). overwrite: true updates existing dashboard with same UID. folderId: 0 puts it in General folder. Get the JSON structure by exporting an existing dashboard from the UI. Response includes the dashboard UID.

Q3

How do you automate deployment annotations in your CI/CD pipeline?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER
curl -X POST /api/annotations \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"text":"v2.1 deployed","tags":["deploy","anihpj"]}'

Add this to your CI/CD pipeline (GitHub Actions, GitLab CI, Jenkins) as a step AFTER successful deployment. Annotations appear as vertical markers on ALL dashboards — providing instant visual correlation between deployments and metric changes.

💡 EXPLANATION

Best practice: Include the version number, commit SHA, and deployer in the annotation text. Tags enable filtering: ?tags=deploy&tags=anihpj. This creates a permanent visual record: "Did the latency spike happen right after deployment?" — just look for the annotation marker on your graphs.

Q4

What endpoint would you use to list all dashboards containing "anihpj"?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

GET /api/search?type=dash-db&query=anihpj. Returns a JSON array of matching dashboards with id, uid, title, tags, folderId, and folderTitle. Add &tag=production to filter by tag. Add &folderIds=5 to filter by folder. The search endpoint is the primary way to discover dashboards programmatically.

💡 EXPLANATION

Common search parameters: type=dash-db (dashboards only, not folders), query= (text search in title), tag= (filter by tag), starred=true (only starred dashboards), limit=50 (pagination). Response includes dashboard UIDs for further API calls. Use this to build scripts that process all dashboards.

Q5

How does the Grafana Terraform provider enable GitOps?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

The Terraform provider manages Grafana resources as declarative HCL code in Git. Resources: dashboards, data sources, folders, alert rules, contact points, organizations. Flow: Developer commits .tf file → CI/CD runs terraform plan (preview) → terraform apply (apply changes). Changes are versioned, reviewed via PR, and auditable. Full GitOps: Git is the source of truth, Terraform reconciles Grafana to match.

💡 EXPLANATION

Terraform vs Provisioning: Provisioning handles dashboards + data sources as YAML/JSON files watched by Grafana. Terraform handles EVERYTHING — orgs, teams, permissions, alert rules, contact points — as code with state management. For anihpj: Combine both — provisioning for dashboards (simpler), Terraform for the rest of Grafana configuration.

Q6

How do you export all dashboards from a Grafana instance as JSON backup?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER
# Step 1: List all dashboard UIDs
curl -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  /api/search?type=dash-db | jq -r '.[].uid' > uids.txt

# Step 2: Export each dashboard
while read uid; do
  curl -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
    /api/dashboards/uid/$uid > "dashboards/$uid.json"
done < uids.txt
💡 EXPLANATION

The exported JSON includes "dashboard" wrapper, "folderId", and "overwrite" fields — ready for re-import via POST /api/dashboards/db. For complete backup: also backup the database (pg_dump) for users, teams, and alert rules. Dashboard JSON export is a SUPPLEMENT, not a replacement, for database backup.

Q7

How do you test if the Grafana API is accessible and healthy?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

GET /api/health — returns {"database": "ok", "version": "10.0.0", "commit": "abc123"} if Grafana is running and the database is accessible. No authentication required. This is the simplest health check — use it in load balancer health checks, Docker HEALTHCHECK, and monitoring scripts.

💡 EXPLANATION

Docker HEALTHCHECK example: HEALTHCHECK --interval=30s CMD curl -f http://localhost:3000/api/health || exit 1. The /api/health endpoint doesn't require auth — it's safe for public health checks. For authenticated checks (verifying tokens work), use GET /api/auth/keys with Bearer token.

Q8

How would you create a new data source for anihpj staging environment via API?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER
curl -X POST /api/datasources \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{
    "name": "Prometheus-Staging",
    "type": "prometheus",
    "url": "http://prometheus-staging:9090",
    "access": "proxy",
    "isDefault": false,
    "jsonData": {"timeInterval": "15s"}
  }'
💡 EXPLANATION

Key fields: type must match the plugin ID (case-sensitive). access: proxy is recommended for security (Grafana proxies queries). basicAuth and secureJsonData for credentials. Response includes the new data source ID. Test with "Save & Test" via GET /api/datasources/:id/health. For production, use Terraform or provisioning YAML instead.

Q9

What is the difference between the Grafana API and Provisioning for managing resources?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

API: Procedural — you make HTTP calls to create/update/delete resources. Good for: scripts, CI/CD, dynamic operations. Provisioning: Declarative — you define resources in YAML/JSON files in the provisioning directory. Grafana auto-loads and watches for changes. Good for: dashboards + data sources, GitOps without API calls. Terraform bridges both — declarative code that uses the API under the hood.

💡 EXPLANATION

When to use which: Provisioning = production dashboards and data sources (set once, Git-managed, read-only in UI). API = dynamic operations (CI/CD annotations, temporary dashboards, automated backups). Terraform = enterprise-wide Grafana configuration (orgs, teams, permissions, everything as code). They complement each other — not mutually exclusive.

Q10

Design a complete CI/CD pipeline that auto-deploys dashboards to Grafana on every release.

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER
  GitHub Actions CI/CD Pipeline:

  1. Developer commits dashboard JSON to Git
  2. GitHub Actions triggers on push to main
  3. Step: Validate JSON (jq or grafana-validate)
  4. Step: POST /api/dashboards/db (create/update)
  5. Step: POST /api/annotations (mark deployment)
     {text: "Dashboard updated in release v2.1.0"}
  6. Step: GET /api/dashboards/uid/:uid (verify)
  7. Slack notification: "Dashboard deployed ✅"

  Service Account token stored in GitHub Secrets
  → Never committed to Git → Secure automation
💡 EXPLANATION

Key considerations: Use overwrite: true to update existing dashboards. Validate JSON before pushing (broken JSON = broken dashboard). Add deployment annotations for traceability. Use different Service Accounts for different environments (prod token can write, staging token is separate). Always test in staging before production deployment.

Chapter 21: Grafana Loki — Log Aggregation Deep Dive 🔴 Extra

Chapter 21: Loki — The "L" in LGTM (Logs)

Loki is Grafana's horizontally-scalable, highly-available log aggregation system — think "Prometheus, but for logs." It indexes only metadata (labels), not full log text, making it incredibly cost-effective. Learn the architecture, deployment modes, Promtail configuration, and advanced LogQL for production log management.

🔴 Extra⏱️ ~2 hours📜 Logs🔍 LogQL

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Loki architecture: Distributor → Ingester → Querier → Object Storage
  • ✅ Labels-only index — why Loki is cheaper than Elasticsearch
  • ✅ Three deployment modes: Monolithic, Simple Scalable, Microservices
  • ✅ Promtail configuration: scrape_configs, pipeline_stages, label generation
  • ✅ LogQL: Stream selectors, line filters, log pipelines, metric queries
  • ✅ Log exploration: Live tail, derived fields, Loki→Tempo links
Loki — 2-3 Exam Questions

Know: (1) Loki indexes labels only, NOT log content (key differentiator from Elasticsearch), (2) LogQL syntax: {job="app"} |= "ERROR", (3) Promtail is the log collector agent, (4) Three deployment modes and when to use each, (5) Derived fields link Loki logs to Tempo traces via trace_id.

🧑‍🏫 Novice's Corner — What Loki ACTUALLY Does (Plain English)

1. Loki is "Prometheus, but for logs." Prometheus scrapes metrics (numbers) with labels. Loki collects logs (text) with labels. Both use the same label-based query model. If you know Prometheus, you already know 50% of Loki.

2. The genius trick: Loki does NOT index the log text. Elasticsearch indexes EVERY word in every log line — powerful but EXPENSIVE (index can be larger than the logs!). Loki only indexes labels (like {job="anihpj", env="prod"}) — the actual log text is stored in cheap object storage (S3). This makes Loki 10-100x cheaper for the same volume.

3. The trade-off: Because Loki doesn't full-text-index, searching log CONTENT is slower — it has to scan through chunks. But 99% of log queries start with "show me logs from service X in environment Y" (label filter) and THEN "containing the word ERROR" (line filter). The label filter narrows the search massively, making it fast enough for practical use.

4. Why anihpj uses Loki: anihpj generates ~2GB of Django logs per day. Elasticsearch would need 4-6GB of storage (index + data). With Loki: ~2.1GB total (just compressed chunks + tiny label index). At $0.02/GB/month on S3, that's $0.04/month vs $0.12/month — and the difference grows exponentially at scale.

21.1 Loki Architecture Deep Dive

📜 LOKI ARCHITECTURE — Write Path + Read Path
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                          📜 LOKI FULL ARCHITECTURE                            │
  │                                                                                │
  │  ═══════════════════════ WRITE PATH (Log Ingestion) ════════════════════════   │

  │  ┌──────────────┐    ┌──────────────┐    ┌──────────────┐                      │
  │  │  Compactor   │    │    Ruler     │    │   Gateway    │                      │

  │  │   chunks into │   │   alert rules│    │ • Rate limit  │                      │
  │  │   larger ones │   │ • Sends to   │    │ • Multi-tenant│                      │
  │  │ • Deduplicates│   │   Alertmgr   │    │   routing     │                      │
  │  │ • Removes old │   └──────────────┘    └──────────────┘                      │
  │  │   deleted data│                                                              │
